## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

• Accredited Entities are required to submit all annexes that were provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in the updated version of the funding proposal.

• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time frame.

• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

• Under GCF Policy 3.1 on Funding Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements as set out in Section G of the GCF Policies and Standards. The IDP Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.

A.19. Complementarity and coherence
The Programme "Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management" (FP117) was approved by the GCF Board on 20 June 2016. The programme is a large-scale, multi-partner project which will be reformed as a programme with 3 Sub-Programmes: 1) Forest Landscape Partnership Facility (FLPF); 2) Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme (LPRP); and 3) Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme (LPRP). The FLPF outlines a programmatic approach with Project 1 covering 3 out of 6 provinces of the country, while Projects 2 & 3 will cover all remaining provinces. The LPRP is a Sub-Programme under the Forest Landscape Partnership Facility (FLPF) and the subsequent Sub-Projects 2 and 3 (hereafter Project 2 and Project 3). Project 1 covers 60 villages in 3 provinces, while Projects 2 & 3 cover all 580 villages covered by the EPR Programme, as well as 40 additional villages (totaling the number of villages to be covered under the LPRP). The LPRP is designed to help reach the envisioned transformational change of forest and land management in the upland provinces of Laos.

A.20. Executing Entity information
The Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH, besides being the Accredited Entity (AE) of the project, acts also as an Executing Entity (EE). Based on the GCF Call for Proposals, the EE is responsible to:
- The Government of the Lao People's Democratic Republic (Lao PDR or the Host Country)
- The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) as a co-executing entity
- Lao PDR Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MNRRE) as co-chair and chair of the Environment Protection Fund (EPF) board

A.21.1 Country context

Lao People’s Democratic Republic (PDR) is a landlocked Least Developed Country (LLDC) where the economy is dominated by agriculture and forestry, especially tree crops, including rubber production (especially hydropower) and logging. Agriculture, forestry and fisheries account for 16% of gross domestic product (GDP) and employ 64% of the population. The country has a high level of vulnerability to climate change due to its poor soil quality, poor access to nutrition, and livelihoods for the rural population and, in particular, the rural poor.

2. **Natural Resources**: Lao PDR is characterized by high biodiversity, low human population density, low life expectancies (as further described in Chapter 8.1). This region is among the poorest in the country, and villagers in the remote areas are highly vulnerable to climate change due to their lack of access to resources and services. The country’s forest cover has been declining over time as a result of deforestation and land degradation; this is highly sensitive to climate change due to its steep terrain, which is naturally prone to erosion events and landslides. The country also faces significant challenges in terms of water availability and quality, especially for rural communities: vulnerability to climate change. Six provinces in Northern PDR (Bokeo, Houaphan, Luang Prabang, Luang Namtha, Xieng Khouang, and Xaignabouli) are particularly vulnerable due to deforestation and land degradation during the period from 2005-2015. Deforestation and forest degradation exacerbate the risk of landslides and soil erosion in these areas.

3. **Emissions**: The country’s total emissions (excluding LULUCF) were estimated at approximately 4.8 million tonnes CO2 equivalent per year, with a significant share from the energy sector (50%), followed by agriculture (27%) and industry (16%). The remaining 7% is attributed to other sectors such as waste management, transport, and construction. The country’s emissions are dominated by fossil fuel combustion in the power generation sector, which accounts for approximately 80% of total energy-related CO2 emissions.

A.21.2 Overview of the overarching programme

This project is embedded in the overarching programme “Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reduction Programme (ERP)” under the Kyoto Protocol’s Clean Development Mechanism (CDM) and Joint Implementation (JI) mechanisms, which was approved by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Board at its 24th meeting board (B.24-16) on 3 May 2019. The overarching programme aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from deforestation and forest degradation in Lao PDR through sustainable and climate-resilient management of forests and landscapes at scale. This will reduce approximately 117 million tonnes CO2 equivalent (tCO2e) by the end of the project period, which is equivalent to the loss of ecosystems over the 7-year implementation period of Projects 1 and 2. The programme contributes to the successful implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reduction Programme (ERP) under the Kyoto Protocol in the aforementioned six provinces covered by the ERP.

Refer to the GCF Co-financing of the CDM Project for more information on the mitigation results which are calculated only up to the duration of the project. Mitigation results are calculated against a sub-national Forest Reference Emissions Level (FREL) for Lao PDR’s Emission Reduction Programme and are based on the most recent available data.

Project 1, which was approved under the Kyoto Protocol in 198 May 2000, since the Project has made significant progress. No further information is provided as it is not relevant to this project.

The programme consists of 3 projects: Project 1 (mid-2005 to mid-2024) addresses the three provinces of Houaphan, Savannakhet and Luang Prabang; Project 2 (mid-2006 to mid-2024) addresses the three provinces of Xieng Khouang, Bokeo and Oudomxay; and Project 3 (mid-2017 to mid-2025) addresses Luang Namtha. The geographical reach of the project is the six additional provinces of Luang Namtha, Bokeo and Oudomxay” (GCF PP 117, page 5). Two subsequent stand-alone projects, embedded in the same programme context and theory of change as this project, will be submitted at a later stage. The first one is Project 4 (mid-2026 to mid-2030) which addresses Luang Prabang and Savannakhet; and the second one is Project 5 (mid-2027 to mid-2031) which addresses Xieng Khouang, Bokeo and Oudomxay.

The Environment Protection Fund (EPF) under the NABIE will continue as a fund manager of the Climate Change Funding Window. The EPF will also continue to provide technical assistance for the project implementation in the six provinces covered by this financing mechanism for the project in the entire project area. More information is provided in Sections B.3 and B-4 below.

For more information refer to GCF’s Project 1 of the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) Emission Reduction Programme Agreement, Project 2 of the FCPF Emission Reduction Programme Agreement and Project 3 of the FCPF Emission Reduction Programme Agreement.

The programme consists of 3 projects: Project 1 mid-2005 to mid-2024 addresses the three provinces of Houaphan, Savannakhet and Luang Prabang; Project 2 (mid-2006 to mid-2024) addresses the three provinces of Xieng Khouang, Bokeo and Oudomxay; and Project 3 (mid-2017 to mid-2025) addresses Luang Namtha. The geographical reach of the project is the six additional provinces of Luang Namtha, Bokeo and Oudomxay” (GCF PP 117, page 5).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

1. Initially, the project was conceptualized as a pure mitigation single project which had to be reformed as a programme with 3 Sub-Projects due to a limitation in available GCF funds and the time of board approval. FP1(17) explicitly approved the revised proposal for the implementation of the three sub-projects (Houaphan, Luang Prabang and Sayaboury), and the subsequent Sub-Projects 2 and 3 (hereafter Project 2) expanded to cover the entire province of Houaphan. The project is designed to help Laos reach the envisioned transformational change of forest and land management in the uplands of the Lao PDR.
1. Project 2 will focus on three components: (i) strengthening the institutional framework for forest governance, mainstreaming, strengthening the regulatory framework, and implementing and improving the measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) system; (ii) promoting sustainable forest landscape restoration in the provinces namely Houaphan, Sayaboury and Luang Prabang. However, to fully reach the paradigm shift towards low-emission development pathways for forests and land use practices outlined in the programme approach within FP1(17), additional support is needed.

## A2.3 Overview of Project 2

The project aims to provide guidance and consolidate the programme's objective of supporting the Government and people of Lao PDR transition to sustainable and climate-resilient management of forests and landscapes at scale.
- Component 1 addresses barriers at the national and sub-national levels, including measures that aim to scale-up forest landscape restoration (FLR) in the provinces of Houaphan, Sayaboury and Luang Prabang. This includes the enforcement and monitoring, and scale-up and ensure access to sustainable financing for the AFOLU sector.
- Component 2 addresses barriers at the sub-national level, including measures that aim to reduce deforestation and degradation within the agricultural sector. It delivers emission reductions at scale through reducing the expansion of agriculture into forests and other natural ecosystems, as well as promoting climate-smart agricultural practices that increase the resilience of local farmers and agro-ecosystems.
- Component 3 addresses barriers at the sub-national level, including measures that aim to reduce emissions and strengthen the resilience of local livelihoods and forest ecosystems through sustainable landscape management and the promotion of Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR), with a focus on village and conservation forests.

* Conservation forests will focus on 5 national protected areas (NPAs) and 1 national park within the project area.

A


## Project 2 is complementary and additional to project 1. Together both projects will facilitate a paradigm shift in the forestry and land use sector in Lao PDR that will be sustained by unlocking additional sources of results-based payments for climate change mitigation, increasing the capacity of the forestry sector to mobilize resources and capacities for mobilizing and channeling climate finance. Project 2 has been revised and re-designed as a cross-cutting project with a focus on strengthening the institutional framework for climate-resilient agriculture and local rural livelihoods, in full alignment with Lao PDR’s latest Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Government of Lao PDR’s Green Growth Strategy (2021). It will scale up investments in climate-resilient and deforestation-free agriculture and forestry practices across 4 provinces, with a focus on the provinces where Project 1 has already been implemented. The activities developed under Project 1.

### Project 2 will result in emission reductions of approximately 4.6 million tCO2e over its duration of 4 years, and directly strengthen the resilience of over 273,700 direct beneficiaries and 2,100,000 ha of ecosystems. It will further generate additional environmental, social and economic benefits that contribute to Lao PDR’s national Green Growth Strategy to 2035, and the country’s recovery from the COVID-19 pandemic.

For more detailed information on project mitigation and adaptation benefits, refer to Chapters D.1 and E.

B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

10. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) approved the “Enhancing REDD+ Programmes through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management” (PF117) in 2019 to B.24. The overall objective of PF117 is to enhance the effectiveness, efficiency, and sustainability of the Enhanced Emission Reduction Programme (ER-Programme) under the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCP) in six provinces of Northern Laos (Lao PDR). The ER-Programme was approved by the GCF Board in 2015 (Project ID: PF116). The programme area constitutes approximately 30% of the national territory and 32% of the country’s forest cover. Around 78% of the population lives within this area, which is characterized by high poverty levels and six provinces, which are also among the most vulnerable provinces to climate change in Lao PDR (see B.1.3).

## Figure 3: Phased approach to implement the Lao PDR Emissions Reduction Programme

| Project 1 | Project 2 |
| --- | --- |
| **Lao PDR Forests, Soils, Governance and Livelihoods (FSG) Programme**<br>Station 1 (2017-2023)<br>Focus: 52 Villages + 240 Villages | **KFW Results-based Payments and public and private finance**<br>Station 2 (2020-2029)<br>Focus: 17 Districts + 240 Villages |

### Project 1
- Implement the Lao PDR Forests, Soils, Governance and Livelihoods Programme.
- Focus on 52 villages initially.

### Project 2
- Scale up project activities in all ER Programme provinces.
- Specifically:
    - Implement RBP payments for forest conservation.
    - Support sustainable land management practices, which were not included under Project 1. This support will scale up successfully implemented approaches to enhance the resilience of livelihoods and ecosystems while promoting climate-resilient agriculture and agroforestry on agricultural land.

### Funding
- KFW (Kreditanstalt für Wiederaufbau) is funding this project.
- Lusang Prabang and Sayaboury Provinces are involved, covering provinces under Project 10.
- Support the continuation of activities in the 240 target villages located within 17 districts included under Project 1.

### Timeline
- Start: 06/2024 when Project 1 ends.
- End: 31/12/2029 (when Project 2 is embedded) in FP 117.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 9

## B

### Strengthening an enabling environment for REDD+ through the following means: enhancing the availability of financing investments in climate resilient and deforestation free agriculture, forestry and land use – including changing the current policy framework to align with the Paris Agreement (PA) and the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC)'s Emissions Trading Scheme (ETS) (EPP) Climate Change Funding Window established under Project 1 (Point 2); revising and strengthening the existing REDD+ project design template (PDT) to align with the PA and UNFCCC's ETS; and implementing a new regulatory framework (both Project 1 and 2). Project 2 will further strengthen the framework to improve the implementation of REDD+ in the context of climate change adaptation, including by aligning it with the Paris Agreement and the latest Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) to the United Nations' Framework Convention on Climate Change. The project will also support the development of a new regulatory framework for REDD+, which, when selected, may not only support reducing deforestation and reducing emissions, but are further informed by climate change adaptation needs and priorities, including those related to food security.

### Unlocking results-based payments and mobilizing additional public and private climate finance. Project 2 is needed to build on the efforts of Project 1, while scaling up climate resilient, low emission and deforestation free investments in agriculture, forestry and land use (AF&L) across the project area. The project will leverage US $42 million in results-based payments for emissions reductions (ERs) under the CFPF Carbon Fund (as per the PA) to support the implementation of climate change adaptation measures that are critical to scaling up and sustain them beyond implementation Mefan. It also builds on the lessons learned from Project 1 and includes the development of a new regulatory framework for REDD+ in the context of climate change adaptation, public finance for climate action. Supporting these efforts, it further includes measures to strengthen the capacity of the project area’s institutions and stakeholders to design, implement, monitor and evaluate effectively and efficiently channel funds for investments in climate change adaptation and mitigation.

## B.1.2 Country Contextual Considerations

### 15. Lao PDR is a landlocked Least Developed Country (LLDC) with an area of 23,868 million ha., has a population of 6.9 million people and per capita GDP of USD 700. The country’s economy is dependent on natural resources, especially forests which cover approximately 54% of the total land area. Forests are among the economically most important sectors in Lao PDR, contributing to about 16% of GDP and employing 64% of the labor force. Livestock and fisheries contribute around 10% of GDP and employ 64% of the labor force. Forests are among the economically most important sectors in Lao PDR, contributing to about 16% of GDP and employing 64% of the labor force. Livestock and fisheries. Approximately 80% of the population are heavily reliant on forests for timber, food, fuel, shelter, medicine and spiritual purposes. Lao PDR is at the heart of the globally recognized Biodiversity Hotspot Indo-Burma.

### 1.2 Climate Risk and Vulnerability

#### 16. Lao PDR is one of a number of countries in terms of climate change vulnerability, and especially experiences the effects of climate change. It is exposed to multiple climate-related natural hazards, including floods, droughts, which are expected to increase in frequency and intensity due to climate change. The country has experienced an occurrence of 50% of all climate-related hazard in the country, followed by storms and drought with 14% occurrence each. Lao PDR is one of a number of countries in terms of climate change vulnerability, and especially experiences the effects of climate change. It is exposed to multiple climate-related natural hazards, including floods, droughts, which are expected to increase in frequency and intensity due to climate change. The country has experienced an occurrence of 50% of all climate-related hazard in the country, followed by storms and drought with 14% occurrence each.

#### Overall situation

* Lao PDR is a tropical climate with two distinct seasons: (i) the rainy monsoon season (May to mid-October), and
(ii) the dry season (mid-October to April). The average annual temperature ranges from around 20°C in the Northern and Eastern regions of the country, to over 35°C in the Southern region. Annual rainfall varies across the country between the range of 25 to 275 cm. Precipitation ranges from 1,300 to 3,000 mm, with the highest rain concentrated in the south and in the central region of the country (especially Luang Prabang, Xeungkhouang and Vientiane). Figure 4.1 shows the northern mountainous stress (above 1,000 m) within the target area is mostly distributed.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 14

## Determinants of Deforestation and Forest Degradation in the Greater Mekong Subregion - Laos PDR Country Report

### Background

The Lao People's Democratic Republic (PDR) is a landlocked country located in Southeast Asia, bordered by China to the north, Vietnam to the east, Cambodia to the south-east, Thailand to the south-west and Myanmar to the west. The country has a population of approximately 6.5 million people, with an estimated annual growth rate of about 1.2%. The economy is predominantly agricultural, with rice being the main crop.

### Determinants of Deforestation

#### Major Drivers
- **Agriculture**: A major driver in the region is the expansion of agriculture into forested areas.
- **Population Growth and Poverty**: Population growth and poverty are also significant drivers of deforestation. The study by UNDP notes that during 2015, the population increased by approximately 3% per year.

#### Vulnerability to Climate Change
- **Northern Laos PDR**: This region is particularly vulnerable due to low coping capacity and adaptive capacity.
- **Southern Laos PDR**: This region has a higher vulnerability index compared to other regions in Laos. The study by UNDP notes that the Lao PDR among the most vulnerable areas in the country.

#### Climate Change Impacts
- **Shifting Climatic Conditions**: Shifting climatic conditions are expected to increase the frequency and intensity of droughts, leading to increased pressure on forest resources.
- **Impact on Forest Resources**: The study by UNDP notes that during 2015, the population increased by approximately 3% per year.

#### Human Settlements
- **Houses in Forested Areas**: There are human settlements located within forested areas. Approximately 80% of the population relies on forests for their income, food, shelter, medicine and other uses.
- **Impact on Forest Resources**: As such, households will be more vulnerable to deforestation and forest degradation.

#### Deforestation and Forest Degradation
- **Drivers of Deforestation**: The main drivers of deforestation are agricultural expansion, shifting agriculture, logging for timber and non-timber forest products, and infrastructure development.
- **Impact on Biodiversity**: Deforestation and forest degradation lead to a loss in biodiversity, including the loss of wildlife and plant habitats.

#### Mitigation Strategies
- **Sustainable Land Management Practices**: Sustainable land management practices can help mitigate deforestation and forest degradation. These include agroforestry systems, improved livestock management, and reforestation efforts.
- **Community-Based Forest Management**: Community-based forest management can also play a crucial role in reducing deforestation rates.

### Conclusion
The study by UNDP notes that during 2015, the population increased by approximately 3% per year. The main drivers of deforestation are agricultural expansion and shifting agriculture. Deforestation and forest degradation lead to a loss in biodiversity, including the loss of wildlife and plant habitats.

26. The following lists the two most serious risks posed by climate change in the project region and which will be addressed through the proposed investments. These risks are described further in detail in the Annex A to this document (Annex A) or the CRVA in Annex 30 for a more extended list of risks and a more detailed overview of the main hazard reduction measures.

## Risk of reduced agricultural production and food insecurity

An estimated 25% of rural households in the highlands are already food insecure, with limited access to land and water resources. The region has very limited valley space for growing rice paddy.72 Local households cultivate upland for subsistence; however, prolonged droughts and floods have been observed, leading physical conditions for land degradation, mass erosion events, and landslides. These natural hazards are expected to increase in frequency and intensity due to climate change. The yields of upland rice in some provinces, such as Huongson, However, the majority of farmers in the region are dependent on rainfed agriculture. Climate change is likely to reduce agricultural productivity and threaten food security and agricultural livelihoods in Northern Lao PDR, especially where key crops such as rice are most exposed to the impacts of climate change.73 The projected increase in temperature will lead to an expansion of areas affected by droughts and floods, among other climate-related hazards, are expected to cause losses and damages to agriculture and food security. In 2015, the total agricultural production value was estimated at LAK 489 billion (US$130,000 tons of rice) due to FLOODS in 2017. Investments are urgently needed in ecosystem-based adaptation, and climate change mitigation measures that will help strengthen the resilience of agroecosystems and smallholder farming communities.

## Risk of loss of reduced provision of ecosystem services

Lao PDR’s updated NDC notes that “changes in temperature and precipitation patterns may lead to increased forest fires, more frequent floods and droughts, and land use perturbations for biodiversity, ecosystems and ecosystems services.74 The increases in temperatures may increase the risk of forest fires, which could have a negative impact on biodiversity and ecosystem services. Such changes may lead to substantial erosion, watershed degradation and massive landslides. If further highlights the risks of changed precipitation patterns, which could lead to increased flooding and droughts. Investments are urgently needed in nature-based solutions to strengthen the resilience of ecosystems, including forest landscape restoration and conservation, to enhance the resilience of ecosystems against climate change.

### Drivers and underlying causes of deforestation and forest degradation

30. Pioneering shifting cultivation is the greatest single proximate driver of deforestation and forest degradation in the Lao PDR. The expansion of agriculture into forested areas in sectors such as agricultural land expansion (19%) and promotion agriculture development (13%) are also significant contributors. Other drivers include road construction, infrastructure development, and urbanization.

31. Shifting cultivation, if managed properly (including control of fires), is sustainable (i.e., rotational shifting cultivation). However, in the Lao PDR, it has been practiced in an unsustainable manner, with a lack of rotation practice in northern Lao PDR (i.e., pioneering shifting cultivation):
- Ecosystem services are being lost due to conversion into cash crops combined with a growing population.
- Fallow periods are becoming shorter, leading to lower productivity, increased soil degradation and the need to clear new areas for agriculture. This is particularly evident in the uplands of northern Lao PDR where subsistence crop in northern Lao PDR – into forest areas through slash-and-burn practices is typically a last resort option and not an optimal land use.
- The shifting cultivation practice acts as a safety net for poor and vulnerable groups.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 17

## Agricultural factors: Upland agricultural yields are often limited by seasonal precipitation. For example, upland rice is highly susceptible to drought, weed infestations and a lack of soil conservation practices to limit erosion. The conversion of forest land for agriculture has led to significant reductions in soil fertility – as much as 80% over 5 years. This reduction of yields drives villagers to clear new areas of forest, leading to deforestation and the loss of biodiversity. Upland rice production also places and pressures on other forest areas.

## Policy and institutional factors: Inappropriate land use planning and inappropriate land use planning is a major underlying cause of deforestation, either because of the complete absence of plans or through a lack of compliance with plans (which are often poorly designed). The lack of enforcement of existing laws and regulations also contributes to this. The absence of integrated spatial planning and watershed planning, as well as village-level participatory land use planning is another major cause of deforestation.

## Economic factors: Strong regional markets, especially in neighboring countries, continue to drive the expansion of agriculture into forest areas. This is particularly true for the export of timber products to China and Vietnamese markets is increasing and incentives clearing forests for agriculture. Laotian agribusinesses are also expanding their operations into forested areas to supply the growing demand from Chinese and Vietnamese market. A further analysis of the role of markets and MMSDs can be found in Chapter 3.2.1.

## Agro-technological factors: The expansion of cash crops is seen as a direct route out of poverty for households and an important source of income for many rural households. However, the lack of appropriate agricultural extension services development in Lao PDR, weak land use planning, law enforcement and agro-technological factors contribute to deforestation.

## B.1.5 Priorities for Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation in the Agriculture, Forestry and other Land Use Sector

### The primary barriers to sustainable forest management and restoration include gaps in forest governance and regulation, lack of capacity and skills for land tenure, poor financing for forest management, and insufficient skills and experience in sustainable forest management.

#### 34. The importance of climate action and sustainable land management are enshrined in the country's highest-level policy documents: the National Green Growth Strategy (2018), the Strategic Action Plan on Climate Change (now finalized), the National Green Growth Strategy 2020, the Strategy on Climate Change with a vision to the year 2050

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

## Background

The (draft) Climate Change Strategy (2021) provides a comprehensive strategy for climate change adaptation and mitigation in the forestry sector, for both mitigation and adaptation. It states that nature-based solutions should be promoted to counter major emission drivers such as fires, landslides and droughts, and that they can also provide co-benefits.

## Key Elements

### 35. The (draft) Climate Change Strategy (2021)

The strategy includes a number of key elements:

- **Enhancing deployment of ecosystem-based adaptation such as crop cover, wetland protection, forest and landscape for food security, water resources management, and climate change resilience**.
- **Restoration of degraded forests and landscapes**, including the restoration of 5 million hectares by 2030.

### 36. Integrated well-designed and planned interventions are needed that consider the interaction of deforestation, land degradation, and forest fires. The strategy is in line with the National Forest Policy of the country (Act No. 19/2009), as well as its action plans.

### 37. In terms of adaptation, forest, agriculture and water resources are listed as priority sectors for climate change adaptation where there are three main pillars: (i) promoting resilience in forestry production and forest ecosystem services; (ii) promoting the technical capacity in the forestry sector for managing forests under climate change; and (iii) promoting integrated land use planning, natural resource management and environmental investments.

### 38. The updated NDC contributes to building climate resilience by:

- **Enhancing forest cover at USD 17.1 billion**.
- **Strengthening water resources information systems for climate change adaptation**.
- **Promoting sustainable land use practices in agriculture, forestry and water sectors**.

### 39. The updated NDC highlights the barriers and challenges for building climate resilience:

- **Weak institutional capacities**, limited sectoral actions, action plans and indicators in most key sectors.

### B.2.1 Need for Project 2

* The overarching programme is to support the Government and people of Lao PDR in further changing the present-day use of forests and landscapes and to ensure a transition to sustainable and climate resilient forest and land use systems.
* This project will be complementary and additional to Project 1 and directly increase the resilience of more than 273,000 villagers.

### Project 2: Forests and Land Use Systems (FLUS) (2018-2024): USD 56 million

## B.2.3 Barriers for REDD+ and strengthening the resilience of ecosystems and local livelihoods

### Barrier 1: Inadequate institutional capacity and coordination

- The GCF is fully committed to improving the policy environment under Chapter B.1 (and further elaborated in Chapter D.5), and moving towards low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways.
- The GCF will continue to support the Government of Lesotho in implementing its NDCs, which include a conditional commitment to invest in low-emission and climate-resilient land management practices.

### Barrier 2: Synergies between REDD+, adaptation, and local land use planning not realized due to limited institutional capacity

- There is substantial potential to improve adaptation outcomes and strengthen cross-cutting efforts for adaptation and mitigation.
- The GCF will continue to support the Government of Lesotho in implementing its NDCs, which include a conditional commitment to invest in low-emission and climate-resilient land management practices.

### Barrier 3: Structural deficiencies in law enforcement

- There continues to be weak policy coherence and limited human resources for law enforcement.
- The GCF will continue to support the Government of Lesotho in implementing its NDCs, which include a conditional commitment to invest in low-emission and climate-resilient land management practices.

As low-income households are under staffed and under-equipped, lacking up-to-date maps and GPS for operations, and even lacking vehicles and budgets to travel to the field. Project 1 developed standardized operational protocols and tools for data collection and analysis, as well as a mobile application for data entry.

Nonetheless, there is a need for continued support with capacity building for the operationalization of these systems in the provinces covered under Project 2. The project will also provide technical assistance to scale up the additional 3 provinces covered under Project 2.

### Barrier 1: Inadequate land use planning within landscapes, which can increase climate change risk and vulnerability by not addressing linkages between land use and upstream and downstream areas. Land-use changes in many valleys is a major underlying cause for deforestation from shifting cultivation and expansion of settlements. The conversion of forested lands to agricultural uses and border demarcation often leads to unclear rights and gradual encroachment into forests. Village-level participatory landscape planning (VLP) has been developed as an approach to address these issues, taking into account considerations such as watershed protection and biological corridors, and integrated into spatial planning exercises at district level. The project will support the development of VLPs in 120 villages within the Lao PDR, and is implementing this across 240 villages in 3 provinces. Additional support is needed to continue with the implementation of VLPs.

### Barrier 2: Insufficient practical experience and skills for implementing sustainable, deforestation-free and climate-resilient agricultural practices. The majority of households in rural areas are characterized by low productivity and cannot typically be considered "good agricultural practices". However, many smallholder farmers have a good understanding of the use of agroforestry systems (e.g., rubber trees) and certain cash crops (e.g., maize, job's tears and cassava) to less severely forested upland areas. Crop rotations and fallowing are also practiced in some cases, but these practices are not always sustainable or climate-resilient.

Consequently, producers seeking out more productive land turn to forest areas for the expansion of agricultural production. This is further exacerbated by the lack of access to markets and credit facilities, as well as a low productivity of agricultural production, as does the limited availability of government agricultural extension services. The majority of households in rural areas are characterized by low productivity and cannot typically be considered "good agricultural practices". However, many smallholder farmers have a good understanding of the use of agroforestry systems (e.g., rubber trees) and certain cash crops (e.g., maize, job's tears and cassava) to less severely forested upland areas. Crop rotations and fallowing are also practiced in some cases, but these practices are not always sustainable or climate-resilient.

#### Barrier 1: Often do not even have bank accounts and many do not maintain reliable financial records, which makes it very difficult for banks to understand the firm's profitability, cash flows, and ability to meet loan terms.

- **Agri-MiSMEs lack proper business and financial planning.**
    - Microfinance institutions (MFIs) are often unable to understand the types of lending products that are available.

#### Barrier 2: Insufficient practical experience and skills for implementing sustainable forest landscape management: Weak policy coherence and limited coordination between competing policy priorities is a key barrier. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 26

## Project 2: Main Objectives: Project 2 aims to generate two main co-benefits, namely:
1. Improved food security.
2. Enhanced conservation of biodiversity.

### Food Security
Food security will be improved through improved participatory land use planning and the adoption of sustainable agricultural practices, while consciously strengthening farmers' adaptive capacities and enhancing co-benefits related to food security and nutrition. This will be achieved by improving access to markets for smallholder farmers in Component 1 and through interventions in Component 2 and Component 3 that reduce deforestation and forest degradation and improve livelihoods.

### Biodiversity Conservation
Investments in NPs and MPAs will also strengthen the protection of important habitats for diverse species including the black gibbon, among others. Investments in NPs and MPAs will also strengthen the protection of important habitats for diverse species including the black gibbon, among others. Investments in NPs and MPAs will also strengthen the protection of important habitats for diverse species including the black gibbon, among others.

### Gender Equality and Social Inclusion
Gender equality and social inclusion are also at the core of the project's approach, and have been mainstreamed throughout all components of the project design, which are described in detail within Chapter B.3, D.1 G.1 and G.2.

## B.2.4 Assumptions and risks:
The project design is based on the following assumptions:

- National governments and local communities are committed to improving planning processes in a participatory manner with local communities, guiding the implementation of forest management plans, support villages in implementing their forest management plans.
- Agri-MSEs are willing to develop sustainable, climate resilient and deforestation free agricultural value chains, and engage in agroforestry practices.
- Vulnerable local communities are willing to implement jointly developed (village) forest management plans and engage in agroforestry practices.

These assumptions are considered to be well founded, as they have been informed through detailed feasibility and market studies conducted with the support of PDR and other project partners, as well as extensive stakeholder consultations conducted for the ER-Programme development process. These assumptions are further supported by the results presented in Annex 1, and more recently during the Project 2 development process (see the Stakeholder Engagement Plan in Annex 7).

## B.2.5 Theory of change diagram
The theory of change diagram illustrates how the project's theory of change, integrating the elements from Chapters B.2-2.B.4 above. Project activities are described in detail within Chapter B.3.

### Additional SDG benefits associated with the project are described in Chapter D.3.

## B

guidelines (skill capacities, training modules, etc.). Other adjustments have been made to the project design to strengthen the role of private sector actors and reflect lessons learned from project implementation in other countries. These changes are reflected throughout Section B.2 under each activity, while an overview of the progress under Project 1, second phase is provided in Annex A. The revised funding proposal is attached as Annex 2c. In addition, adjustments have been made to the reflection of Government of Leo PDR’s updated NDC, which highlights the importance of private sector engagement and adaptation to climate change for climate change mitigation in the agriculture and forestry sectors.

Due to the above changes, the project implementation process at village level has significantly adjusted in terms of terminology and the role of EPF who will channel matching grants to sign MSMEs, as shown in the revised funding proposal. These adjustments are consistent with the overall approach of the project, which is core of the project’s approach, and is fully integrated into the project design within all three components.

## Figure 12: Project implementation process at village level

### Implementation process at village level

| Step | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Step 1** | EPF submits a request for funding to the GCF. |
| **Step 2** | GCF reviews and approves the request, if approved, EPF signs an agreement with the MSMEs. |
| **Step 3** | EPF disburses funds to the MSMEs. |
| **Step 4** | MSMEs implement the project activities. |
| **Step 5** | MSMEs submit a report on their progress and achievements, which is reviewed by EPF. |
| **Step 6** | EPF disburses remaining funds to the MSMEs based on their performance. |

### B.1 Component 1: Creation of an enabling environment for REDD+ implementation

#### Output 1.0.1 Organisational structure and capacities of domestic (financial) institutions are strengthened to access, mobilise, manage, and monitor the use of climate finance from diverse public and private sources.

- **Output 1.0.1.1** EPF procedures is provided in Annex B under the Ethiopia Group Development Plan.
- **Output 1.0.1.2** Rotational changes from PFP to IUCN include the removal of activities on mainstreaming REDD+ into the NDC and socio-economic development plans, as well as the inclusion of a new activity on the establishment of a data system that will supported by JICA.
- **Output 1.0.1.3** Outputs under this output are aligned with the activities that contribute to the achievement of Objective 2 of GCF 2025: Guidance Note to support the completion of the PFP elements of the revised funding proposal template for PAP and SAP, p.4.

# Activity 1.1.1: Climate Change Funding Window and sustainable finance

As mentioned under both Project 1 and Project 2, there is potential to raise additional long-term climate finance remains partly still untapped. While both Project 1 and Project 2 are designed to support Lao PDR to unlock REDD+ emission reductions, the proposed funding window will be used for a broader range of activities.

This funding window is intended to mobilize resources from multiple sources to support the strengthening of the enabling environment to facilitate the shift from business as usual agricultural and forest land management towards low-emission development pathways. The funding window will also aim at increasing access to climate finance, including additional sources of public and private finance for both climate change adaptation and mitigation investments in agriculture and forests.

The funding window is designed to mobilize resources from multiple sources (e.g., the Green Climate Fund, other multilateral and bilateral funds, domestic revenues, etc.) as well as from additional sources of public and private finance for both climate change adaptation and mitigation investments in agriculture and forests. ### Activity 1.2.1 – Law enforcement and monitoring:

75. Enhanced law enforcement capacity is needed to support Lao PDR’s commitment to REDD+, as well as other high-level initiatives including PMU 15 and the FLEGT-VPA. As mentioned in B.2, illegal logging remains a major challenge for forest management authorities due to limited budgets and technical capacities as well as understaffing.

76. Project activities will support law enforcement capacity building at the national level by providing training to relevant authorities to address due to limited budgets and technical capacities as well as understaffing.

77. Project activities will also provide law enforcement capacity building at the provincial level, including through provision of training on Provincial Deforestation Monitoring Systems (PDMS), while supporting the implementation of the PDMS in the 3 provinces covered under Project 2 (Bolovens, Luang Namtha and Xieng Khouang). The project will support the development of a national law enforcement framework, provide revisions to the Decree on Protected Areas (ongoing), Foreststry Law (ongoing) and other relevant laws and regulations. This will be done in close collaboration with relevant authorities and stakeholders.

78. Project activities will also include providing training for forest management planning (June 2021), among others, which have created a stronger basis for reducing deforestation and forest degradation. The project will support the development of a national law enforcement framework to address outstanding barriers related to forest law enforcement and monitoring.

79. Project activities will provide law enforcement capacity building at the sub-national level by providing training on PDMS and best practices for forest monitoring and law enforcement, including through provision of training in the PDMS in the 3 provinces covered under Project 2 (Bolovens, Luang Namtha and Xieng Khouang). The project will also provide technical support to the Department of Forest Inspection and sub-national line agencies in all provinces on PDMS, FONAR and other relevant laws and regulations. Training will be provided by DORNE, DOFRI staff and their respective sub national line agencies in all provinces (on PDMS, FONAR and other relevant laws and regulations). The training will include the development of new curricula to be developed by the project and used for training and knowledge exchange, including introducing new staff. A consistent standard for law enforcement is applied across the ER-Programme area.

## B

### Extracted display of deforestation areas: Provides a weekly deforestation monitoring using Google Earth Engine (GEE) Script in Web Application online within PoMS (take 1–2 Days). The results are FREEDOM polygons, which are automatically displayed on the Web application running on a computer. Based on this information, POPI/PAPO and/or DFU/DAPF staff will verify the polygons and send it to the tablet for onsite verification.

### Onsite Verification: DFU/DAPF staff will visit the area where deforestation occurred in the field together with Villages. They will collect data from the villages and record them on the tablet, which is then sent back to PoMS via the Web Server in the Forest Inventory and Planning Division (FIPD).

### Reporting of Deforestation: The information collected by the tablet is semi-automatically transferred to the National Forest Monitoring Systems (NFMS) server, which then creates the reporting and statistical information.

## Land Use Planning and Use Planning (LUP) is an essential prerequisite and forms the base for Village Forest Management Plans (VFMPs), FLR, water protection activities (with a focus on EUE) and the Promotion of Agroforestry (PAF). The lead agencies responsible for land use planning activities at national level are the Department of Forestry (DoF), the Department of Agriculture (DA), the Department of Environment and Nature Conservation (DENAC) under the Ministry of Land Management, Tourism and Handicrafts (MLTH), the Department of Land (DoL) under Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MoNRE). At provincial level, PAFO and/or PAFRE or DAFO are responsible.

### 90. Priority Areas for LUP

- **Village Forest Management Plans (VFMPs)**: These plans (i.e., LUP phase 2), which outline village boundaries, categories and separates agricultural land use from forest zones, define future land use options and provide a basis for the development of Village Forest Management Plans (VFMPs) at the village level. This leads to clearly defined village land use regulations.
- **LUP Phase 2 Documents**: These documents need approval by the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MoNRE). The LUP phase 2 documents will be used as a basis for support the development of a "climate change adaptation supplement" to the LUP 2 guideline and related tools. This supplement will provide guidance on how to integrate climate change considerations into strengthening the use of climate information and climate adaptation measures within LUPs, at all levels (national, provincial, district and village). The LUP phase 2 documents have been prepared for Bokeo, Luang Namtha, and 50 villages in Houaphan, Sayaboury and Luang Prabang.
- **LUP Phase 3**: This phase will focus on the implementation of the LUPs. It will include focusing on land use compatibility (including upstream and downstream dynamics), ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) approaches, climate change considerations within the LUP process, forest zone restoration and landscape design, and the design and designation of eco-corridors.

### 91. Sub-Activity 3.2.1: Maintaining climate change adaptation into land use planning manuals and guidelines

- **Climate Change Adaptation Manual**: This manual will be developed in line with the LUP process through the development of an "adaptation supplement", which can be integrated into the LUP guideline and related tools.
- **Training Materials**: Training materials for the LUP Project 1, including capacity building efforts of provincial and district PLUP teams. Adaptation considerations will be included as part of the training modules.
- **Feasibility Study**: A summary of results will be mainstreamed into both the training materials, informative material, and guidelines.

### 92. Sub-Activity 3.2.2: LUP in target project districts (linked with Activity 1.2.1 and Components 2 and 3) on hotspot areas (E.G. GIZ and GoL, KfW, BMZ co-finance): Under this sub-activity PLUP will be developed for the six provinces of focus (Bokeo, Luang Namtha, Sayaboury, Houaphan, Phongsaly and Xieng Khouang) to ensure sustainable forest productivity of land use, while also promoting the sustainable management of forest areas. The LUP approach will be based on the LUP 2 guideline and related tools with a climate change adaptation supplement (developed under Sub-Activity 1.2.3). This sub-activity will focus on the implementation of PLUP in six provinces of focus, including: (i) land use planning workshops; (ii) development of land use plans (a summary of the PLUP process can be accessed in the activity sheet in the Feasibility Study).

### Activity 1.2.2 Knowledge management, FPC safeguards and gender:

This activity will facilitate comprehensive communication and exchange of information about topics like REDD+, climate risks and best practices for climate risk reduction in the agriculture and forestry sectors, and the role of women in these sectors. It will also support the implementation of the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and Gender Action Plan (GAP), and ensure compliance with FPC and safeguards (EE: GIZ and GoL; GoL co-finance). The ESMP is provided in Annex 6b; the Gender Assessment is provided in Annex B3a.

### Sub-activity 1.2.3.1: Knowledge management and engagement (EE: GIZ): Under this sub-activity, seminars will be conducted at the provincial level to engage national stakeholders and policymakers on Project 2 progress and share key lessons that can support the implementation of REDD+ in the agriculture and forestry sectors. These seminars will also produce a wide array of stakeholder meetings on REDD+, climate resilient agriculture, and sustainable land management (SLM) practices. The ESMP will provide guidance for these activities.

### Sub-activity 1.2.3.2: Implementation of the Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) and Gender Action Plan (GAP), and ensuring compliance with FPCs and safeguards (EE: GIZ and GoL; GoL co-finance): The ESMP is provided in Annex 6b; the Gender Assessment is provided in Annex B3a.

This sub-activity will be conducted through a series of activities, including information dissemination and IT services (e.g. website services), awareness raising campaigns on climate change and gender issues, and capacity building for project staff and local stakeholders on environmental social gender (ESG), among other topics. Subsidiary agreements will also be established to support monitoring and evaluation in close cooperation with NAMCO.

B.3.2.1.2.2: Market solutions for agricultural drivers of deforestation: Implementation of sustainable and deforestation free agricultural practices (on 17 ha)### 104 Community-based Agroforestry Practices (CBAP) will be implemented in the project area, and continues to contribute to emission reductions through reducing the expansion of agricultural activities into forested landscapes. It adheres to the principles of the Promote Sustainable Agricultural and Forestry Practices (PSAFP) and the definition of 31 sustainable farming models in a “White List”. These farming models are based on the promotion of agroforestry, intercropping, mixed cropping and agrosystemic practices; the promotion of intercropping, mixed cropping and agrosystemic systems; and the adoption of low-emission processing technologies. The project area will be divided into 104 CBAPs, each covering an area of 17 ha. These support agricultural diversification, which will build the resilience of smallholder farmers – who are often dependent on a single cash crop for their livelihoods. This is in line with the principles and practices of the implementation of the PSAFP approach and the associated “White List”, with funding from the VFAFs developed by the project area’s local government authorities, including 104 CBAPs in Hounoua, 25 in Luingu Naamta, Bokele and Oudomay and 50 additional villages in Hougang, Sayoundi and Luingu Pekhong.

105.In contrast to the initial conceptualization under Project 1.### 104 CBAPs now includes a stronger focus on climate resilience and deforestation free agricultural practices (on 17 ha) as drivers of deforestation. It builds on recent in-country experiences and studies that note the relevance of engaging with and supporting smallholder farmers to implement sustainable, climate resilient and deforestation-free and low-emission agricultural value chains. Only if the private sector further develops its potential for scaling up these practices will it be possible to achieve a significant reduction in deforestation. Project: Sustainable, climate resilient, and deforestation-free practices are promoted among target beneficiaries (mostly swidden upland farmers) through the use of a "White List" which defines suitable agricultural land uses for each village.

The Project specifically focused on strengthening climate change adaptation within the approach (building on improved livelihoods and food security). The project has developed and implemented a PSAP (Participatory Sustainable Agricultural Planning) process, and this is now being scaled up to cover all villages in the project area. This PSAP process was developed under this activity and will be used for the implementation of its VFAGs (Village Financial Assistance Grants).

## Sub-Activity 2.1.1.1 Capacity building and knowledge management for sustainable, climate resilient and deforestation-free agricultural practices and technologies (EE: GIZ; GoL - IFAD co-finance)

The project will provide capacity building to the villages through training on PSAP processes and considerations into PSAP training material through the development of an adaptation supplement. It will also provide technical assistance in the implementation of PSAP processes at village level.

Project 2 provinces (Boleng, Luang Namtha, and Ou/doum) are focused on sustainable, climate resilient and deforestation-free agricultural practices and technologies. The project has developed a PSAP process for implementation (2.1.1.3) which covers the entire project area and is now being implemented in all villages.

## Sub-Activity 2.1.1.2 Scaling up of PSAP planning processes to cover 290 villages (EE: GIZ; GoL - IFAD co-finance)

Selected villages will be supported through the scaling-up of the PSAP P2 process (Objective 1.2.3).

Trained PSAP teams (Sub-Activity 2.1.1.1) will start the PSAP planning process village by village.

Under Phase 1, PSAP teams will conduct a baseline survey and develop a PSAP plan for each village specifically at replacing deforestation-prone practices such as processing and rotational shifting cultivation with more sustainable land use practices (e.g., crop rotation, agroforestry). Where customary land use rights are established, the primary impact pathway is to break the cycle of shortening representation of forest tenure. The PSAP process will also support villages in developing plans to compensate for these by opening up new cultivation areas. Based on the PSAP Guidelines developed under this activity, PSAP teams will develop a village-level PSAP plan (referred to as the "White List") and will be supported in finding the option that best fits their circumstances (for a detailed description of the PSAP process please refer to Annex 2).

This sub-activity will be covered by GCF grants and co-finance from BMZ. GCF funds will support project staff in providing technical assistance to villages during the PSAP planning phase, while BMZ will provide financial resources for PSAP planning at the village level (including travel costs for PSAP staff members) to visit with the villages with 5-6 staff members.

## Sub-Activity 2.1.1.3 Implementation of PSAP Agricultural Activity and Investment Plan (EE: GIZ; GoL - BMZ co-finance)

The implementation of the PSAP plans will ultimately depend on the farmers' willingness to adopt new agricultural practices. The project will provide technical assistance consisting in the provision of information (see Sub-Activity 2.1.1.2) and ongoing extension and technical support services, including training and capacity building for village-level PSAP teams (EE: GIZ; GoL - IFAD co-finance). # Table B: Selection of "White List" farming models and their adaptation and mitigation benefits

| Threat / Climate Change | Climate Change (CC) impacts and risks | Adaptation benefits | Mitigation benefits |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Coffee**<br>Intensification and increase in extreme events (floods through droughts)<br>Increasing temperatures and precipitation variability (in the seasonality of the rain)<br>Changing production zones due to climate change (with impacts on ecosystems)** | CC Impacts: Arabica coffee needs to adapt to a warmer climate. Northern Lao PDR some plantations are already affected by increasing temperatures with increases in annual mean temperature, which is expected to increase gradually. There is high vulnerability of the coffee sector to extreme weather conditions** leading to decreased yield and increased susceptibility to disease infestation** The alternation of wet and dry seasons due to climate change leads to water stress and reduced soil fertility. | Interventions: Planting under shade trees as a multi-story agroforestry system, which provides protection from the sun and reduces evaporation. Improved irrigation and watering management at a landscape or basin scale, which increases water availability for coffee plants, increasing soil moisture due to increased infiltration and reduced runoff. Increasing bioclimatic suitability for Arabica coffee by selecting varieties that are more tolerant of high temperatures in a wide range for rubber trees. This is important as with increasing temperatures, the growth rate will increase, noting that with increasing temperatures, the vulnerability to pest infestation** | Benefits: Protection stabilization and improvement (reducing evaporation from water, soil moisture and nutrients) Variety planted under shade trees increases rainfall interception Increased efficiency of irrigation due to better climate buffering Diversification reduces risk of tree failure |
| **Rubber**<br>Intensification and increase of extreme events (floods through stormy weather) and increased pest infestation | CC Impacts: Rubber is a tropical crop that grows best in lowland forests, enabling the implementation of ecosystem-based adaptation practices that strengthen the overall resilience of forest ecosystems. The vulnerability to pests and diseases increases with increasing temperatures. | Interventions: Multi-story agroforestry systems (e.g., rubber trees under shade trees) which provide protection from the sun and reduce evaporation. Improved irrigation and watering management at a landscape or basin scale, which increases water availability for coffee plants, increasing soil moisture due to increased infiltration and reduced runoff. Increasing bioclimatic suitability for Arabica coffee by selecting varieties that are more tolerant of high temperatures in a wide range for rubber trees. This is important as with increasing temperatures, the growth rate will increase, noting that with increasing temperatures, the vulnerability to pest infestation** | Benefits: Multi-story agroforestry systems provide better GHG sequestration. Increased soil organic carbon |

## B

### Sub-activity 2.1.2: Scaling up the VFAG approach to 290 villages (EE, GIS, GIZ, IAF) co-financing (implemented by GIZ)

#### 112. ICCF grants and/or finance from BMZ and GIZ will finance this sub-activity. GCF grants will be used for enabling monitoring by the VFAOs, developing climate-informed training modules and training sessions, providing gender-responsive extension support at the village level (including tree planting activities), and conducting knowledge exchange meetings and workshops at the cluster district and level.

#### 113. To support shifting cultivation and upland farmers in their transformation of land use towards sustainable and resilient agricultural practices, GCF grants will be used for enabling investments in innovative, sustainable, and deforestation-free production systems requiring grant support to establish and maintain these systems. The GCF funds will also be used for the implementation of climate-smart fund selected activities from the “White List” and annual village forest management activities. A Village Forest Management Committee (VFMC) will be established at each VFAG.

#### 114. VFAGs are managed by a committee of 3 selected village representatives who are endorsed by the district government. The committee is responsible for the implementation of the project in their respective villages. Each VFAG is the nearest commercial hub, following the VFAG Guidelines (see activity sheet in the Financial Plan). The GCF funds will be used to establish and maintain the production systems that will be transformed through the Climate Change Funding Window to the account. Transferred funds will be used among other things for the purchase of seeds, fertilizers, tools, and equipment. Funds will also be used directly but not be used to pay for approved items needed to establish and implement the chosen activity from the “White List” or annual village forest management activities. The GCF funds will be used in addition to the amount provided by BMZ and GIZ. The GCF funds will also be used by channeling bonus payments of up to €10,000 EUR (the amount depends on the size of the VFAG) per year for each household member who is a member of the VFMC. This will be implemented over a monitoring period of 2 years. Funds from the bonus payments will be used in the same way for further expansion of the project.

#### 115. Sub-activity 2.1.3: Scaling up the VFAG approach to 290 villages (EE, GIS, GIZ, IAF) co-financing (implemented by GIZ). Through this sub-activity the project will establish operational VFAG funds in the GCF window.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

## Sub-activity 2.1.2.2 Capacity building of new VFAG committees (EE; GIZ)

This sub-activity will support capacity development of VFAG committees to be able to effectively manage the VFAG fund. The Committee is responsible for ensuring that all income and expenditures within the VFAGs are managed in a timely, correct, and complete manner.

### Key responsibilities:
* **The VFAG President** assumes the leadership of the Committee and the VFAGs. They are charged with ensuring that the VFAG funds are used as intended.
* **The VFAG Cashier** is in charge of accounting and recording all money transactions within the VFAG. He/she is also responsible for safeguarding cash stocks.
* The VFAG Treasurer is responsible for ensuring that all income and expenditures within the VFAGs are managed in a timely, correct, and complete manner. They keep a cash book, collect bank statements and ensure that all receipts are properly recorded.

They prepare quarterly reports, which are submitted from the VFAG committee to EE, as stipulated in the VFAG bylaws.

### For the new committee:
For the newly formed committee to perform these tasks appropriately, each member must be familiarized with the rules and standards that have been defined in the VFAG Guidelines and govern the entire process. Committees will also receive training on financial management and general capacity building on financial management.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2.3 Monitoring of VFAGs (EE; GIZ and SGS)
Under this sub-activity, the necessary actions to ensure that all committees adhere to VFAG guidelines will be carried out, and corrective actions are taken in the event of non-compliance with the VFAG guidelines (actions as described in detail in Sub-Activity 2.1.3.1).

This sub-activity will be entirely financed by GIZ, and will cover equipment to support VFAG monitoring (comprising a laptop, printer, scanner, etc.).

## Activity 2.1.3 Watershed forest management to support small-scale irrigation projects (EE; GIZ, ADB and IFAD co-finance implemented by GIZ)

### Sub-activity 2.1.3.1 Forest resources management in selected watersheds
Specifically, the activity is ensuring that the forested lands are managed sustainably through improved land use practices (e.g., agroforestry, livestock grazing, and agricultural practices), and reduced pressure from drivers of deforestation and degradation by addressing key underlying causes such as lack of alternative livelihoods, poor access to markets, low soil fertility, low productivity, lack of value adding activities, weak negotiation-marketing skills. The SRWSM and PICSA projects will be implemented in the following watersheds: Phatang, Khungyang, Sayaboury, and Houaphan. These crops grown on irrigated land plots in 11 schemes which will be rehabilitated and modernized by SRWSM, PICSA, and GIZ. The project will also support the implementation of agroforestry practices to improve agriculture capacity building for Water User Groups (WUGs) and provision of farmer group investment facilities. A total of 40 farmers’ groups are expected to be supported in each watershed. Training workshops will be promoted and first market assessments have been conducted (Sub-Activity 2.1.3.1). A total of 11 irrigation schemes will be rehabilitated and modernized by SRWSM, PICSA, and GIZ (Sub-Activity 2.1.3.2). Finally, nutrition-sensitive agriculture is implemented in all target villages of SRWSM/PICSA (sub-activity 2.1.3.3).

**Page 46**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 42

## Sub-Activity 2.1.3 Market oriented production:
This sub-activity is applying a value chain (market) linking producers and consumers of high-value crops and rural farmers to the market, and operating on the 13 subprojects (11 irrigation schemes) have been consulted while making market assessments. These assessments are based on consultations with key stakeholders including smallholder producers and other actors of the high value crop (HVC) value chain, the prices, and margins at the various stages in the HVC supply chain. The results of these assessments will be used to design a market access. These completed assessments are currently being reviewed. Based on these assessments, the project will develop a market access strategy for each subproject, including plans for the dry season following the completion of the RSPs, identifying the targeted markets (wholesalers, retailers, etc.), and developing strategies for accessing these markets through the HVC's value chains to discuss investment needs from the SRWSMIP cash grant programme.

## Sub-Activity 2.1.3.2 Watershed ecological services protected:
The Project will contract private sector companies to provide watershed management services in the representative catchments.

## Sub-Activity 2.1.3.3 Improved nutrition status:
PCAS and SRSWM were designed as nutrition sensitive interventions, with a focus on improving food security and the improvement of nutrition awareness and Nutrition Sensitive Agriculture (NSA) activities of the SRWSMIP programme. The project will continue to implement these interventions in all provinces for nutritionally vulnerable groups and school-based nutrition activities in all target provinces. District Nutrition Committees will be strengthened through capacity building, and a provincial coordination mechanism will be established to ensure that the nutrition intervention packages are coordinated between different sectors. Future priorities will be on strengthening the coordinating function between district nutrition committees and other relevant stakeholders such as the Ministry of Health, and guidance on nutrition interventions.

## Activity 2.1.3 Implementation of benefit sharing plans for food-smart agriculture and sustainable land management:
The project will implement the Benefit Sharing Plan (BSP) in all subprojects under the GCF Project 1 and 2 and the main investment projects contributing to the Lao PDR GFF programme, aligning with the GCF’s Strategic Objective 3. As such, the GCF-funded Project 1 and Project 2 and the implementation of the Benefit Sharing Plan (BSP) in all subprojects under the GCF Project 1 and Project 2 are aligned. Through World Bank co-financing, this activity will sustain the implementation and results of activities related to food-smart agriculture and sustainable land management beyond the lifespan of GCF Project 2, with specific linkages to activities 2.1.1 and 2.1.2. The activity will be implemented in all subprojects under the GCF Project 1 and Project 2 and will contribute to the implementation of unsustainable agricultural practices and support farmers’ transition to CSA practices and technologies with higher resilience to climate change, while ensuring that the project is aligned with the Lao PDR’s National Agricultural Investment Plan. It will be followed to ensure that in addition to climate-related risks considered above related to social, environmental, gender, and economic safeguards are also effectively integrated.

## Sub-activity 2.1.4 Operationalization of benefit sharing plan for climate-smart agriculture and sustainable land management (SLM) in forest landscape communities (FLCs)

### Output 2.1 FLCs are exclusively financed using World Bank co-finance. No GCF funds are required for the implementation of this activity.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.4.1 Operationalization of benefit sharing plan for climate-smart agriculture and sustainable land management in forest landscape communities (FLCs) - sub-activity 2.1.4.1

This activity will support the development of a benefit-sharing plan that ensures fair distribution of benefits from the implementation of climate-smart agricultural practices, such as agroforestry techniques such as terracing and intercropping and are all aligned to CGIAR principles and will bring direct benefits to FLC communities.

### Output 2.2 Agri-MSEs are capacitated and have improved incentives and access to finance to invest in marketing and processing for locally sourced deforestation-free and climate resilient agricultural products

#### Activity 2.2.1: Catalyzing private sector investments in sustainable, climate-resilient, and deforestation-free value chains

This activity will become clear that the cooperation with local agribusinesses needed to be further defined and that a more comprehensive approach was needed towards establishing sustainable deforestation-free value chains. The focus of this activity is on chain development for smallholder farmers (Activities 2.1.1, 2.1.2) and upland farmers in target villages (Activities 2.1.1 and 2.1.2). The focus of the activity is on value chain development for smallholder farmers (Activities 2.1.1 and 2.1.2), and upland farmers in target villages (Activities 2.1.1 and 2.1.2).

This activity will support the development of sustainable, climate-resilient, and deforestation-free value chains through engagement with relevant agri-MSEs (Small and Medium Enterprises) and micro-enterprises (MMEs). This engagement with agri-MSEs aims to ensure that there is a strong link between the results of the activities under Project 1 and the results of the activities in the target villages. A categorization of the most relevant agri-MSEs for contributing to the results of the project will be developed during Project 2.

### Sub-Activity 2.2.1.1: Climate resilient and deforestation free value chain development (E.G. EIZ)

This sub-activity will directly assist a selected number of agri-MSEs through value chain and business management support, including financial management, technology innovations to lower emission value chains, simple market analyses and planning, and technical assistance for the implementation of climate resilient and deforestation free practices.

This sub-activity will provide matching grants for the marketing of sustainably produced commodities in accordance with the White List.

## Matching grants are defined as partial contribution for investments to support sustainable value chain development in agriculture and forestry.

- **50% of the total investment amount comes as a grant channelled through EPF, and**
- **50% of the total amount comes from an equity co-finance of the ag-MiME or as a loan from a local financial institution.**

### After a selective process of suitable companies (**) and the necessary contractual agreements, the project will be implemented in accordance with the following criteria:

#### Table 6: Maximum matching grant amount according to ag-MiME categorization

| Company Type | Maximum Grant Amount | Number of grants |
|--------------|---------------------|------------------|
| Category A   | EUR50,000           | 20               |
| Category B   | EUR30,000           | 10               |
| Category C   | EUR15,000           | 4                |

#### Table 7: Eligibility criteria for consideration of the matching grant mechanism

**Eligible sector and value chain description**

- **Sector:** Agriculture
- **Value Chain:** Agri-processing or marketing at least 1 commodity within the subset of the "White List" (**)

**Company types: ownership**
- Sole ownership, partnerships, and joint ventures.

**Business purpose of the applicant and/or eligible investment area**
- Machinery
- Infrastructure investment (e.g., storage facility etc.) (max 50% of grant)
- Renewable energy
- Merchandising

**Company Age**
- Company must be in operations for at least 2 years.

**Company registration**
- Must be registered according to the Law on PDR.

**Company operational location**
- Must have operations in one of the target provinces (at the province, district, or municipality level).

**Financial right & accounting requirements**
- Must have a bank account at the point of matching grant application; must show financial statements for at least one year.

**Matching contribution**
- The ag-MiME must meet 50% of the matching grant with a contribution of its own (**)

(*) The screening process will look into various technical, financial, and institutional factors, as well as environmental and social safeguards and gender considerations. These include: climate change adaptation and mitigation; water resource management; ecosystem services; environmentally friendly packaging; transport management; and higher values for products for alternative markets.

(**) A company that has no active participation to the agriculture value chain (e.g., pure logistics company) would not be eligible.
(*** ) The contribution from the ag-MiME will be verified by the EE.
(****) The contribution of the ag-MiME will be verified by the EE.
