B.13 Component 3: Climate change mitigation measures through forestry

### B.13.1 Component 3.1: Forest management (FMM) (including VFA establishment and operationalization). and aims to strengthen the resilience of forest ecosystems and the livelihoods of communities in the project area by promoting sustainable forest management practices, the conservation and promotion of FLU. The Component supports measure through the following forest categories as in PFM 31:

- Production forests are natural and planted forests classified for the purpose of timber and NTFP production and harvesting in accordance with the Forestry Law (although PFM 31 has imposed a ban on such harvesting since 2013).
- Protection forests are forests classified for the function of protecting water resources, riverbanks, roadsides, etc., as well as for soil conservation. They are managed to ensure protection from natural disasters and environmental protection. In forest protection, individuals and villages may not harvest timber or NTFPs but they can collect firewood for their own consumption. They are managed by the Department of Forestry.
- Conservation forests (CFs) and Natural Protected Areas (NPAs): forests classified for the purposes of conserving nature, preserving plant and animal species, forest ecosystems and other sites of natural, historical, cultural, tourism, environmental, educational and scientific value within conservation areas. They are managed by the Department of Forestry.
- Forest products for their own consumption.

#### 147 Notable changes from PFM 31 include the management of deforestation from land use, involving strengthening the consideration of climate information and integration of climate change adaptation measures with the VFM and NFM management plans, and facilitating the implementation of the VFA. The project area will be characterized by a high level of forest cover (80%), which has been facilitated in the facilitation of the investment of mixed-beds payments into the project area through the improvement of the approved PFM 31.

#### For Activity 3.1.1: VFA(s) have to be in place after Village Forest Management Planning is completed, to provide funds for the implementation of the agreed forest management plan. FPC, subgroups and gender - FPCs will be to be comprised as a partnership between village forests (village forests may be characterized by production, protection or conservation purposes), village forest management plans, knowledge management, FPC subgroups and gender. FPC has to be completed as a partnership.

#### For Activity 3.1.2: VFA(s) have to be in place after Village Forest Management Planning is completed, to provide funds for the implementation of the agreed forest management plan. FPC, subgroups and gender - FPCs will be to be comprised as a partnership between village forests (village forests may be characterized by production, protection or conservation purposes), village forest management plans, knowledge management, FPC subgroups and gender. FPC has to be completed as a partnership.

#### For Activity 3.1.3: VFA(s) have to be in place after Village Forest Management Planning is completed, to provide funds for the implementation of the agreed forest management plan. FPC, subgroups and gender - FPCs will be to be comprised as a partnership between village forests (village forests may be characterized by production, protection or conservation purposes), village forest management plans, knowledge management, FPC subgroups and gender. FPC has to be completed as a partnership.

The VFMPS guidelines already under implementation in Project 1 (CIP-GIZ2016) provide the following: i) forest zoning; ii) fire prevention and control; iii) pest and disease prevention (e.g. digging fire breaks, ploughing firebreaks, controlling burning of the fires); etc., building climate resilience through the development of a community-based early warning system for forest seedlings; reforestation to promote river bank stabilization and ecosystem-based adaptation; identification and marking of protected areas; ii) establishment of buffer zones around village forests; iii) fencing off of some parts to encourage regeneration; product weeding around valuable tree seedlings; iv) fire prevention and control (e.g. in case of fire damage, shifting cultivation, excessive degradation/no cutting; direct burning in the forest); v) pest and disease prevention (e.g. digging fire breaks, ploughing firebreaks, controlling burning of the fires).

## Activity 1.2.2: However further guidance is needed within the context of VFM to strengthen the resilience of forest ecosystems and local livelihoods. This sub-activity will provide the following support:

### Revisions to the VFM guidelines, including the development of a revised training manual and climate adaptation guidelines, and systematically considered through the process (from planning to implementation and monitoring). This will ensure that all relevant stakeholders are aware of the importance of forest ecosystem resilience and identify suitable measures to strengthen the adoption of ecosystem-based adaptation measures within VFM. Some measures are already often included within VFMPLs e.g., restoration and revegetation, sustainable forest management practices, etc.

### Development of training materials for training provincial VFM teams and training materials for district VFM teams on the revised guidance, and climate change adaptation within VFM.
### Training of trainers (ToT) to ensure that all relevant stakeholders are aware of the importance of forest ecosystem resilience and identify suitable measures to strengthen the adoption of ecosystem-based adaptation measures.

## 152. GCF grants and BMZ and BMG will be used for the implementation of this sub-activity. GCF grant will cover the provision of training materials (modules, manuals, etc.) and other related materials (e.g., printing and disseminating material) to facilitate VFM planning and conduct training, as well as training costs, and costs for development, printing and disseminating related materials for capacity building and awareness raising.

### Sub-activity 1.2.3: Development of VFM PLs, GCF, BMZ, and BMG are involved in this sub-activity. Based on the climate-informed PULP plans developed under activity 1.2.2, VMPMs are designed using a combination of national and international best practices and guidelines for forest management and sustainable development and sustainable management of village forests. Among others, tools developed by FAO for planning Forest and Land Use (FLU) and the Community Forestry Investment Plan (CFIP) will be used.

### VFM development will follow the following key stages as per the aforementioned guideline:

* **Stage 1: Planning and preparation**
* **Stage 2: Participatory Forest Resources Assessment (PFRA) and basic forest inventory**
* **Stage 3: Preparation of five-year village forest management plan (VFMPL).**
* **Stage 4: Implementation of VFMPL.**
* **Stage 5: Village forest management agreement**

### GCF grants will cover the provision of training materials (modules, manuals, etc.) and other related materials (e.g., printing and disseminating material) to facilitate VFM planning and conduct training, as well as training costs, and costs for development, printing and disseminating related materials for capacity building and awareness raising.

### For more detailed information on the various stages, refer to the Feasibility Study (FS) document. Additional information is available in the GCF project proposal (CP04D GIZ 2018, p. 16). To prevent overcutting of forest resources, the village forest management plan should be developed and implemented as a long-term strategy for sustainable forest use. The VFMPL should be prepared as a three-year action plan with annual targets to ensure that the village forest is not overexploited.

### For more information on national tree planting day and replacement of demarcation poles along the forest boundaries after 2-3 years: For more information on content, structure, and development process of VFMPLs, refer to the GCF project proposal (CP04D GIZ 2018). The VFMPL should be developed in accordance with the principles set out by the GCF project proposal. These principles are aligned with the national forest policy and other relevant policies and guidelines for sustainable forest management.

## Sub-activity 3.1.1: Implementation and monitoring of VFMPs (IE: GIZ and KfW co-finance)

### Background
The implementation of the Village Forest Management Plans (VFMP) is a key component in the development of the VFM plans and programs outlined in the Village Forest Management Guidelines, including the following stages:

* **Stage 1: Preparation** - This stage includes the preparation of the VFMPs. It will be carried out by the project team after they have signed the village forest management agreement, following annual plan of operation and which will be financed from the GCF.

* **Stage 7: Monitoring and evaluation** - Monitoring and evaluation will be in line with the PMS established and operated under sub-activity 1.2.1.

### Resources
Resources will be transferred to Village Forests (VF) following the procedures as approved by Project. VFAs will be responsible for implementing sub-activity 3.1.1, including their set up and implementation of the Guidelines for the Supervision and Operation of Village Forest Landscapes and the Guidelines for the Governance of Village Forest Landscape and Livelihood (GFLL). Programmes. The EFF will receive budget coverage from GCF for the implementation of sub-activity 3.1.1. A detailed description of the resources is available in Chapter B4 and the Climate Change Funding Window Operations Manual (Annex Z).

### Implementation
The project team will be responsible for implementing sub-activity 3.1.1, including the preparation of VFMPs. KfW, GIZ, GCF grants will cover training for PAFO and DAFO staff on best practices for village forest management to support VFM implementation, travel costs for government staff and transit to support on-the-ground visits to villages and facilitate with the project team in the development of the VFMPs. The project team will be responsible for implementing their VFMPs, and provincial exchange and knowledge dissemination events on best practices.

### GCF Finance
GCF finance will also provide direct investments in village forests for the implementation of their VFMPs, and to support on-the-ground visits (and travel costs) for government staff to visit villages and facilitate on-the-ground monitoring and checks).

## Activity 3.1.2: Implementation of benefit sharing agreements for Sustainable Forest Management (SFM) and Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR) (IE: GIZ, World Bank co-finance)

### Background
The project will implement the Benefit Sharing Agreement (BSA), which further provides the framework for the implementation of the Land Emission Reduction Programme. As such, the GCF BSA is closely aligned with the GIZ and KfW BSA, as well as the World Bank BSA, and are closely aligned (see institutions involved below).

### Activities
Activities will be implemented through the GFLL, and the FCPSF Emission Reduction Payment Agreement (ERPA), including the implementation of the associated benefit sharing plan (BSP). The project team will also seek to apply a landscape approach that will be informed by lessons learned from other projects, including DIPAD and ICBF. This activity will see to apply a landscape approach that will be informed by lessons learned from other projects.

### Programme Targets
The programme targets implementing and scaling up forest landscape restoration and management on at least 100,000 ha of degraded land in the project area through afforestation, reforestation, plantation development and agroforestry systems to enhance forest carbon stocks.

### The actors involved will include:
* Government agencies (at all levels).
* Rural communities, hereinafter named as communities.
* Actors in pilot initiatives, which will include the private sector, non-profit associations, and research and education institutions.

### Sub-activity 3.1.2.1: Operationalization of benefit sharing plan for SFM and FLR
This sub-activity will focus on operationalizing a benefit-sharing plan (BSP) to ensure that all stakeholders have an enabling environment for scaling the implementation of SFM and FLR, support the implementation of village forest management plans, and provide incentives for the development of commercial forest activities. The BSP will be placed at FLR in SFM, including with the objective of restoring degraded forests. The sub-activity group will work closely with the private sector to ensure that all stakeholders have an enabling environment and relationships with private sector actors including for the development of commercial forest activities.

The sub-activity will focus on operationalizing a benefit-sharing plan (BSP) at the national level, as well as at the local/national level and of communities. Emphasis and focus will be placed on the development of capacities of stakeholders to ensure that all stakeholders have an enabling environment for scaling the implementation of village land and forest resources, by supporting and scaling up SFM.

Outcome 3.2 NPA management plans are developed and communities are enabled and actively engaged in implementing conservation agreements in NPAs that enable sustainable forest management and forest conservation

### Activity 3.2.1: National Protected Area (NPA) management
This activity will support the development of NPA management plans with the NPA will address one over the barrier that there are insufficient resources (financial, technical and administrative) to support the effective implementation of NPA management plans. The NPA management plan will focus on the largest remaining high-carbon stock forests, which are particularly at risk of deforestation and forest degradation.

Based on the successful approach from ICBF, it applied under GCF project 1, and the experiences and lessons learned in the development of NPA management plans, this activity will support NPAs to develop NPA and national parks (NP) supported under Project 1, as well as three additional NPAs:

* **Pongolapoort National Park** in Limpopo province
* **Phalaborwa National Park** in Limpopo province
* **Phou Louy National Park** in Hhohho province and Nam Phou Npa in Sayaboury province. They span approximately 10,500 km² of land and are home to a diverse range of wildlife. Together they cover approximately 96% of the protected area is classified as forest.

Prioritizing areas for NPA management plans will be done by:

* **NPAs in Limpopo province:** In Luang Namtha, Nan Kao in Bokeo province and Phou Hi in Oudomxay province. They span 12 districts, which have all deliberately been selected to ensure that the most important areas for biodiversity conservation are included.

In particular, activity 3.2.1 will support adaptation mainstreaming within NPA planning and management (Sub-Activity 3.2.1.1), the revision of management plans for NPAs, scaling up participatory and cooperative processes in NPA management (Sub-Activity 3.2.1.2), the development of a monitoring framework to monitor forest concession agreements (VFICFA) in guardian villages (Sub-Activities 3.2.1.3 and 3.2.1.4). Such sub-activities will be implemented through the following measures: developing NPA management plans under project 1, considering lessons learned and best practices from the aforementioned projects. Together with these, this activity will support the development of a monitoring framework to monitor ecosystems against climate change, provide additional incentives for conservation, and strengthen local livelihoods in NPAs by mainstreaming adaptation into NPA planning and management as well as to SFM.

* **This will be addition to the activities reported under Project 2 Activity 3.1.1**

### Sub-activity 3.2.1: Capacity building on climate change adaptation and NPA management (EE-GIZ and GoJ)

This sub-activity will support climate change adaptation mainstreaming within NPA agencies and provide capacity development for NPA staff, including the development of guidelines to facilitate the integration of climate information and best practices for strengthening the resilience of NPA management plans. This will be achieved through the development of a climate action plan (see 1.2), and training provincial and district staff on revised guidelines, climate-informed NPA planning, and best practices.

### Sub-activity 3.2.2: Improvement of law enforcement and NPA conservation landscape (EE-GIZ and GoJ; GoL; GoF; GoLance)

The NPAs in the project area have NPA Management Plans that are currently being revised to include climate change adaptation considerations as part of their planning period. The revision of these management plans will build on the climate-informed land use plans developed by the LEAPS project and will be guided by the guidelines for climate change adaptation mainstreaming, based on guidance and capacity building provided under Sub-activity 3.2.1.

The revised NPA Management Plans will be implemented in collaboration with the National Parks. Specifically, it will include:

- Investment in law enforcement tools, including patrolling equipment, in line with the needs identified in the LEAPS.
- Development and implementation of Law Enforcement Action Plans (LEAPs) for 5 NPAs, supporting regular monitoring and enforcement of management plans where high-risk areas will be targeted by PAFO staff.
- Development and implementation of a biodiversity monitoring strategy aimed at evaluating the effectiveness of NPA management actions.
- Implementation of an integrated data management system to standardize the monitoring of enforcement efforts, threats and results over all NPAs.
- Capacity development for NPA and DAFO staff, support for strengthening inter-agency cooperation, and capacity building in law enforcement tools and equipment.
- Village engagement by EPAWMA and DAFO staff within guard zones.

### 176. Grants from GoJ and CoL co-finance will be used for the implementation of this sub-activity. GCF finance will cover costs associated with the development and implementation of LEAPs, including the cost of GPS, radios, IT equipment, cameras, sign boards and billboards for boundary demarcation, as well as other related events (including travel costs for government staff to visit villages within and in areas adjacent to NPAs).

The revision of NPA management plans will consider, among others: assessment of climate risks and vulnerability for the NPA, development of adaptation strategies, and identification of priority actions. These will be informed by best practices from the development and implementation of this LEAP will be considered when developing the plans in the other 5 NPAs. The project will also focus on lessons learned from each NPA and develop a harmonized approach.

#### Sub-activity 1.2.1.3: Development of new and amendment of existing village forest conservation agreements (VFiCA) (Ee, GIZ and GfK). This sub-activity will support the development of new and amendment of existing VFICAs in villages that are not yet covered by a VFiCA. The agreement will allow villages to access village-based grants for a set of eligible agricultural and forestry activities that support village-level forest conservation and sustainable land management (SLM) practices. The agreements developed by the ICFB project, which are based on a participatory process applying FPIC, and will be consistent with the GIZ’s VFiCA guidelines. These agreements will include provisions for adaptation to climate change and related risks and considerations for resilience building, linked with best practices for reducing GHG emissions and increasing carbon sequestration in forest ecosystems as well as livelihood enhancement.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.1.4: Implementation of VFiCA (sustainable land use, forest conservation and management) in villages covered by a VFiCA (Ee, GIZ and GfK). This sub-activity will support the implementation of VFICAs developed under the previous sub-activity. Technical and financial support will be provided to implement the activities agreed upon within the VFICA. The guidelines for the implementation of VFICAs developed by the ICFB project will be followed, which permit activities such as the following:153
* Natural regeneration of forest areas
* Improved forest use through bamboo management for income generation.
* Agroforestry for food security and income generation.
* Non-timber forest products (NTFPs) harvesting.
* Aquatic conservation zones for livelihood benefits.

#### B.4 Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagrams)

182 Since Project 1 will build upon Project 1 and expand the area being covered to the initial six provinces, the institutional arrangements already in place will only change slightly (see Figure 14 below). This is impacted through the ongoing implementation of PMF17, which validated the suitability of the implemented approach.

#### B.4.1 Project steering arrangement

The National Project Steering Committee (NPSC) provides administrative oversight of the project, ensuring coordination across ministries – initially for Project 1 and upon approval also for Project 2. The NPSC provides overall guidance on the implementation of the project and ensures that the project aligns with the Provincial Project Steering Committees (PPSC), the provincial REDD+ Task Forces, whilst ensuring compliance with national policies, strategies, and guidelines as well as international commitments and objectives. The structure of the NPSC builds upon the existing institutional structure for EeD in Lao PDR, which includes the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MoNRE) as the lead entity, GIZ, NSG, GfK as the Accession Entity (AE), and one or two Executing Entities (EEs). MoNRE will maintain overall responsibility for the project implementation. The AE and EEs are responsible for ensuring that compliance is provided to the national actors. The National Project Steering Committee will meet twice per year throughout the project implementation period, as well as on an ad hoc basis as and when required.

153 GIZ (2017), Village Fund Development and Management Guidelines.
154 MoNRE (2016), Lao PDR Forest Policy. The AE will lead the implementation of Activities 3.1.4 and 3.1.2 with FCPY funding, while the EEs will be responsible for implementing Activities 3.1.3 and 3.1.5. JICA, who previously was a cooperation partner under Project 1, will not be formally involved as an implementation partner under Project 2.

### Government of Lao PDR - represented by MAFF and MAFRRE as the main political partners

The Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Rural Development (MAFF), in cooperation with the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MAFRIE), will serve as the main political partners. The government is responsible for:

- That MAFF serves as the Chair of the REDD+ Task Force and is together with the Ministry of Finance responsible for disbursing payments to project beneficiaries, and that MAFF will receive results-based payments. MAFRIE is further responsible for the FFP, which will have two roles: a) As a knowledge management and capacity building support; b) once its capacities are satisfactorily built to potentially channel funds to beneficiaries.

| Micro, Small and Medium-sized Enterprises (MSMEs) | Granting grants (up to 50%) for climate resilient and agro-ecological enterprises | Agri-MSMs: Evidence of processing or marketing at least 1 commodity within the subset of the "White List". Enterprise development SMEs: See table indicative positive list of investment categories. |

20. The choice of payment modality for particular project elements has been made primarily on the basis of benefits to be derived from the project element (e.g., whether it is a one-off activity or an ongoing activity using pre-existing skills and financial resources). Note, however, that in some respects the distinction between upfront payments and performance-based payments is somewhat blurred. Upfront payments are clearly defined as such; however, if additional funding is required to benefit from subsequent payments, then, even for upfront payments there is a performance-based element.

24. Grant amounts will be in the range of approximately Euro 10,000 to Euro 50,000. The value of each grant payment will be determined by the pertinent NPMU team (on the basis of the budget request submitted by the beneficiary), the amount subsequently agreed with the NPMU technical team and the beneficiary on the basis of the approved project plan, and the actual performance achieved in terms of the quality and/or performance of the implemented activities. The basis for all payments besides matching grants is detailed above.

25. The Climate Change Funding Window will be open to applications from VFGs and MSMEs during joint planning meetings and through the participation/invitation of all concerned stakeholders (government, CSOs, private sector, etc.). Within those plans, the timelines, budgets, concerned organizations, and other relevant information will be determined.

26. Through the Climate Change Funding Window, the EFF will channel Euro 139 million of the GCF grant to support VFGs and MSMEs.

### B.4.4 Regulation, Taxation and Insurance

**Prerequisites:**

- The Federal Republic of Germany has concluded Framework Agreements on Technical Cooperation under international public law with the Government of Lao PDR, which provide for certain privileges and immunities in respect of German citizens and companies. These agreements cover matters such as taxes, customs duties, and fees. GIZ will endeavor to reach arrangements to have these privileges and immunities extended to include GCF proceeds. However, formal agreements will only be entered into after the project has been approved.

**Appendices: permits, licenses and land**

### Current Status

- The local currency in Lao PDR is the KIP (code: LAK). The project's local transactions will use LAK. The AE will manage GCF proceeds in Euro. GIZ will apply its standard rates for the conversion of currency payments.
- GIZ, as the AE, will ensure that project activities are adequately insured per JMC GIZ standard operating procedures and common practices. GIZ standard operating procedures require contractors to ensure adequate insurance coverage before commencing work on site. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

## Background

The forest sector is both a major carbon sink and a source of greenhouse gases. However, capacity and funding gaps remain. No developing country has ever managed to achieve sustainable management of its forests and landscapes alone anywhere in the world.

### The Lao PDR Context

The Lao PDR is one of 14 countries identified as being highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. Climate change is already affecting the livelihoods of the most exposed agricultural sector for their livelihoods and wellbeing. Climate change poses a risk to human well-being, food security, water resources, and biodiversity.

### Project Background

The Lao PDR Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR) Project is among prioritized terms in terms of climate change adaptation, and specifically highlights the need to strengthen cross-cutting measures for FLR implementation at national level. The AFOLU sector has substantial potential to strengthen cross-cutting measures, and Project 2 has been designed with this objective.

### Project Objectives

The project will support the Government of Lao PDR (GoLP) in strengthening its capacity to plan, design, implement, monitor, evaluate, and report on FLR activities. The project will provide information in planning and decision making, mobilizing financing for both mitigation and adaptation in the AFOLU sector.

### Building on, and coordinating with, complementary initiatives

Lao PDR will deploy GCF support to remove investment barriers and create enabling conditions for scaling up climate-smart investments in a sustainable environment for scaling up REDD+ and climate resilient land use activities (see also Sections B.5 and B.6). The project will also provide technical assistance to the GoLP to engage with private sector actors in the forestry and agriculture sectors. However, sustainably focused markets are currently not well developed in Lao PDR. In order for the private sector to be engaged in the development of these markets, it is necessary that significant technical support and targeted financial support is provided.

### Alternative 1 – More public financing

The Government of the Lao PDR (GoLP) is committed and contributes a substantial amount of public funding ($20 million to Project 1) and anticipates co-financing contribution of EUR 6.9 million to Project 2.

### Alternative 2 – More grants from other development partners

All of Lao PDR’s main development partners have expressed interest in supporting the project, and committed grant financing to Project 1, ADB and IFAD have committed loan packages for the implementation of Project 2. The World Bank (WBG) has committed $30 million for the restoration of forested landscapes.

### Alternative 3 – More concessional loans from other development partners

The World Bank (IFC) Carbon Fund is funding Lao PDR REDD+ results-based payments (RBP) project, which will provide a significant amount of financial resources to support the development of the Project. The Program is scheduled for the budget year and gender work assesses to match the project with the Program. The IFC has also committed concessional loans for technical assistance support, and all are geared to meet the country’s ambitious climate targets.

### Alternative 4 – Loans for households and agri-MTIs

The beneficiary groups - village administration committees (VACs), community forest management associations (CFMAs) and local government authorities - have expressed interest in accessing loans from the Lao PDR Development Bank (LPDB). However, there is limited ability for PDR to a. take on additional loans for climate change in this particular context. The LPDB has provided support for a pilot action and a green recovery to COVID-19 pandemic is urgently needed.

### Alternative 5 – More grants from other development partners

UNDP’s Lao PDR, 2021 (Mobilizing GCF) is also a Loan For...

B. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

216.The entire project approach is designed around a distinct exit strategy. GCF grant financing will enable the GCF to support the design and implementation of an exit strategy that aims at three main objectives: (i) ensuring effective use of grant resources [highlighted in throughout Section B.3, and further discussed within Annex Box 2.A]; (ii) creating conditions for a transition from project-based funding to a more sustainable financing model; and (iii) contributing to a sustained paradigm shift, and continue to mobilize additional public and private financial resources in support of REDD+ through continued scaling up of sustainable land use practices and investments [see Sections B.6 and B.2 here].

### Enable access to REDD+ results-based payments to facilitate the upscaling of cross-cutting measures

217.The project will continue to build on the lessons learned from Project 1, and continue to scale-up necessary activities that build up an enabling environment for REDD+ and sustainable land management (SLM) in general. These include: (i) strengthening law enforcement and monitoring; while scaling investments in deforestation free and climate-smart agriculture; (ii) supporting the development of a market-based approach to carbon sequestration and restoration. These measures will continue to help deliver emission reductions that enable Lao PDR to access results-based payments from REDD+ mechanisms, such as those under the UN-REDD Programme and other potential [e.g., UNFCCC] channels. The project’s focus on strengthening adaptation will also help build resilience in the face of climate change.

### Attract increased private sector investment

218.The project is designed to facilitate greater (and more sustainable) private sector engagement, by scaling up existing activities specifically devoted to private sector development [Component 2] will mobilize new and additional resources from the private sector. The project will also seek to engage with a wider range of actors with substantial potential to facilitate a transformational change in the target sectors. By developing a more enabling environment for private sector investment, the project aims to leverage USD 1.8 million for supporting the development of climate resilient and deforestation free value chains increasing the overall impact of the project. The project will also seek to attract additional resources by enabling an environment for facilitating additional private sector investment be further strengthened by improving the business case for private sector engagement in REDD+ and SLM, through improved law enforcement and monitoring that will create an improved investment climate by strengthening the institutional framework for REDD+ and SLM. This will help ensure greater transparency and accountability for sustainable land-based investments.

### Mobilize additional resources to support the scaling up of sustainable and climate resilient land management and REDD+

219.With GCF support, the structural funding gap will be closed by unlocking new private sector investment in the forestry and agricultural sectors. Matching grants for agri-MESMAs will catalyze private sector investments in deforestation-free value chains. The project will also seek to attract additional resources from planned VPA funds that will take into account new provisions under the forest law, enabling villages to benefit from the sale of their timber and non-timber forest products (NTFPs) directly to agri-MESMAs and villagers, and providing revenue to the Forest Protection Fund through forest license fees, and taxes. These measures are expected to increase private sector investment in sustainable land use and gain access. Project 2 [specifically Activity 1.1.1] will further explore additional and suitable sources of co-financing.

### Alternatives REDD+ results-based payments for Etsi generated in excess of the contract volume of the Lao PDR Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) under the UN-REDD Programme, such as the Licensing Emission by Accelerating Forest Fire Finance Co-participation or participation in broader voluntary carbon market demanding Ets from unsustainable projects. The project will

## Provide overall support for facilitating conformance with the methodological requirements (accounting and safeguards) of market-oriented carbon standards such as ARRTIES® or others.

- **Market and cooperative approaches under Article 6 of the Paris Agreement**, where Lae PDR will be required to ensure that all projects are consistent with the NDC, including the use of methodologies within their NDC, adherence to integrity principles, and with capacities to meet the requirements of an effective carbon market.
- **Overall support to the NDA to develop a financing strategy to identify and fundraise climate finance from domestic and international sources**, in line with the financial architecture for Lae PDR outlined in its NDC and that considers domestic government revenues as well potential strategies in place with private impact investors active in the region.

### 217 Figure 17 below provides an overview of the financing mechanisms the project plans to utilize to achieve the project’s overall carbon target, and envisages an overall long-term commitment of Lae PDR:

- **Develop the capacities of EPF and FPF to mobilize and channel climate finance:** The project will continue to strengthen the capacity of the EPF, which acts as an IFI, to manage parts of grant financing (including the GCF) in a more efficient manner. This will be achieved through a performance-based manner) through the Climate Change Funding Window to participating villages and communities. Project 2 will also continue to support the FPF in its role as the implementing agency for grants to agri-MSMEs. Project 2 project will further build the capacity of EPF as it undergoes its GCF accountability review, including by supporting the development of a new business plan for EPF and develop and implement their own GCF projects. Project 2 will also continue to support capacity building of FPF in its role as the implementing agency for grants to agri-MSMEs.
- **Broaden access to alternative livelihood opportunities that facilitate a transition towards low-emission, deforestation free and climate resilient livelihoods and land use:** Additional efforts will be made to ensure that the project’s approach to sustainable land management, including land use planning and reasonable law enforcement, will create a cascading effect and mutually reinforcing benefits for both climate change mitigation and adaptation. The project will include adaptation measures, including integrating planning in a landscape approach and scaling up climate resilient livelihoods and land use practices, which are consistent with the NDC and the ecosystems upon which they depend. The financial and economic analysis (Annex 3) demonstrates that these activities will provide significant co-benefits for biodiversity conservation values as well as many other social, economic and environmental co-benefits.
- **Ensure alignment of project activities with national policies:** The project is closely aligned with, and explicitly supported by government policies and plans. By aligning the project with key national priorities, it will ensure that the project’s benefits are maximized in the short term as well as after the GCF investment ends are very high.

218 It shall be the responsibility of the Government of Lae PDR, including the National REDD+ Task Force, Multilateral Donors and other stakeholders to ensure that the structural funding gaps for low-emission, deforestation free and climate resilient land use will be met by the National REDD+ Task Force with its grant mechanism. The project will also support the Government of Lae PDR in ensuring that it has the necessary resources, to ensure that the project is aligned with the GCF’s financial paradigm shift pathway. In addition, the project will support EPF and FPF as described above further facilitate improved monitoring, mobilization and channelling of public and private climate finance.

The Methodological Framework of the Carbon Fund allows flexibility of ER and the ARRTIES® standard, which is a market-based carbon standard. This will allow for the project to be consistent with the NDC and the ARRTIES® standard.

## BMZ financing: The total BMZ financing for the project amounts to Euro 311 million of which Euro 8.68 million will be implemented through GIZ as an Executing Entity in Lao PDR. This includes the following components: 2.1.1, 2.1.2, 2.1.3, 2.1.4, 2.1.5, 2.1.6, 2.1.7, 2.1.8, 2.1.9, 2.1.10, 2.1.11, 2.1.12, 3.1.1, 3.1.2, and 3.2.1 as well as parts of activities 3.1.1. The remaining Euro 2.5 billion will be implemented by KfW through its Village Forest Management Programme (VFMP).

## ADB will provide co-financing of Euro 1187 million to implement Activity 2.1.3 in conjunction with the Lao Government and IFAD as executing agencies for the same project, SRWSVM.

The IFAD PICSAs loan-project with the Government of Lao PDR will be financed Project 2 with Euro 504.6 million to support the implementation of Activity 2.1.3. IFAD will provide loans direct to the Ministry of Agriculture. The IFAD loan is a stand-alone, sovereign loan and will not be combined with any other financing instruments. It will cover both supervisory procurements. IFAD supervision will involve field missions to the project site as well as regular monitoring.

The World Bank through the FCFPR Carbon Fund will co-finance Project 2 with EUR 1564 million to support and sustain the implementation of activities under Components 2 and 3. World Bank financing will be provided in the form of a loan, which will be channeled through the operationalization of the Benefit Sharing Plan in the six target provinces.

### Co-Financing Flow

IFAD will sign loan contracts with the Lao Government (MoF/MAT) to channels funds to MAT and then onwards to the Departments in the Ministry and to sub-national agencies. The World Bank will provide a loan to the World Bank Trust Fund for advisory technical assistance to local partners. See Chapter B-4 for more detailed information.

Other financing (a.- mobilized private finance) and b.- grants from other sources: The project will also benefit from mobilized private finance from the private sector, and specifically align MMSMEs, for supporting the development of climate resilient and deforestation free value chains through the strategic use of matching grants (Activity 2.5.1).

## D.1. Impact Potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 228. The ER-P Reference Level (RL) accounting area is the same province (Bokwe, Houghsir, Limpopo), as that used for the baseline scenario described by the GCF project. Therefore, the geographical footprints of the ER-P Reference Level and the GCF project are the same.

### 229. The RL methodology is based on the same reference period (2015) as the RL of the Reference Level (RL), which was submitted and approved (on June 2018) by the FCPF Carbon Fund and is compliant with the Carbon Fund’s Technical Guidelines for the Development of Reference Levels (RLs). The RL methodology uses GCP terminology. For a detailed description of the Reference Level (RL) methodology, please refer to the ER-CD Project Document (PDD) and the GCF Project Document (PDD).

### 230. The same methodology will be replaced for the ex-post assessment of the achieved GHG emission reductions and removals.

#### Project 2 [this project] mitigates 4.6 million tonnes CO₂eq during its 4-year implementation period, at a cost to the GCF of EUR 158.7 million (EUR 34.0 per tonne CO₂eq). The average annual mitigation rate is 1.15 million tonnes CO₂eq during the programme implementation period of 7 years (2020-2026).

#### Project 2 applies the GCF’s ER Programme methodology, which:

* Applies the RL methodology.
* Includes the application of the RL methodology for the baseline scenario and the projection of the GCF implementation period.

#### The baseline scenarios of the baseline scenario and the projection of the GCF implementation are outlined in the GCP Methodology Note on Annex 22a.

#### Line item 1.3.4 is estimated to be achieved by regenerating vegetation with the possibility to recover to mature forest under sustainable forest management (based on expert consultation). In 2050, when all designated forest areas have been restored, the project will achieve a net carbon sequestration of approximately 6 million tonnes CO₂eq and net forest emission, contributing to La PDR’s target to reach carbon neutrality by 2030 as mentioned within LCF Project Document (PDD).

### D.1.2. Impact Potential - Adaptation

#### Even though the project has its origins in mitigation, it does have adaptation merits, and Project 2 has been designed with a strong focus on climate change adaptation throughout its activities. La PDR is LDC with limited adaptive capacity, having highly vulnerable ecosystems and communities that are exposed to climate change impacts (e.g., water scarcity, heat waves). As such, La PDR’s National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) – as well as two of five NAPAs in the country – has been identified as being highly vulnerable to climate change. The country is also known to have a high risk of natural disasters and wildfires, as evidenced by its estimated 10% annual rate of climate change impacts identified for the regions to be targeted with this project (see also the CIRA in Annex 2a).

#### Adaptation measures are proposed through the implementation of cross-cutting measures in the forestry and agricultural sectors. The proposed interventions that will promote adaptation include: strengthening forest management practices; promoting agroforestry systems; linking local level planning to the watershed level; investments to mitigate infrastructure, reforestation in catchment areas, and water harvesting; and investments for climate change awareness raising and education. These measures are designed to strengthen climate resilience and low-emission agricultural value chains, which will provide incentives that help mobilize private sector investment in adaptation activities. The project also includes a component that will support the mobilization and improved channeling of public and private funds for climate finance, including a strong emphasis on supporting investments that have cross-cutting climate change impacts (not only mitigation).

### Background

228 As described in annexes B and D-1 and D-4, the project will work in some of the most vulnerable areas of Lao PDR, focusing on a sub-set of the population who are particularly at risk - smallholder farmers in predominantly upland areas.

#### Project 2

229 Project 2 will directly support 135,700 persons (76,850 men and 76,850 women – equivalent to 2.15% of the national population) through improved livelihoods and food security in upland areas, including smallholder agriculture, as well as various measures for forest landscape restoration (implementing FLR in village forests and NPA’s). The project will support the development of a climate-resilient agricultural sector by improving participatory land use planning, considering the link between local land use planning and the landscape scale. It will also support the implementation of sustainable land management practices that are adapted to climate resilient agriculture, based on integrated planning processes. It will further strengthen awareness of climate change impacts in upland areas through a series of awareness raising activities. The project will provide direct benefit from the Programme (135,800 women and 136,850 men), equivalent to 3.9% of the national population). This is expected to result in an additional 247,000 people (123,500 men and 123,500 women – equivalent to 0.7% of the national population) through investments in sustainable land practices that will maintain or improve the productivity of their lands and increase their resilience to climate change impacts.

#### Ecosystems brought under improved, deforestation-free and climate resilient land management

231 The overarching Programme will bring 2,112,000 ha of forest ecosystems under improved sustainable and climate-resilient land management practices. This includes 574,000 ha of village forests (under Project 1) and 968,000 ha area agro-ecosystems.** This includes also sustainable land management in 5 NPA’s and 1 National Park, where the project will facilitate the restoration of forested land and the vital ecosystem services it provides. Through close collaboration with communities living within these areas, the project will support the development of a climate-resilient agricultural sector by improving participatory land use planning, considering the link between local land use planning and the landscape scale. Working closely with local communities to implement village forest conservation agreements the project will support the restoration of degraded forested lands in NPA’s and National Parks. For more information on the practices implemented in NPA’s** The total area of forests covered under village forest management practices is 1,082,450 ha.

## D.2 Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words; approximate 1/4 page)

### D.2.1 Overall context: Supporting a paradigm shift towards climate-resilient and deforestation-free land use planning and management, and low-emission development pathways

To overcome path dependencies and support a paradigm shift, the project will continue the initiated changes in current land and forest management projects of Project 1, and expand the approaches (based on lessons learned from previous activities) to include climate-resilient and deforestation-free land use planning and management, including the following (see also Figure 18 below).

- **Implement participatory land use planning:** This will be done by scaling up the pilot project of Project 1. The project will continue to scale up the pilot project of Project 1 in the three villages of Kambang, Bokor, and Sipahay, including the integration of participatory land uses plans into broader village cluster level plans, to facilitate holistic planning for climate-resilient and deforestation-free land use. This includes integrating forest management with agriculture, livestock production, and other livelihoods.

- **Scale up investments in sustainable, climate resilient and deforestation free agricultural practices:** The project will continue to scale up the pilot project of Project 1 by providing incentives for farmers who adopt agroforestry systems on their farms. Farmers will be provided with technical assistance and training on agroforestry systems, as well as access to markets for their produce.

- **Support climate-resilient forest management:** The project will continue to support the implementation of forest management practices that are resilient to climate change impacts such as increased temperatures and droughts. This includes providing farmers with information on how to adapt their farming practices to these changes.


---
**Page 78**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 74

### Catalyst for change: Investments in value chains with incentives to transition towards climate resilient, low-emission and deforestation free value chains in the AFOLU sector.

The project aims at supporting the development of a market-based approach that will enable real land use changes and more sustainable forest and food systems. The project is also supporting the development of a framework for the implementation of VMPFs and VCFAs, which are aligned with the agroforestry aim with the agriculture of the timber marketing access for sustainably produced products at village level. Agri-MDFs will be established in 10 villages in each province to provide technical assistance to local producers are key to enable a paradigm shift towards low emission and climate resilient land and forest management.

### Scale-up investments in sustainable and climate resilient forest landscape management, and establish VMPFs and VCFAs at scale

The project will support the development of VMPFs and VCFAs, which are aligned with the agroforestry aim with the agriculture of the timber marketing access for sustainably produced products at village level. Agri-MDFs will be established in 10 villages in each province to provide technical assistance to local producers are key to enable a paradigm shift towards low emission and climate resilient land and forest management.

### Strengthen law enforcement and monitoring

Project 2 will continue to bridge national strategies with regional and provincial strategies, as well as with the implementation of new regulations. Project 1) will be applied at scale within the region, improving the enforcement of new regulations. The project will also support the development of a framework for the implementation and operationalization of these systems (and related institutional strengthening in all provinces). A strengthened emphasis on law enforcement and monitoring is critical to ensure that forest resources are managed sustainably.

### Conclusion

The Climate Change Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 aims at scaling up investments in climate resilient and deforestation free sustainable land management:

- Foster the development of a market-based approach.
- Strengthen law enforcement and monitoring.
- Increase mobilization of additional climate finance and facilitate a transition towards more value-sustainable charge.

D.2.2 Potential for scaling-up and replication

237.The ER Programmes provide the basis for investments at scale to initiate the anticipated transformational change, and overcome the barriers facing Lao PDR to transition towards low-emission, deforestation-free and climate-resilient development pathways. The Programme is designed to be replicable in other countries, with a view to promote sector engagement to mobilize additional finance for climate action, and ensure the long-term sustainability of project results.

238.The Programme (Project 1 and 2) is already ambitious in its scale, impacting approx. 30% of Lao’s annual GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, covering approximately 35% of the national territory, 32% of the country's forest cover, and where around 40% of


---
**Page 80**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 76

The widespread deforestation and degradation occurs. Deforestation and forest degradation exacerbate the vulnerability of local villages and the ecosystems to climate change, and action is urgently needed in this region of Laos to address these issues. The project will be implemented over a period of five years (2018-2023). In addition, the project provides the basis for scaling up REDD+ and climate-resilient and deforestation-free sustainable land management practices across the country.

### 2.2.4 Cost-effective and scalable solutions, both for the success of the project, and also for future replication and up-scaling in other provinces: are (a) human and institutional capacities; and (b) sustainable financing. Both are essential to ensure that the project is not only a success during its implementation period but also contributes to long-term development and unlocking additional public and private financing streams for strengthening the sector.

#### D.2.3 Potential for knowledge and learning

The project will be implemented in close collaboration with comprehensive actions that will be applied (within the FG in Laos) under Annex 26 of the GCF proposal provides relevant tools to enable decision makers, provincial governments and local communities to better understand the climate change impacts on forests and land use. The project will also promote approaches in order to change the business-as-usual model and facilitate scaling up public and private investments in climate-resilient and deforestation-free sustainable land management practices. This includes strengthening communication and exchange of information to build awareness and capacity development on the revised forest regulatory framework, including REDD+ and other related policies and strategies, as well as best practices for adaptation to climate risks, and best practices for climate-resilient agriculture, sustainable land management, ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA), and integrated water resources management. The project will also support the development of tools in decision making processes, and ensure that land use is climate-informed to reduce strengthen adaptive capacity and resilience of local communities. This will be achieved through a combination of activities, including with national universities will be involved them in climate resilient and deforestation free AFOLU related activities.

To facilitate the adoption of climate-informed, sustainable agricultural and forest practices the project will use information and communication technology (ICT) to support decision making processes at the Department of Agriculture and Forestry (DAF) at the district level. This will help to ensure continued capacity building and knowledge transfer after project completion. The project will also provide tools for:

- Improving information systems and mapping.
- Generating climate-informed land use planning information (including climate risk and planning, implementing and monitoring suitable risk reduction practices).
- Applying a participatory approach in decision making on climate change adaptation, and
- Training individuals able to use remote sensing data and generate maps to enable Provincial Offices of Forestry (POF) to continuously monitor forest degradation and deforestation and to provide information for sustainable land management and forest degradation via POFs.

### D.2.4 Contribution towards creating an enabling environment

The project proposes approach, involving Projects 1 and 2 aimed at creating an enabling environment for sustainable land and forest management and REDD+, in a systemic manner, helping to unlock results-based payments through support to the development of a national framework for REDD+ and other related policies and strategies. The project will also support the development of a regulatory framework, establishment of SOPs (e.g., for law enforcement and monitoring), and REDD+ mainstreaming within socio-economic development plans. As such, there are several key outputs that will be achieved through Project 1 which will continue to raise awareness to facilitate compliance with the regulatory framework. In addition, Project 1 will contribute towards the implementation of Project 2 (which will be supported by PUF, VPM and VVM guidelines).

#### D.2 Strengthening the regulatory framework and policies

##### D.2.1 General strengthening of the regulatory framework and policies

249. A strategic component of the GCF Programme is to strengthen the policy and regulatory framework. The GCF will support the development of a comprehensive forest sector strategy, including by supporting the development of new strategies; strengthening the regulatory framework (revision, modification and drafting of relevant laws, decrees and regulations); improving the institutional capacity for forest management and sustainable land use planning and resource allocation; and strengthening law enforcement and compliance monitoring. It will support land use planning and resource allocation in line with the national strategy on climate change adaptation and forest degradation and strengthening the overall resilience of vital forest ecosystems.

##### D.2.3 Capacity building of EPR for GCF Accession

250. With support from the GCF Project 1 and other development partners, the regulatory framework has improved significantly over recent years (see Section B.4). However, there are still some gaps in the implementation of existing regulations. Subsequent policies are being revised but often sub national agencies still lack the capacities to fully apply them.

251. In addition, the programme continues to support Law PDR to meet their climate targets. It will support Law PDR and other relevant actors in developing a comprehensive forest sector strategy, including by supporting the development of both mitigation and adaptation (see B.1 for more detailed information). A strengthened focus on adaptation has thus been included in the strategy, which is currently being developed with the aim to be finalized and planned projects on climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction.

#### D.3 Sustainable development

##### D.3.1 Economic benefits

254. The project will generate diverse economic benefits, including:

- Poverty alleviation: The project area includes some of the poorest districts of the country are in the project area, which are among the most vulnerable to climate change. The poverty headcount ratio (percentage of the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 78

The project will ensure the participation of poor and vulnerable households (Component 2 and 3), and help them to strengthen their adaptive capacity through improved livelihoods, food security, nutrition, and health. This is achieved by identifying the most suitable land use practices, facilitating access to markets by strengthening of agri- value chains, and improving the resilience of local ecosystems. The project will also support the implementation of agreements with local communities in NPA's (among other measures).

### Increased productivity and income

The project will promote increased agricultural productivity through investments in the implementation of PSAP-promoted practices: will help farmers increase yields and land productivity. By strengthening the capacity of MSMEs to produce, process, market and add value to their products, it will also contribute to adding, and marketing capacities of agri-MSME's subject of climate resilient and deforestation free value chains. This will lead to increased income generation for smallholder farmers and agri-MSMEs, as well as increasing incomes of supported farmers. In the long-term, income can also be enhanced through the sustainable use of natural resources.

### Strengthened adaptive capacities and resilience of local livelihoods to climate change in order to avoid vulnerability

The project will support the development of a climate-resilient agricultural value chain that is particularly vulnerable to climate change in Lao PDR (see Chapters 8.1 and D for more information). By providing smallholder farmers with access to climate-smart technologies, inputs, services and knowledge - capacity enabling them to adapt to climate change and reduce future impacts (e.g. through diversification, improved land use practices, etc.) - the project will help communities to better manage their livelihoods in the future; whereas diversification can help strengthen farmers' coping capacities and ultimately their resilience to climate change.

### Improved mobilization and channelling of finance for climate action and sustainable land management

Improved access to financial resources through the VTAGS mechanism will enable smallholder farmers of climate finance to VTAGS will improve villagers access to funds for investments in low-emission and climate smart agriculture practices.

### Strengthened business plans and value chains project

Support under Activity 2.1.2 will further improve the agri-MSME's possibilities to access loans and private financial institutions, as well as to diversify their production towards more sustainable agricultural commodities under the "White List" supporting the development of a more sustainable agricultural value chains. This increased income generation can also play a role as a result of this enhanced economic activity.

### D3.2 Environmental and Social co-benefits

Although this GCF Programme began as a mission-focused (REDD+) initiative, Project 2 incorporates elements that will contribute to the achievement of other SDGs. These include links between agriculture to irrigation infrastructure, reforestation in catchment areas and climate resilient agriculture. These linkages will help to improve water availability for both agricultural production and domestic use, while contributing positively to long-term effects (environmental and social co-benefits) measured against the backdrop of climate change.

### Social benefits

The project is expected to continue to generate additional social benefits in the project area:

- **Improved food security and nutrition:** Malnutrition is a general threat to the rural population in the project area, which is particularly vulnerable to climate change. The prevalence of undernourishment in Laos is currently 17%. The project will support the implementation of measures to improve food security and nutrition for beneficiary households (Component 2) through improved deforestation free and climate resilient agricultural practices.

- **Health benefits:** The project will generate various health benefits. As mentioned in Section B.1 Northern Lao PDR, there is a high correlation between poverty and exposure to harmful particulate matter from fire-burning, which is exacerbated by climate change and increasingly dry conditions. The project will support the implementation of measures to improve air quality (e.g., through improved deforestation free agricultural practices, reducing fire burning, building small water reservoirs for providing water for firefighting etc.), which will have a positive health impact on the population in terms of reduced exposure to harmful particulate matter (e.g. asthma and haze). Ecosystem-based adaptation measures will further strengthen the resilience of local ecosystems against climate change; natural hazards that often result from fire, loss or die-off of trees, floods, wildfires, etc.

This is particularly relevant in northern Lao PDR, where there is a high correlation between poverty and exposure to harmful particulate matter from fire-burning. The project will support the implementation of measures to improve air quality (e.g., through improved deforestation free agricultural practices, reducing fire burning, building small water reservoirs for providing water for firefighting etc.), which will have a positive health impact on the population in terms of reduced exposure to harmful particulate matter (e.g. asthma and haze). Ecosystem-based adaptation measures will further strengthen the resilience of local ecosystems against climate change; natural hazards that often result from fire, loss or die-off of trees, floods, wildfires, etc.

Studies in many regions of Southeast Asia have found tropical deforestation has accelerated local warming and worsened local exposure to heat and related ailments.

### Indicators of Project Progress

The project aims to work with diverse ethnic groups, and provide targeted support that is culturally appropriate and aligned with the needs of these communities. The framework for this project is based on the Community Forest Management and Actions. Project 2 aims to continue to promote an inclusive approach, ensuring beneficiaries from diverse ethnic groups are included in decision-making processes.

### Environmental Benefits

252 In addition to the climate change mitigation benefits described under B3.1, the project is expected to provide a range of other environmental benefits, including (among others):

- **Enhanced biodiversity conservation:** Enhancing biodiversity conservation through the protection of natural forests and their associated ecosystems, as well as by promoting sustainable land use practices in protected areas, national parks and NPOs through the implementation of the full Programme. Project 2 will work to 367 ha of high conservation value forest (HCVF) under the PLOP 1.0 approach (see Annex 4a for more detailed information), while Project 3 will work to 5,890 ha of HCVF under the PLOP 2.0 approach (see Annex 4b). These areas in each province will also help to restore landscapes, foster healthy and resilient ecosystems, and help establish biodiversity corridors that connect protected areas with other forested areas.

- **Enhanced provision of vital ecosystem services:** Reducing deforestation and forest degradation, and promoting the sustainable use of forests and their associated ecosystems. This will contribute to improved ecosystem services, including (among others): carbon storage, water regulation, climate regulation (e.g., micro-climates), soil formation and maintenance, pollination, and pest control. Project 2 will work to 367 ha that benefit from watershed protection and more holistic land use planning under the PLOP 1.0 approach (with strengthened forest management practices). Project 3 will work to 5,890 ha of HCVF under the PLOP 2.0 approach (with strengthened forest management practices) that will help improve the resilience of ecosystems and the dynamics of land use practices and ecosystem services.

- **Reduced vulnerability to climate change:** Improving land-use planning, governance, PCMs and enforcement tools; processes and capacities will also help reduce unauthorised clearing of forested land, and ensure the sustainable management of forests and their associated ecosystems.

- **Facilitation of sustainable production practices:** Strengthening the development of sustainability-oriented livelihoods that are resilient to climate change. This includes promoting the use of climate-resilient and deforestation-free agricultural practices will also help improve soil quality, reduce erosion and sedimentation, and reduce agricultural runoff (and thus the risk of soil and water contamination), while improving access to markets.

### D3.3 Gender-Sensitive Impact

253 Women are disproportionately impacted by climate change and deforestation and forest degradation. Women, as the primary users of natural resources, know forests well and have seen the impact of deforestation and forest degradation on their livelihoods. As forests become increasingly degraded, women must either travel longer distances to collect firewood or sell their land for cash crops. This has a significant negative impact on their livelihoods, where 82% of working women are employed in the sector.** Consulted female stakeholders noted they face the risk of some forest products being sold by men and not shared with them.**

**The project will not promote nor provide synthetic fertilizers or pesticides. It will instead promote organic alternatives, integrated pest management (IPM), and the conservation of the state of agro-technical use. 29. The proposed project is based on the Gender Action Plan (Gap), Annex 8b, which builds on the assessment and action plan conducted for FP17. The Gap forms the basis for operationalizing the results and outputs from the assessment and action plan. The Gap will be used to guide the design of the project implementation, where gender equality is considered a cross-cutting element throughout the project's output areas. The Gap will also provide guidance on how to ensure that women are meaningfully engaged in all aspects of the project, including through their active participation in village committees. The project will include training on business skills development for women that will help to address barriers to women’s access to markets and financial services. It will also aim at increasing awareness among men and boys about gender equality and empowering women to participate fully in agriculture, forest management and climate resilient agricultural activities. It will also strengthen awareness of the importance of women's participation in decision-making processes for sustainable land use and strengthening the resilience of local communities and ecosystems. All extension staff and leaders supporting Project 2 (see Section D.3) are required to be trained on gender equality and women’s activities (see also Section G.2 below, and the gender assessment and gender action plan in annexes 6a and 6b).

## D.4 Elements of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### D.4.1 Economic and social development level of the country and affected population

Laos is a landlocked developing country (LLDC) bordered by Myanmar to the northwest, Vietnam to the east, Cambodia to the southeast, and Thailand to the southwest. The country has a HDI of 0.673, ranking it as a medium human development country. Its economy is heavily dependent on agriculture, which has fasted in Asia, averaging more than 7.7% per year for most of the last decade. According to the World Bank country ranks among the top five countries with the largest share of land under forest cover (35%), followed by forests (mainly energy), and a resilient services sector, led by wholesale and retail trade growth.

Despite its rich biodiversity, Laos is one of the world’s most vulnerable countries in terms of climate change and environmental degradation. Environmental degradation is taking an economic toll on the country due to the depletion of natural resources and loss of ecosystem services. The majority of the population (70%) lives in rural areas, 70% of the Laop population still depend on forests and waterways for income and nutrition. Laos PDR has recognized the limitations of the economy model and is implementing reforms to give a greener and more inclusive model.

### D.4.2 Impact of COVID-19 on La PDR

Since the development of Project 1, La PDR’s economy has been greatly affected by the COVID-19 pandemic. The economic impact of the pandemic was felt across all sectors in Laos, with a decline in GDP growth to -3%. In addition, the pandemic has generated substantial other impacts including loss of life and long-term health impacts for those infected, as well as significant disruptions to food security and agriculture production, among others. A rapid assessment of Food Security and Agriculture conducted by World Food Programme

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 82

### D5. Country ownership (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

#### D.5.1 Inclusion within GCF country programme

The GIZ Project Scaling up the Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved forest management and landscape planning (hereinafter referred to as Project 2) is part of the Lao PDR GCF Country Programme 2020-2023 under Priority Area "Supporting an increase and maintenance of national forest cover in the context of climate change adaptation". The project work programme highlights the alignment of the project with the country's long-term adaptation objectives, where it aims to contribute to the achievement of the Lao PDR NDC targets. The project is aligned with the GCF Strategy 2018-2023 and highlights the nomination of EPP for the Lao PDR NDA to become a national RE, and additional support provided by GIZ. The project also contributes to the implementation of the National Forest Policy (NFP) and supports its advance with their accreditation process, and eventually develop and implement their own projects to strengthen forest governance.

The project is also included in the GIZ AE work programme.


#### D.5.2 Coherence with existing policies and commitments

As part of the Lao PDR NDC, the project aligns with the FIS (in Annex 2a), the project is closely aligned with the Government of Lao PDR's socio-economic development plans and strategy, as well as provides for climate change adaptation. The project also contributes to the achievement of the Lao PDR NDC targets. With the approval of AP11/1 by GCF, design of Project 2 has been adapted to reflect the (draft) Climate Change Strategy 2030, which is in line with the Government's commitment under the Paris Agreement and the Lao PDR's engagement on the AFOLU sector, and Forestry Strategy to 2035, among others.

The project also contributes towards achieving the GCF NDC target in the forestry sector. The engagement with the ACPF Carbon Fund and the ER Programme is seen as key for the transformation of the forest and land sectors in Lao PDR.

**Page 87**
---

### D.5 Capacity of AE and EEs to deliver

#### D.5.3 Capacity of AE and EEs to deliver

270. The Project has been implemented under Project FP117, where GIZ with its headquarters in Germany serves as AE, and GIZ with its management structure in Laos and EE as EE. Thus, all AEs and EE have demonstrated experience implementing projects in the country and are well equipped to implement this project.

271. GIZ is one of the largest international providers of capacity development and technical assistance on climate change adaptation and mitigation in Lao PDR. It has been involved in the implementation of various projects under proposals implemented under FP117 (FP117, FP153; FP109).

272. EEP 117 was developed with substantial engagement from GIZ. The AE is responsible for channeling EUR 11 million to project beneficiaries at provincial and district level (e.g., PAFs, DAFOs, private sector and final beneficiaries (a & b) under the Lao PDR’s National REDD+ Strategy). This includes providing technical assistance and facilitating investments in conservation at the local level. They also implemented the following projects (among others):

- Sustainable Land Management Project (SLM) (GIZ/DE0904179, EUR 2 million)
- Safe Ecosystem Project (GIZ/D0096179, EUR 1.5 million)
- Forest Landscape Restoration Project (FLR) (GIZ/D0083179, EUR 1.5 million)
- Improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management project (Project 1 GCF, GIZ; EUR 11.1 million)
- Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) Project (GCF/DE0924179)

273. The EEP also received technical support from a GIZ-implemented GCF Readiness Project to become a GCF Readiness Project, which was approved by the GCF Board in October 2018 and is now in its second phase of implementation (GCF/DE0954179). The project has been certified under the Green Climate Fund’s Quality Assurance Accreditation System (QAAS) as of the EEP in October 2019, and got in January 2021 the second phase of funding from the GCF. The AE is responsible for implementing Project 1 without Delivery Partner.

#### D.5.4 Role of the National Designated Authority (NDA)

##### As mentioned, this project is embedded in the Programme (FP 117), and has thus been informed by substantial engagement with the NDA, government ministries and other key stakeholders. Regular communication and updates have taken place through meetings between GIZ and the NDA, as well as through the Project Management process. Representatives of the NDA have attended cross-sector multi-stakeholder workshops to provide technical advice on the project’s implementation.

##### The NDA has been informed about the development of the EEP in January 2019 (FP117), confirming its project conforms to the country's national priorities, strategies and plans, and that it is in accordance with relevant laws and regulations. The NDA has also been informed on the progress of Project 1 “Implementation of the Lao PDR Emissions Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management” (Project 1 GCF) at the end of the first phase of implementation, i.e., Annual Performance Report (APR) in March 2021 and the 2nd APR in March 2022.

##### The NDA has been informed about the development of both Projects 1 and 2. Individual meetings between GIZ and the NDA regarding the development of Project 2 have been organized on January 20, April 28, May 30, June 7, July 5, August 9, September 6, October 4, November 2, December 14, February 15, March 15 and May 10 in order to discuss possible implications of the geographical and technical overlap of the GIZ QCF Project with Lao PDR’s National REDD+ Strategy. The NDA has also been informed on the progress of Project 2 at the EEP Update Meeting which was conducted by the NDA. On both occasions, GIZ had been invited to present the progress on Project 2.

##### Thus, the NDA has been involved throughout the development of both Projects 1 and 2. A letter of no objection has also been issued for both projects, confirming that they conform to the country’s national priorities, strategies, plans, and that it is in accordance with relevant laws and regulations.
