## D.5 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### D.5.1 Efficiency and effectiveness

281 The project is estimated to contribute to reducing 4.6 million tCO2e relative to the FREL over its 4-year project implementation period. This represents a reduction of 37% in emissions compared with the baseline, or 12.25 EUR per tCO2eq and the cost to the GCF is €9.8 EUR per tCO2eq. For the Programme (Projects 1 and 2), and the mitigation potential is estimated at 11.7 million tCO2e relative to the FREL over its 4-year project implementation period, or a reduction of 35% in emissions compared with the baseline, or 10.8 EUR per tCO2eq and the cost to the GCF is estimated at EUR 32.1 per tCO2eq.

### D.5.2 Financial viability

282 The activities under Component 1 of Project 2 fall as a public good nature and will not generate financial returns for the government. However, the project will contribute to reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation in local forests and ecological conservation areas. These activities under Component 1 are scaled for receiving foreign funding. Grant funding will scale the cost of these activities by 50% (i.e., EUR 24 per tCO2eq). The government practices (PFP) and technologies and Component 3 aim to scale SFPA and FLR in village forests and NPFAs.

### D.5.3 Activities

283 Activities under Components 2 and 3 will generate enhanced financial returns for target beneficiaries relative to the baseline, or EUR 10 per tCO2eq. The project will contribute to reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation by shifting on-site cultivation in upland areas and face knowledge capacity, and market access constraints, positive impacts on the environment and social benefits. These activities under Components 2 and 3 are scaled for co-financing that the project will mobilize both as loans and grants. The ability of the GCF to take on additional loans or grants is subject to the availability of resources in the GCF Fund. Given the high level of external debt, the government borrowed EUR 34.1 million from ADB and EUR 11.35 million from IFAD to complement GCF Project 2. The project will also generate financial returns for the government through the sale of carbon credits (see Chapter B.5 for a more detailed description of the approach taken to ensure minimum levels of conservation).

### D.5.4 Economic rate of return

284 The economic rate of return to the GCF finance is of close to 37%. The economic rate of return is estimated based on the financial flows from Components 2 and 3 as well as from the value of abated emissions due to project implementation, which will be generated during the project's implementation period. This estimate should not be considered conservative as it does not integrate any other social or environmental benefits that will be generated during its implementation.

**Page 89**

D.6.3 Application of best practices

The agriculture and forestry sectors are identified as key for climate change mitigation and agriculture as a key sector for climate change adaptation in the country's UNFCCC technology needs assessments for adaptation and mitigation. The agricultural systems currently in use in the project area tend to have low productivity and for the most part cannot be considered as climate resilient or good agricultural practices (GAPs) based on current management practices. The concepts of good agricultural practices provide additional principles to ensure that agriculture is sustainable, productive, and resilient.

Deforestation and deforestation free agriculture have been integrated in the PSAM approach developed under Project 1 and further supported by the project’s climate change adaptation strategy. A review of the PSAM approach for Project 1 found that the current approach is effective and well-tailored to the local context in the project area. Supporting this, a recent study (see Annex 2a) has identified several best practices that can be scaled up under Project 2.

The TNA outlines a range of recommended interventions, such as watershed protection and reforestation, irrigation, climate-resilient crop varieties, and improved livestock management. These interventions are expected to be further scaled up under Project 2. These interventions reflect best practices such as participatory land use planning (PLUP) and integrated land use planning and environmental management.

An Agriculture Action Plan for Project 2 will build on the improvements to the regulatory framework, SOPs and guidelines developed within Project 1. These guidelines have been developed considering best practices and successful experiences from other countries. The guidelines are based on the findings of the TNA and the Feasibility Study in Annex 2a), and will facilitate replication and upscaling. Additional support will be provided in Project 2 to strengthen climate adaptation and cross-cutting synergies, supporting the NDC’s intention to promote integrated land use planning and environmental management.

A Climate Change Adaptation Strategy has been developed for the elaboration of Project 2, and are reflected in the design, as highlighted throughout Chapter B.3 (and further described in the Feasibility Study in Annex 2a).

## Replicability

At the time of this Funding Proposal (2021-2025), Project FP117 will have gone through the transformational change process, including supporting policy mainstreaming and strengthening the Medium

If capacities can be built and additional finance mobilized, public policies can be mobilized. Climate resilient land use management can be replicated and scaled up in other provinces and districts, and provinces in Laos PDR, with very insight on lessons learned and best practices.

This project aims at scaling up successful approaches, and linking them to private sector investments for the long-term sustainability of climate action, and ensure the long-term benefits of climate resilient land use management. At the time of this Funding Proposal Formulation (2013/2014), the Government of Lao PDR has shown commitment and ownership to REDD+ and is implementing a law in Lao PDR, e.g., through the National REDD Strategy and Action Plan up to 2020; among others. Lao PDR’s REDD strategy emphasizes the need for synergies between adaptation and REDD+, as well as technical and financial support – otherwise efforts are limited.

Sustainability

Lao
The ER-Programme, in which both Projects 1 and 2 contribute to, provides the basis for achieving the long-term goal of transformational change and overcoming the challenges of low-emission, deforestation free and climate resilient land use. The programme aims at scaling up successful approaches, and mobilizing additional finance for climate action.

Essential enabling pre-conditions, both for the sustainability and success of the project, as well as for the implementation in other provinces, are: (a) human and institutional capacity building; (b) public financing. Both are cornerstones of the programme’s approach to scaling up, including capacity building, institutional strengthening and mobilizing additional finance for climate action.

In terms of financial support, Lao PDR has been investing in low-emission and efficient land use is the limited availability of public and private

Lao
Project interventions are expected to increase the overall level of investment in low emission funds for climate action. The project includes: (a) capacity building; (b) institutional climate finance, including mechanisms raised through the LPRF Agreement as well as from voluntary REDD+ financing benefits, based on market and non-market approaches; (c) public financing, including support to EFU being accessed to the LPRF and national climate finance (Article 1.1.1).

Project activities also seek to engage the private sector in the development of results. Specifically, through the project’s approach to agroforestry and agri-MSBs, a key actor at the interface of REDD+ and agriculture, it aims at developing the sustainable value chains for climate action, including low-emission and agro-biodiversity

The long-term sustainability of measures will be ensured by their continued replication and scaling.

### E

**Finance (see also Sections B and C)**

- **Lao PDR faces severe liquidity stress and has a high risk of not being able to take on further loans for land sector action**
- **Producers and agri-MMEs face limited access to financing, particularly in rural areas, and often do not have the necessary conditions (e.g. collateral)**
- **While private sector finance for climate action is considered attractive, it has been lacking**, there is a gap between private sector and public sector land management action. This tends to favor the state perspective in land use management over that of the private sector and product market conditions, which limits investments in crop-based land management.

**and mechanisms to channel climate finance would be strengthened e.g. through the Emission Reductions Fund (ERF) or FCPF)**

Beyond this, various issues have arisen regarding adaptive capacities and increased incomes through adopting climate-resilient agricultural practices and livelihood opportunities. This could be through the development of agri-MMEs via agri-MMEs), through VFM benefiting from the adoption of climate-smart sustainable forest products and NTFPs and through the promotion of climate resilient agricultural practices that increase yields and diversify income.

**facilitated by the institutionalization of trainings, workshops, and meetings, the development of SOPs and clear guidance, guidelines and standards for agri-MMEs. These will be strengthened throughout project implementation and vulnerability, climate change adaptation, livelihood opportunities, and emission SLR.**

The operationalization of POMs in all 4 provinces with technical and financial support, will enable FOM to conduct near real-time monitoring and evaluation and provide increased transparency and accountability through the project delineation. Improved monitoring, combined with a focus on developing guidelines, standards and SOPs for agri-MMEs, will contribute to the overall sustainability and success of the POMs. This will also allow FOM to unlock further results-based payments in the future.

The project interventions will facilitate greater (and more sustainability-oriented) phase integration between forestry and agriculture sectors. The matching grant mechanism will provide additional private sector investments from MMEs – which play a key role in the development of FOM, with a focus on developing guidelines, standards and SOPs for agri-MMEs. This will also contribute to the overall sustainability and success of the PFSAT promoted practices.

In terms of mobilizing additional sources of finance, the project interventions will support the Lao PDR in developing the necessary enabling actions necessary to participate in the market.

## Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

This Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (in addition to the ESMP and GAP) will comply with the relevant GCF policies, as stipulated in the LMA, FAA and project-related Financing Agreements and Implementation Agreements with Executing Entities and Implementation Partners, which the EEs will extend to sub-activities.

The project will apply a customized results-based monitoring and evaluation (M&E) system. The M&E system will track project inputs, activities and sub-activities. Outputs and impacts, as well as associated financial flows across all outputs in all project provinces and districts.

### 291. This system will be based on:

* GCF Monitoring, Evaluation Procedures (GCF’s “evaluation policy – principles, guidelines and requirements”)
* M&E System from Project 1 (ensuring it is optimized based on the lessons learned during implementation)**
* Project 2 monitoring and evaluation plan (Annex 11)
* Phased approach to project design, including logical framework
* Project 2 implementation timetable (Annex 5)
* Requirements of GCF’s Annual Performance Report
* Phased approach to project design, including logical framework and stakeholders in Lao PDR

### 292. The oversight for M&E and reporting lies with the GCF AE. The NPMI in Lao PDR will implement the M&E system and work closely with the FPMUs, DPMUs, GIZ EE in Vientiane, as well as government and development partners.

## Recruitment of M&E staff

Immediately when Project 2 commences, GCF’s GCF AE will make available one expert to oversee and coordinate the project M&E and reporting routines. As soon as possible thereafter, the NPMI will recruit a dedicated M&E expert to work with the GCF AE in overseeing the project M&E system. The M&E expert will be responsible for the management of the M&E system and provide on-the job training for dedicated M&E experts from the NPMI, the FPMUs and other stakeholders of the system (e.g., agriculture or forestry).

## Independent monitoring and evaluation studies

293 GCF AE will initiate a mid-term evaluation and a final evaluation of Project 2.

### 294. The mid-term and final evaluation of project 2 is steered by a corporate unit that is separate from operational business and reports directly to the Management Board. Moreover, evaluations are conducted with the support of external evaluators.


**Overall, the set-up of the Monitoring System of Project 1 has been functional, including its technical aspects (using Cloud Data), staffing requirements (see chapter below) and the structure of the system (Action Inputs, Steps, Activity Requests, Units). Also, the integration of the Monitoring System with the sub-projects and general monitoring system has shown to be successful.**


---
**Page 117**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 113

295. GIZ applies the "Evaluation criteria for German bilateral development cooperation" (2006; revised 2020) on a standard basis in project evaluations. These are based on the following evaluation criteria agreed by the Development Assistance Committee (DAC) of the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD): relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, overarching development results (impact), coherence and sustainability.

296. Additional evaluation criteria include: gender equality, poverty reduction, climate change adaptation, country ownership, innovativeness in results area, replication and scalability as well as unexpected results.

297. Reporting on the above criteria is required to ensure that projects are based on sound theory of change and relevance, use and participation. All provisions for upholding the GCF evaluation principles described in Chapter V (a-d) of the Evaluation Policy are followed (see DAC standards and DeGcEi evaluation standards). In line with the UNEP guidelines, the project will be evaluated using a combination of quantitative and qualitative methods.

298. Regarding GCF's Evaluation Principle: Credibility and Robustness: As a matter of principle, GIZ takes care to use an appropriate combination of quantitative and qualitative methods in order to ensure robust evaluation results that are credible and can be used for decision-making purposes.

To ensure robust evidence, GIZ uses a theory-based approach as a minimum standard for its central project evaluations. The contribution analysis has proven to be the most suitable method for this purpose. This is because it allows for an assessment of what works well in terms of results and impact, what does not work and why, and how these can be associated with a project. Furthermore, the analyses should increase the understanding and knowledge of what works, what does not work and why.

299. Presenting the evaluation results in a clear and understandable way is essential for decision-making purposes. The GIZ Evaluation Report will be presented on a scale from 0 to 100. Using a points system serves to increase the transparency of ratings, whilst enabling better comparability between individual projects.

300. Results of both an Interim Evaluation Report and a Final Independent Evaluation Report will be submitted to the GCF at a per-instance agreed in the funding activity agreement (FAA).

301. Results from the mid-term evaluation should be reflected within the project design, to influence upscaling, and improve overall project implementation (adaptive management).

### Data collection and frequency

302. The NPMU will coordinate data collection for implemented activities through responsible executing department / divisions at the district and provincial levels; the GIZ and partner will guide and monitor the evaluation and evaluations. Each PPMU will aggregate monitoring reports based on GPSM inputs at least every six months. The NPMU will aggregate all the PPMU reports and make a summary report available to GIZ’s AE unit at head office, again at least every six months.

### Measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) of reduced GHG emissions

303. The MRV system is designed to provide information on project efficiency and effectiveness as well as for continued learning across the programme. In cooperation with JICA, PPIF 2117 included a dedicated technical assistance package that was specifically devoted to the measurement and reporting of additional GHG emission reductions. A similar approach in place will continue to support GIZ MRV during Phase II Project. The MRV will be subject to verification by the FCFF Carbon Fund (as a basis for results-based REDD+ payments). Methodologically, the same approach will be used as for the Reference Level development (See Section 0.1 and Annex Zzz).

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1. Mitigation measures (max. 5 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1 - Selected Risk Factor 1 – Loss of capacities and knowledge due to turnover among project mission staff

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

**Description**

High staff turnover and limited numbers of government mission staff impedes retention of skills and knowledge in the relevant sectors and institutions.

* Lessons learned during project 1 showed that training, communication, and dissemination are important to facilitate knowledge retention, capacity building and knowledge exchange. This includes the development and implementation of a mentoring program for new staff members (e.g., through the use of mentors, role models, but not limited to: PUPIL 2.0, PSAT, VFM and VFTM guidelines), the institutionalization of training workshops (e.g. 8 development workshops on forest management planning in June 2021) and the establishment of a knowledge centre and improving knowledge management systems.
* Continuous professional development at all levels at the national, provincial and district PMUs will further help strengthen knowledge exchange and maintain organizational knowledge. National, provincial and district PMUs are advised to establish a mentoring program for new staff members (e.g., through the use of mentors, role models).
* Experience from project 1 has also shown that training of multiple staff members in each department/ provinceal district or team will mitigate the risk of knowledge and capacity drain. Such an approach will be continued within Project 2.
* A knowledge management plan has been developed for Project 2, which is available in Chapter 7 of the Feasibility Study.

### Selected Risk Factor 2 - Weak forest law enforcement and corruption

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

While efforts to reduce corruption in the forest sector have improved law enforcement and reduced corruption, there is still a risk that corruption could persist.

* Project 1 developed SOPs, improved investigative procedures, whistle-blower systems and improved anti-corruption measures. These actions will continue to be implemented and further strengthened. Continued support under Activity 2.1.2 will further strengthen law enforcement, increase transparency and reduce corruption in the forest sector. The project has also established a performance management system (PMS) for the implementation and operationalization of PMSs; PMS includes a transparent reporting system that is effective, accessible and culturally sensitive. Project 1 highlighted the relevance of this tool, and the need for continued support for scaling up PMS within the project and beyond.
* Project 1 further strengthened the regulatory framework, supporting revisions in the Decree on Protected Areas and Forests (DPAF) and the Decree on Forest Management Planning (DMP). The DPAF was revised to include forest management planning (June 2021), among others which have created a stronger basis for reducing corruption.

## Selected Risk Factor 3 - Insufficient demand from agri-MiSMES for matching grants and limited participation of private sector actors in the implementation of project activities

### Technical and operational
- **Description**: Investments in climate resilient and deforestation free value chains may not materialize. This could be due to various factors: (a) if some actors do not see the benefits of agri-MiSMES activities and prefer to continue using suboptimal practices or face competing priorities, private sector actors may not meet the project's eligibility criteria, or business plans do not meet a sufficient quality.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- **Description**: Private sector assessments undertaken for Project 1 and interviews undertaken for the feasibility study of Project 2 are being used to identify potential agri-MiSMES partners. The project will provide technical assistance and receive technical support for strengthening operational capacities and diversifying their business models.
- **Project 3**: The project will engage with private sector actors, including agri-MiSMES and private sector engagement, to support all activities under Component 2; given the project's strengthened emphasis on Component 2 and private sector investments through agri-MiSMES.

## Selected Risk Factor 4 - Promoted investments implemented by villagers and agri-MiSMES could become financially attractive and create perverse incentives that could accelerate deforestation and forest degradation

### Category
- **Description**: Promoted investments implemented by villagers and agri-MiSMES could become more financially attractive and create perverse incentives that could accelerate deforestation and forest degradation.

### Technical and operational
- **Medium**

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- **Awareness raising is cross-cutting throughout project activities on climate change and the importance of forests for both climate change mitigation and adaptation, and other socio-economic and environmental benefits.**

## Investment in PLUP, law enforcement and monitoring will facilitate improved transparency and monitoring and law enforcement, improving the detection and enforcement of infractions. Villages will be incentivized to:

### At the village level, the PLUP developed under activity 12.2 informs the PSFP process which with its White List (developed in activity 13) will guide the selection of projects for investment under the REDD+ project area and Village Forest Management Plans. In addition to law enforcement, compliance with the plans will be promoted through community engagement and awareness raising activities as part of the project’s monitoring cycle.

### Selected Risk Factor 5 – Limited institutional and cross-sectoral coordination limits the effectiveness of project activities:

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technology and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description

Sub-optimal cross-sectional planning and potential conflicting interests may limit the adoption and effectiveness of REDD+ and related measures.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

### Chapter 8.13 outlines the project’s alignment with the national policy framework, where it is evident the project is well aligned with the priorities set out in the National Climate Change Policy. The project has been developed together with the Government of Laos PDR through extensive consultations and meetings with government actors and other stakeholders. The project also aligns with the REDD+ Strategy and Action Plan (2015-2030) which sets out how to address the barriers identified in B.2 as well as other challenges related to coordination, is further highlighted under Chapter D.4 where it is outlined that the project will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach.

### Improved cross-sectoral planning and dialogue between key actors (including MPR, MAF, MONRE, PONRE, PAFO, etc.) will ensure alignment with national policies and strategies.

### The GCF Project 2 continues to build on the strengthened relationships and cross-sectoral planning established through the REDD+ Task Force and promotes continuous dialogue and ongoing capacity building and coordination. It continues to support the development of a multi-stakeholder approach for project implementation, as outlined in Project 1: the National and Provincial REDD+ Task Force will support project steering, and ultimately facilitate communication between key actors.

### REDD+: Results based payments from the CFP Carbon Fund provide an additional incentive for the Government to implement its REDD+ Strategy and Action Plan (2015-2030).

### Improved land use planning under PLUP (Activity 12.1) will continue to improve monitoring and enforcement of land use based on plans. PSFP and other agricultural support under Component 2 and improved planning and monitoring of forest resources under Component 4 will ensure sustainable management, productivity and enforcement, ultimately discouraging encroachment, over-harvesting and unsustainable forest use.

## Selected Risk Factor 6 - Non-compliance of users with land use plans:

### Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | High |

### Description

Participating households may not comply with the land use plans that are developed and approved and undertake illegal harvesting activities to generate income. These groups will be most affected, as they are the most likely to experience economic dislocation due to their reliance on the land.

## Selected Risk Factor #7 - Insufficient capacities within the Forest Protection Fund slow project activities

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

### Description
The capacity development process of FPF takes too long and FFP is unable to channel RGFs and project funding during the implementation of the project.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
- **Dedicated support to FPP:** As considered in activity 1.1, including the provision of support for the FPP to enhance its capacity to manage climate change adaptation projects in a forest sector context. This will ensure that it is able to screen, assess, and monitor climate mitigation and adaptation projects in the forest sector.
- **A clear financial framework:** The FPF will be funded by the World Bank’s own resources (WBR) and payments as co-finance for FPP for Activities 2.1.4 and 3.1.2. As stated in the draft Benefit Sharing Plan document, the FPF will be managed under a separate trust fund with its own budget line item.
- **Results-based reporting:** The FPF will be subject to results-based payments under the ERPA, while committing to improve the FPP’s capacity to meet World Bank’s funding requirements for project implementation and reporting. This One FFP is deemed ready-to-take over fund management by Ofo; the WB will conduct a complete and comprehensive review of the FPF.

## Selected Risk Factor #8 - Risk of money laundering, terrorist financing, prohibited practices and sanctions

### Description
Risk of project funds being used for money laundering, terrorist financing, prohibited practices and sanction risks.
- None of the project activities will be undertaken in any jurisdiction which is subject to or affected by United Nations Security Council (UNSC) resolutions.
- No individual or entity that is listed on any UNSC sanctions list will be involved in any manner with the project or its implementation.
- The project will not provide direct cash payments from FPF to villagers. ERFA transfers funds amounting to an average of 10,000 EUR to each validated FHP account; plus the equivalent of 1,500 EUR for the first year of

## VFM and White List Activities

Only the elected VFAG committee is entitled to withdraw funds from the VFAG account (multiple signatures) and only based on the approved "PVAS investment plan" and the "annual village forest management activity plan". The annual village forest management activity plan will be prepared by the EED. For the White List activity, the grant amount to receive from the VFGF up to 400 EUR, their own contributions, and the tools and materials purchased directly by the beneficiary family (e.g., tools and small materials) against receipt, while seeds or planting material are purchased directly by the beneficiary family against receipt. In case of any other items that are purchased directly, cash payments will be made by the VFAG committee to the individual family. This could be for any of the White List activities, including those not covered in the PVAS investment plan and/or annual village forest management activity plan.

These measures (VFAG committees) and ensuring alignment with the White List and approved VFM annual plan and FFG annual plan are described in greater detail in the Project Operations Manual and related guidance (see Annex 21).

In addition to controls applied for VFAGs (described above), the project will follow GIZ's approved compliance management system (CMS) as well as other relevant policies. As part of its accreditation process, Procurement will be carried out by the EED and procurement activities will be agreed upon between the EED and the GCF. The EED will ensure that all procurement activities are transparently documented, as per Plan in Annex 15a.

The GCF has developed a code of conduct for project partners. Since GCF became accredited to the GCF, a developed a code of business conduct where principles to be considered related to money laundering and anti-terrorist financing have been updated. The GCF has also developed a code of ethics, annual compliance reporting on an anti-corruption policy, and a whistleblowing procedure. GIZ's internal policies include a code of ethics, annual compliance reporting on an anti-corruption policy, and a whistleblowing procedure. GIZ's internal principles of conduct, internal rules as well as applicable laws (e.g., related to corruption and bribery, embezzlement, fraud, misappropriation, conflicts of interest, sexual misconduct and sexual exploitation, and violation of human rights).

G.1 ENVIRONMENTAL AND SOCIAL RISK ASSESSMENT (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The ESIA of the project is in Annex B. The ESIA takes the risk of the project as category B (medium risk). There are potential adverse impacts due to the project's activities, but they are not unprecedented in the project area and can be managed through mitigation measures. The project will also take into account that there are good measures available to avoid, mitigate and/or manage these risks and potential adverse impacts.

30% of the project area is located within the Laotian Protected Areas (LPA) network. These areas have a high level of biodiversity and are subject to various pressures such as human settlement, land use change, forest degradation, and climate change impacts. The LPA network also includes protected areas with different levels of protection status.

The project will take into account that the project area is located within the LPA network and will ensure that the project's activities do not have adverse impacts on these areas. The project will also consider the potential for conflicts as well as overarching policy and regulatory framework, as outlined in Chapters D1-D5. That said, the project will also take into account that there are no known existing or planned development projects within the project area.

The project area is located within the Lao-Tai ethnic group's traditional territory. The Laotian government has recognized this fact by designating the project area as a priority for the conservation and interacting dynamics of landscapes, ethnic groups and policy implementation (among others) have created a diverse range of social and environmental risks that may arise during project implementation. These risks include but are not limited to: land use change; forest degradation; climate change impacts; biodiversity loss; and conflicts with other stakeholders.

The project will take into account the potential for these risks as well as overarching policy and regulatory framework, as outlined in Annex B (2). The high percentage of non-Lao-Tai ethnic groups in the project area also requires a higher degree of consultation and engagement to ensure that their rights are respected. The project will also consider how their livelihoods are adequately safeguarded.

30% of the project area is located within the LPA network. These areas are often underfunded, under-budgeted and under-equipped. District Agriculture and Forest Offices (DAFO) have limited capacity in terms of human resources, budgeting, and equipment to effectively manage climate change impacts, gender equality and social inclusion. Project 1 put effort into capacity development of implementing agencies and local communities. The project will continue this effort during the implementation phase.

308 free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) is at the core of the project's approach. All activities under Project 2 are consistent with FPIC principles. The project will ensure that all relevant stakeholders have been consulted and their views taken into account in decision-making processes. The project will also continue and be updated under Project 2 (Figure 12 in Chapter D3) depicts the project's overall implementation strategy, including the FPIC process.

The ESIA identifies potential adverse impacts on individuals and members of ethnic groups that are affected or potentially affected by the project. # G.1.3 Project Risk Mitigation Approach

**3.1 Of the nine RFC Performance Standards (PS) the project triggers all eight**, and its management system will require the development of mitigation hierarchies to manage and mitigate risks (as per Performance Standard 1 and Table 2.4). For more detailed information refer to the ESA and ESMIP in Annexes A & B.

## Table 13: Overview of environmental and social (ES) risks and mitigation measures

| **Mitigation Measure** | **Description** |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 121

## PS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

There is potential that some biodiversity hotspots (e.g., the areas which have been pre-screened for their sustainability and subject in the project) will be impacted by climate change, land use changes, and/or interwoven with soybean farming. Detailed extension and supporting activities are required to ensure that these areas are not negatively impacted on biodiversity.

## PS 7: Indigenous peoples

The project area contains many people of the non-Lao-Tai ethnic group in most villages (see Annex A).

## PS 8: Cultural heritage

During project design and consultation, no cultural monuments were identified in the project area. Nonetheless, there is a residual uncertainty relating to forest monitoring.

### G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

312 The project’s Gender Assessment (GA) is provided in Annex A6 and the Gender Action Plan (GAP) is provided in Annex B8. The GA was the one conducted for FP171, but has been revised to ensure the GAP is “for purpose” reflecting the adjusted design and expanded area under Project 2. The revisions of the GA and the GAP have drawn on lessons learned from previous projects, including the use of gender equality frameworks, discussions with the Project Team on lessons learned and implementation experiences, and have also considered the findings of the Gender Equality Assessment (GEA) conducted by the World Bank for Construction, government staff at the national, provincial and district level, and local men and women at the village level in the project area (for more information refer to the GA in Annex A6 and the Stakeholder Engagement Plan in Annex 7).

313 Although women are often responsible for household food production and care-giving tasks at the same time they still face discrimination in many aspects of their lives despite government policy to promote gender equality and protect women’s and children’s rights. This discrimination, in turn, results in ongoing barriers for them.


---
**Page 126**
---

# Overview of gender gaps and recommendations integrated into the design of Project 2

## Gender Gap
The current capacity of government staff at all levels to actively integrate gender and ethnic considerations in forest management and law enforcement implementation agencies are very limited, and there is a lack of information on women's roles. Women have traditionally been excluded from decision-making related to land use planning and forest resource allocation, and agricultural activities are seen as men’s domain.

## Recommendation
The project will train all relevant government staff, including La Women's Union (LWU) on gender climate resilient and deforestation free forest management practices. The project will also provide targeted training in support the capacity development of female staff for forest law enforcement and environmental protection. This will include interactive tools and gender-sensitive communication materials and methods to ensure that women are included in decision-making processes, including in land use planning and forest resource allocation. The project will also promote gender equality in programme implementation.

The project will provide training on gender-responsive approaches to forest management and law enforcement efforts on climate change impacts and mainstreaming adaptation across project activities. As part of the capacity building component, the project will ensure that all training activities are gender responsive and will pay attention to the different needs of women and men.

The programme will continue to support inclusion of women in law enforcement and environmental protection activities at all levels.
Early experiences with gender-sensitive "good practices" (e.g. female-led patrols) have been positive, particularly in provinces where women's groups are active.

All project staff will be trained on gender equality considerations. All extension agents and trainers will be provided with training to ensure that they can provide appropriate programming guidance for the implementation of VPAF, VFMG and VFM plans, special attention should be given to the needs of women in these activities. The project will also provide specific challenges faced by men and women (e.g. time poverty) into the mainstreaming of VPAF, VFMG investment plans, MPA, VFMCA and VFM plans.

Formative household surveys will be conducted on a representative sample of participants.
Gender-sensitive monitoring will be conducted that monitors active learning and management throughout programme implementation.
Budgetary allocations for gender equality will be made in the compilation of materials into local languages, as necessary.

Women are often excluded from economic activities related to forest products markets. Their access is limited by safety concerns (especially in northern provinces) and higher financial requirements (e.g. not having land titles), and may be more reluctant to take loans.


---
**Page 127**
---

314 Based on the identified gender gaps and recommendations, the following are some of the measures and targets that are included in the project’s GAP (for more detailed information, refer to Annex B):

* The Lac Women’s Union is part of the Project Steering Committee and joins key village level activities.
* Community awareness raising workshops will be conducted on topics such as GBV, HIV/AIDS, “Empowerment” workshop held for DPOs, DOF, and LURU staff – each in a different province.
* Target established for 50% of women in target communities state that they felt actively included in project-related activities.
* Training courses related to gender-based violence (GBV), sexual harassment (SHE) and violence against women (VAW) will be conducted. The objective is to ensure that the community has access to information, tools, and support gender sensitization trainings for men and women, and provides resources to participants communities related to combating GBV, SHE, and VAW.
* Facilitators are trained on the role of women led village patrolling groups, and disseminating best practices and lessons learned
* The results and outputs of the programme are reviewed by the programme’s gender specific prior to implementation to ensure mainstreaming of GBV (See Annex B for more information).
* A Gender Action Plan (GAP) developed under Project 1 will be followed, which set standards of accessibility for women, with a quota of at least 30% female committee members
* Major awareness raising and awareness raising campaigns are reviewed by the project’s safeguarding/gender specialist
* Gender-sensitive recruitment policy is in place in the business partner screening, and targets are established to support women-owned or led agri-MSEs.


G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately / page)


### G.3.1 Financial Management

315 The financial management of the project will follow GIZ’s internal rules and regulations. GIZ has bank accounts with Deutsche Bundesbank and Commerzbank. GIZ will not open a separate bank account for the GCF project but will ensure that all transactions are recorded in accordance with GIZ’s accounting principles and procedures as specified by German Government. As a general principle, GIZ disburses funds to its partners in accordance with the agreed budget and payment plan. The Government of Laos (PDRR) is required to report on the proper use of funds and defined progress as a prerequisite for any further disbursement of funds from the GCF.

316 At the beginning of each year, an annual plan is prepared, which expresses activities to be implemented by the project during that period. The annual plan is divided into four quarterly plans at the beginning of each quarter. From these quarterly plans, a quarterly budget is then prepared for each partner and submitted to the GCF. The quarterly budget is approved by the Project Director and the NPMU via the EPP. Each Partner Object Reporters on the use of these quarterly budgets to the EPP on a monthly basis. These reports are reviewed by the Project Director, NPMU, and the EPP. In addition, these monthly financial reports form the basis of the EPP’s financial reporting to GCF. In addition to the disbursement of funds from the GCF, in the case of PFAU, DPAU, funds are disbursed annually to the partner villages as Village Forest Agriculture Grant (VFAG). For this purpose, each partner village proposes an annual budget, which is transferred to the villages by the EPP.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP200

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project builds on the success of FP17-L (Lao Project 3). It continues to mobilize subnational actors and build on the lessons learned from FP17-L. | Like FP17-L, it is a multi-year programme drawing from multiple parties using various financial instruments. This implies that there may be a need for additional funding in order to ensure the implementation of project activities. |
| The project team (from Lao PDR) now includes 20 per cent women and 50 per cent youth, with teams focusing on increasing adaptive capacities of agroforestry systems and promoting cross-cutting project monitoring, mainstreaming adaptation actions into forest management plans. | The social and economic effects of COVID-19 may have an impact on the implementation of this project focused on capacity development and system strengthening, as well as on the quality of operations. There is a need to strengthen the project team in order to ensure its sustainability. |
| The project is expected to facilitate replication and upscaling as well as the broader distribution of PDR’s Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR) approach. | The Forest Protection Fund (FPF) is not yet fulfilling the fiduciary standards of the World Bank Group. The Lao PDR government has to handle the flow of funds for this time being. |

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum IX, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations”.

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

In Laos, forest cover accounts for 50 per cent of the country's surface area, significantly lower than the 70 per cent forest cover of the mid-1960s. In the past 15 years, net forest loss has amounted to more than 300,000 hectares (ha). The provinces in the northwestern part of Lao People’s Democratic Republic (PDR) (Hobon, Luang Namtha, Luang Prabang, Ouaddany, Sekong and Houaphan) experienced more than 40 per cent of the country's deforestation and forest degradation.

---

**Page 138**
---

Adaptation benefit (total beneficiaries): 510,392

As with FP117, the proposal remains fully aligned with the Land PDR/GCF Country Programme updated in February 2019), which identifies REDD+ as one of five short-term priorities.

With an implementation period of four years, this proposal will be implemented in all six provinces of Lao PDR (Bolikhamxay, Champasak, Khammouane, Luang Prabang, Savannakhet, and Vientiane). While Project 1 focused on areas which have the highest vulnerability to climate change impacts, Project 2 will concentrate on areas where poverty and nutrition are main issues, namely Luang Namtha, Bokeo and Oudomxay provinces. The project will focus on adaptation and mitigation interventions. The project is categorised as Category B based on the Environmental and Social Policy of GCF.

The project design is considered appropriate as it addresses both cross-cutting climate objectives. The climate change contribution of Project 2 is detailed below:

(a) Mitigation: the project is expected to reduce emissions by 4.6 million CO2e (an average of 13.7 per cent compared with FCPF Reference Level). These emission reductions due to reduced deforestation and forest degradation, and increase removals equivalent to 1.5 GtCO2e over a period of four years. This is an average reduction in emissions of annual emissions compared to FCPF Reference Level. The funding proposal has high impact on the environment.

(b) Adaptation: learning from Project 1, Project 2 intends to monitor its generated adaptation benefits. Laos is a least developed country (LDC) with limited adaptive capacity and is one of the most vulnerable countries in Southeast Asia and among the most climate-vulnerable countries in the region. Its economy is dependent on sectors which extract natural resources such as agriculture, forestry and mining, which account for 16 per cent of gross domestic product and employ 64 per cent of the Laotian workforce. These are also key areas identified in the country’s national adaptation programme and NDCs.

The programme, both Project 1 and 2 aims to address the decades of forest loss with support from the Lao PDR government and other stakeholders through the Lao PDR emission reduction programme. Building on and coordinating with complementary initiatives, Land PDR can leverage opportunities to further strengthen investment policy and building barriers in which to protect land-based poverty, economic gain, and the natural environment for deforestation-free development.

The total contribution of Project 2 under GCF is EUR 38.22 million. The total contribution of Project 1 includes grants, EUR 32.82 million, for the total GCF contribution; and co-financing is committed at EUR 41.26 million. These include contributions from the Government of Lao PDR, Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ), European Union (EU), International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD), KfW, and the World Bank (via FCFP). The estimated co-benefits are USD 105 million.

The executing entities (EE) for the project are the Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) in Laos and the Government of Lao People’s Democratic Republic (LPRD), Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry and the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment.

2.2 Component-by-component analysis

13. The project aims to catalyse a shift in land use, land-use change and forestry, and establish viable models for the management of forestry and landscapes. The project will have three outputs: (i) reducing deforestation and forest degradation; (ii) addressing agricultural drivers of deforestation; and (iii) climate change mitigation through forestry. These outputs are composed of closely interrelated activities, including those aimed at the development of a market-based approach to REDD+ implementation in Laos; strengthening of enforcement and monitoring; knowledge management; investments in good agriculture practices; capacity building for forest sector stakeholders; investment in rural infrastructure and watershed management; village forest management; and sustainable forest management.

Component 1: Enabling environment for REDD+ implementation (total cost: EUR 8.77 million; GCF cost: EUR 6.93 million)

14. The primary focus of this component is to unlock GHG emission reductions traded by the Climate, Community & Biodiversity Standard (established under Project 1) of the Environmental Protection Fund (EPF) as the EPF in exchange for results-based payments amounting to approximately USD 40 million.

15. This component strengthens the enabling environment for REDD+ through the following means: enhancing the availability of financing investments in climate resilient and deforestation-free landscapes; strengthening the institutional framework and development of new regulatory frameworks completed under Project 1, Project 2 will further strengthen the framework to ensure that all forest sector stakeholders can access climate change adaptation and mitigation benefits, in line with the latest NDCs in the United Nations' Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC); ensuring that landscape-level spatial planning are climate-informed; and selected measures not only support reducing deforestation and reducing emissions, but strengthen the resilience of local livelihoods, ecosystems and food security.

16. While scaling up Project 1 practices across all six provinces, the processes and lessons learned will be shared with other stakeholders in Laos to ensure that they can replicate and sustain them beyond implementation lifespan. In addition, this component includes targeted investments in rural infrastructure (e.g., water supply systems) through the EPF and the EPF). This component will be co-financed by BMG, KfW and Government of Laos.

Component 2: Market solutions for agricultural drivers of deforestation (total cost: EUR 37.61 million; GCF cost: EUR 41.11 million)

17. This component addresses key drivers of deforestation and forest degradation and contributes to reducing emissions through reducing the expansion of agricultural activities into forested landscapes. It addresses deforestation and forest degradation through the Promotion of Sustainable Agriculture (PSA) project, which will support smallholder farming models in a "White List". These farming models also include aspects of adaptation to climate change such as the use of drought-resistant crops, improved water harvesting, mixed cropping and agroforestry systems; and the adoption of low-emission processing methods applied by farmers. By promoting various farming models, the project also aims to support smallholder farmers in Luang Namtha province who are often dependent on one or two climate-sensitive crops (e.g., upland maize). Project 2 will see a continuation of the PSA project with an additional 100 villages supported under Project 2 with funding from the Village Forest and Agriculture Grants (VFAGs) developed under Project 1. PSAP activities will simply be scaled up to an additional 298 villages (240 in Luang Namtha).

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

27. The project, during its four-year implementation period, is expected to unlock REDD+ results-based payments of USD 40 million from the FCPF Carbon Fund. The project provides a strategic opportunity for scaling up and replication at national level in the context of Lao PDR while mitigating GHGs from the forest sector. Lao PDR has embraced REDD+ as a way to address its climate change challenges, and it is expected that the project’s design and financial arrangements (e.g., REDD+ funding window, deforestation-free agricultural supply chains) will ensure long-term sustainability of the investment.

28. The project provides an opportunity for scaling up and replication, knowledge learning, creation of an enabling environment and contribution to the regulatory framework development in Lao PDR. At the national level as well as at the pilot programme level, considerable scope exists to scale up/replicate in the remaining 11 provinces of Lao PDR as well as in neighbouring countries with similar landscape/brechey forests.

29. The project will target a number of key sectors and commodities where there is strong potential for transformational change. These sectors and commodities are: agriculture, forestry and financial sectors in particular will be targeted as key sectors for transforming the forest sector. This includes the development of agricultural and forestry supply chains, including input suppliers, producers, traders and buyers. The project will target sectors and commodities where there is strong and consistent demand to ensure the profitability of the investments.

30. New finance streams for forests will be mobilized, which will serve as a model for the rest of the country. ## Sustainable development potential

### Scale: High

As with Project L1, Project 2 creates economic, environmental, social and gender co-benefits beyond the typical climate mitigation and adaptation benefits. It directly contributes to targets 13.1, 13.2, 13.3 and 13.A of Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 13 (climate action) and contributes indirectly to SDGs 5, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 14, 15 and 17. It also contributes directly to targets 8.2, 8.3, 8.5, 8.9 and 8.10; SDG 15 (life on land) targets 15.1, 15.2, 15.3, 15.4, 15.5, 15.9, 15.14 and 15.20; SDG 8 (decent work and economic growth) target 8.7; SDG 10 (reduced inequalities) targets 2.3, 2.4, 2.5 and 2.6; and SDG 5 (gender equality) targets 5.5, 5.4 and 5.8. Indirectly, the project contributes to SDGs 7 (affordable and clean energy), 10 (reduced inequalities), 13 (responsible consumption and production) and SDG 17 (partnerships for the goals).

Regarding the economic pillar, the project is expected to improve the livelihoods and incomes of the rural population through increased agricultural productivity and diversification; strengthen domestic resource mobilization. As for the social pillar, the project will contribute to reducing poverty by increasing access to education and training opportunities; improving productivity; improving employment opportunities, including for women and ethnic groups; and improving health services in the region.

Finally, with regard to the environmental pillar, the project will reduce GHG emissions from the forestry sector and increase carbon sequestration from the atmosphere, and contribute to the sustainable management of forests through improved forest governance and land use planning.

In terms of environmental benefits, the Project will promote the sustainable management of 2.1 million hectares of degraded lands in the Lao PDR, including through the Promotion of Agroforestry Agricultural Practices, the 31 sustainable farming models in a "White List", and participatory land use planning, which is an essential prerequisite and forms the base for Village Forest Management (VFM) and Community Forestry Management supported by the VFGAs arrangement under the LPP.

All these improved land uses contribute to mitigation on greenhouse gas emissions and sequestration, with reductions of 17.8 MEOG over 20 years. Co-benefits from biodiversity protection from habitat restoration and conversion are also expected.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

50. Project Background: The project (Project 21) is the second project under the GCF-funded programme for the implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Program through Improved Governance and Sustainable Forest Landscape Management (FP171) approved in 2019. This project builds on the achievements made by Project 1, which has been fully covered under Project 1 and new provisions. The project’s co-benefits include improved food security for local communities, participation and engagement of LUPD (Local User Production and Development) groups, and enhanced biodiversity conservation through the integrated LUPD and targeted interventions that reduce deforestation and forest degradation.

51. Environmental and Social Risk Category: The project is primarily designed to address deforestation and ensure ecosystem and social resilience. Hence, the anticipated adverse environmental impacts are being contained by the project’s mitigation strategies. Residual impacts are mostly activity-specific which are minimal due to the small scale of the interventions and the fact that they are complemented with other complementary conservation measures. The project is thus classified by the AE as “Category II” in terms of environmental risk, while the Secretariat confirms this risk level and that it is within the AE’s E&S risk accreditation level.

52. Safeguard Instruments: The AE has submitted an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) for Project 21 to the Secretariat for approval under the GCF Safeguards Framework (GCF SF). The ESIA is based on the original ESIA of FP171, “Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission


---
**Page 146**
---

Reduction of greenhouse gas emissions through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management (Project 13), which was updated to reflect changes in the activities, experiences and lessons learned from the implementation of the ongoing Project 1 as well as with new information available.

5.4 Compliance with the GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) Standards.
The discussion below provides a description of how the project will comply with the requirements of GCF ESSs.

### 5.4.1 ESS1 (assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts).
The ESIA described the range of activities that are included in the funding proposal and identified the key areas where potential environmental and social risks may arise, including the nature, extent, frequency and scale of the activities that it intends to support and provided the identification and analysis of these risks. The Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) is also provided.
The ESMP described a set of actions that will address the identified risks and impacts in order to ensure that they are managed effectively. Given that the exact locations of the interventions are yet to be identified during implementation, the project has a checklist for environmental and social screening to ensure that various risks relevant to the project are identified at an early stage and addressed through mitigation measures and monitored throughout the project duration.
The project also adopted the International Finance Corporation (IFC) Performance Standards. The IFC Performance Standards require that those with potential significant adverse E&S risk or/and impacts are not funded under the project.

### 5.4.2 ESS2 (labour and working conditions).
Given the small scale of its activities, the project does not expect to involve large scale hiring of staff or sub-contractors.
The project's activities could however lead to exposure to occupational health risks for workers involved in forest management activities such as cutting operations. Training and capacity-building will be conducted on good agricultural practices and the use of appropriate tools to reduce any risk to health and safety of workers. The capacity development and support that will be provided through the project is expected to have a positive impact on working conditions with adherence to minimum labour laws and regulations will also be included in the grant agreement with every MSME that will receive funding through the Matching Fund Mechanism (MFM). This will ensure that no workers are subjected to forced or exploitative forms of forced labour or harmful child labour.

### 5.4.3 ESS3 (energy use).
The project is not expected to use significant amounts of fuel and power.
In terms of pollution issues, some project activities, such as reforestation and crop production may involve the use of chemical fertilizers and pesticides which could lead to air or water pollution if not used in accordance with good agricultural practices on the scale of operations. However, the use of chemical fertilizers and pesticides will not be advocated by the project.
The project will promote the application of good agricultural practices. The role that fertilizers and pesticides may still be used by project beneficiaries is expected to be minimized through the promotion of alternative measures.
Crop processing facilities will not be established at large scale than the amount of solid and liquid waste generated from these activities, which can be managed through training in good agricultural practices and the inclusion of proper waste management in the development of the respective business plans.

### 5.4.4 ESS4 (health and safety).
Potential community health and safety issues in the project include potential exposure of local communities to forest fires/wildfires, possible exposure to vector-borne diseases such as malaria and dengue fever due to deforestation and ecologically induced outbreaks of vector-borne diseases from the forest forest management and forest restoration activities. The risk of outbreaks is however considered low and the project will provide training in good agricultural practices, fire prevention and control measures, landscape restoration and forest fire control measures.
The ESIA has also discussed the hazards


---
**Page 147**
---

From ILO and pesticides and included a UXO Protocol and a Pesticide Management Plan in the ESMP.

### ESSS (land acquisition and involuntary resettlement)
The project involves formulating and implementation of land use plans, village forest plans, and natural protected area plans as well as technical assistance to communities. The project will also ensure that the process of land acquisition or promote involuntary resettlement entails both physical or economic displacement and loss of livelihoods. Activities include awareness raising and capacity building among others, activities that would entail physical and economic displacement, and those that would result in a loss of livelihoods. The project will provide technical assistance to communities employed to ensure that those who may be impacted by the invasion of lands during the PLUP process are provided with land or other means to reach an agreed land use plan. An Empowerment Governance Redress Mechanism (GEM) will also be established and implemented to ensure that no beneficiary will lose access to land. Additional measures to minimize the remaining risk of unintended displacement include awareness raising, capacity building among others which includes awareness raising and capacity-building in an inclusive, culturally appropriate manner to ensure proper implementation of the ESMP.

### ESS6 (biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources)
The project is intended to contribute to biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of forests. However, implementation of changes in land use plan, protected area management plans, and village forest plans may potentially have risks and unintended impacts to biodiversity and the natural ecosystems on which it depends. These include but are not limited to: emergence of new pests and diseases, negative impacts on endangered species and impacts negative impacts on genetic diversity in agricultural crops and livestock, expansion of agricultural areas and pollution from agrochemicals. However, the project and its design and approach are expected to mitigate these risk factors for instance; the project will only introduce specific genetically modified organisms (GMO) that have been approved by the national authorities. Practices will be promoted. The ESMP further emphasizes the promotion of awareness on biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources, proper sourcing of seeds and planting materials including mitigations measures for possible pesticide use non-compliance. The project will also promote the establishment of community nurseries. It also discusses measures for wildlife management, watershed protection, establishment of biodiversity conservation areas, and the development of forest-based enterprises and diversification of agricultural biodiversity due to the introduction of sustainable practices.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS7 (Indigenous Peoples)
There is a high potential for negative impacts on indigenous peoples' rights to land and forest livelihoods. Given the focus of the project there is a strong possibility impact on livelihoods, particularly among land users who are not formally recognized. The ESMP provides a considered analysis of the issues in the ESIA and EEP and recommendations into the ESMP. An Ethnic Groups Development Plan (EGDP) is also provided to guide the implementation of activities that will promote the empowerment of indigenous peoples. If these activities are not monitored closely, the project could unnecessarily exacerbate externally caused trends toward denial of land rights and forest tenure security for indigenous peoples. Such the ESIA and EEP provide input on shifting cultivation and the differences between "traditional" shifting cultivators and those who have been displaced by development into (designated) forest land at village level (thangha houh). The project notes to support culturally appropriate livelihoods, including the promotion of sustainable agriculture and pioneering shifting cultivation. While the project aims to promote sustainable patterns of land and forest use, the national legal framework on customary uses of land and forest does not accord recognition to indigenous peoples' rights to land and forest tenure security. Implementation barriers that include language and literacy there is some risk of marginalizing customary forest users.

### ESSB (cultural heritage)

The ESSB has not identified cultural heritage sites, buildings or monuments that are at risk of being impacted by the project activities. However, actual sites of the activity will be subject to a cultural impact assessment prior to implementation.

In terms of co-benefits, the ESF is among its measures the respect for customary use of lands and forests, including traditional land tenure systems, indigenous knowledge and practices, values, traditional knowledge management system and practices, occupations and livelihoods. To support this, the Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE and EES.

### Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment

The revised GCF Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) includes a new section regarding sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) in GCF-financed activities. The ESF for scaling up forest landscape restoration (FLR) projects under the Sustainable Land Management and sustainable forest land management (Project 2) includes key SEAR safeguarding measures to address SEAH risks. These include a commitment to follow the application of social inclusion principles to inform project affected people about the availability of services, including those related to sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment. The Redress Mechanism for redressing grievances. The ESF has also indicated the alignment of social inclusion regulatory frameworks including those on SEAR and gender-based violence in general (to include SEAH), criteria for eligible grievance exclusions and that will actively include SEAR and criteria for the identification of cases related to SEAH, as well as the mechanisms by which these are investigated. Additionally, the project has linked the ESF and the gender action plan (GAP) regarding the inclusion of measures to address sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment in gender, gender-based violence and SEAH awareness, establishment of coordination mechanisms and referral systems in order to deal with SEAH cases and provide timely services to survivors.

# Implementation arrangements

The project will be implemented under the existing institutional arrangements of Project 1 which consists of a National Project Steering Committee and a National Project Management Unit (NPMU). The NPMU is responsible for implementing the project in coordination with Provincial PMUs. The provincial PMUs are ultimately responsible for engaging with communities to implement project activities.

The arrangement for the implementation will be similar to that with the Project Safeguards Team, taking into account their composition, expertise, and experience. Provincial and district focal persons will also assist in coordinating and ensuring the benefits of other government programs and projects are integrated into the project.

# Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

The project has extended multi-stakeholder engagements and consultations which have informed the preparation and design of Project 1. The project intends to continue this process, with continuous stakeholder participation during project implementation through the actions in the ESMP that will be developed for each component of the project. Such actions may include, but are not limited to, such as the PFLIP process, the project intends to employ an inclusive engagement of the poor and vulnerable groups including women, youth, indigenous peoples, and people with disabilities.

# Grievance Redress Mechanism

The project will establish a GRM to enable the beneficiaries and stakeholders to raise any environmental and social grievances they may have with the project and the Project Safeguards Team will act upon and monitor any issues. The GRM will be established in accordance with the Lao PDR’s National Action Plan on Access to Justice and the engagement process and will be available throughout the project cycle. Stakeholders will also be able to submit their grievances through the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism or through the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF indigenous peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

## Gender policy

### The gender assessment is undertaken based on a desk review of available information and consultations with communities in villages of target provinces: Sayaboury, Luang Prabang, Xieng Khouang, and Bokeo.

### Laws PDR is a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and has ratified the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action. The Lao PDR as a member state of the South Asian Nations it has made a number of gender mainstreaming declarations such as the Declaration of the Advancement of Women in 1986. The country has a legal framework for promoting the equality between men and women, including the law on equal rights to education, equal rights in all spheres of life – political, social, cultural and in the family. This is further supported by national policies such as the National Action Plan on Gender Equality and the Law on Women and Mother-Child which is responsible for formulating and implementing national policy for the promotion of women, and mainstreaming gender aspects in all sectors. The Lao Women’s Union


---
**Page 150**
---

which has an extensive network of representations, even at the village council level, but lacks a constitutional right to protect women’s rights and interests. The Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry also has a division for the advancement of women and focal points.

### Gender assessment has identified challenges faced by women in the sections of interventions. Women who are heads of households or members of extended households in rural Laos PDR have access to land, livelihood diversification and cash income. Traditional gender norms and practices that restrict women’s mobility and decision-making power negatively affect women's participation, their confidence to speak up and voice opinions against men, and their ability to participate in community activities.

For example, stakeholder engagement undertaken by the AI pointed out women’s roles in collection of non-timber forest products, and how households with female heads are more likely to be significant but without institutionalized participation in decision-making in village forest management committees due to not having met the minimum education requirement. This results in their voices being ignored or not taken into consideration when decisions are made.

The land law gives both women and men equal access to agricultural and forest land but has no provisions for women’s rights to inherit land. The collection of resources by women also extends to farming inputs, finance and knowledge of alternative livelihoods. Women are the primary users of natural resources in rural Laos PDR, yet they face critical issues since 80 per cent of the agricultural workforce are women. Violence against women is a problem that has not been adequately addressed as legal and social mechanisms are influenced by gender norms and may not be easily accessible to women.

### The GAP contains activities, performance indicators, sex-disaggregated targets, responsible persons, and monitoring and evaluation criteria. The first year of implementation will see the introduction of additional beneficiary provisions with the understanding that the project will be fully implemented in the second year. Project 2 seeks to build on progress made by Project 1 through a series of recommendations for the revised GAP which also includes results reported in the latest annual performance report of Project 1. Measures included in the action plan to address issues raised in the review include: increasing women’s participation in forest management committees and other village-level decision-making bodies, particularly at local structures, by setting a quota of at least 30 per cent women in village committees in forest management and administrative skills. Land use plans will be developed in a participatory manner with women and men to ensure that they are gender-sensitive while shifting from practices that result in deforestation and land degradation. Other benefits are agricultural inputs for women, training for women on business development including female entrepreneurs, and support for women-led MMEs in the agricultural sector to access finance through the VEFID funds. The project also seeks to empower women with information pertaining to their rights and responsibilities under the law by establishing a grievance mechanism and referral service. Perception surveys will be collected on GAP activities.

### Three recommendations are made to the AE, First, to assess the distribution of labour among men and women to ensure that all actors receive adequate resources to strengthen their resilience in the face of climate change. Second, to ensure that women’s participation is sustained with PSEP activities where women and men have different roles and responsibilities in the national context. Third, to ensure that gender equality is reflected in the project’s rational targets. For example, the target of 40 per cent women participating in meetings was often not achieved for Project 1 and may therefore be unrealistic for Project 2 or might require
