### 4.4 Fiduciary

In the GIZ head office will serve as the accredited entity (AE), while the GIZ office in Lao PDR together with the Government of Lao PDR represented by the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment and Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry will fulfill the roles of executing entities (EE).

As the AE, it will oversee oversight responsibility as defined in the accreditation master agreement between GCF and GIZ (AE). The AE will administer the project funds on behalf of GCF and provide oversight guidance and quality assurance of the Environmental Protection Fund (EPF).

In its capacity as an EE, GIZ will lead and provide overall management of the technical assistance to the EPF. This includes overseeing the implementation of the EPF, managing the project budget of GIZ (EE); liaising with the GIZ Regional Office based in Vietnam regarding the disbursement of funds; ensuring the quality assurance of the EPF; and coordinating project implementation with co-financing development partners and their projects and counterparts, as well as other donors and projects operating in the same technical and/or geographical area.

Project management units will be established at the national, provincial and district levels. The Project Management Unit (PMU) at the national level will remain under the Department of Forestry of MAF; under Project 1 it will remain in place to manage Project 2. The Provincial Project Management Units and District Project Management Units, under the instruction of the NPMLs, will be responsible for implementing the EPF activities within their respective areas and mirroring national-level responsibilities at the local level, project management units at the provincial and district levels will be established as required.

The financial management of the project will follow the internal rules and regulations of GIZ. GIZ will not open a special bank account for the GCF project but will ensure that all hands provided by GCF are used exclusively for this purpose. The AE will establish and manage separate cost units exclusively for the funds disbursed by GCF for each fund activity (ledger accounts). The AE will be responsible for ensuring that the EPF is managed as a single cost unit according to generally accepted accounting principles and procedures accepted by the Government of Lao PDR.

Additionally, the EEs are mandated to report on the proper use of funds and define progress as a prerequisite for any further disbursement. GIZ will engage independent external auditors to conduct annual financial audits of the project in line with the International Standards on Auditing.


### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

As a mitigation project, the forestry and land use interventions of the investment expects to benefit 153,700 direct and 332,990 indirect beneficiaries. Through coverage of approximately 2,116,000 hectares under reforestation/afforestation, the mitigation interventions estimated a GCF cost per tCO2eq removed of EUR 5.7 per tCO2eq.

## Legal assessment

The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and that it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

The GCF is not privy to the Privileges and Immunities Law (Law PIB), in a country in which the GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, according to the law, the GCF cannot be considered as an entity of the country, which risks need to be further assessed. The GCF Secretariat provided the NDA of Lao PDR with a draft privilege and immunity agreement on 21 February 2019 and again on 23 March 2019. Please see the draft agreement.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have provided their comments on the proposed conditions, including legal activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are subject to the approval of the IRM or IIU in cases of potential expropriation in the country(ies), or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

## List of proposed conditions (including legal)

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following:

- Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and
- Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP200

## Proposal name: Scaling up the implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable landscape management (Project 2)

### Accredited entity:
Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH

### Country/[ies]:
Lao People's Democratic Republic

### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The Lao People’s Democratic Republic (Lao PDR) is a landlocked least developed country (LLDC) with a population of approximately 6.5 million people, and an area of 236,800 km². Its economy is dependent on natural resources, especially forestry, agriculture, electricity generation and hydropower. The forest sector in Lao PDR accounts for about 17% of the GDP and account for 16 per cent of gross domestic product and employ 64 per cent of the Laos workforce. The forestry sector provides a livelihood to approximately 50 percent of the population, especially for nutrition and livelihoods for the rural population, in particular, the rural poor. Approximately 80 per cent of the population lives in remote areas with limited access to health services, clean water, medicines and spiritual protection. The forests of Lao PDR are at the heart of the globally recognized Mekong Biodiversity Hotspot.

In 2019, as part of the review process of the Board (B234) of GEF Board-approved FP117 submitted by GIZ to accredited entity (AE) and representing the Lao PDR programme “Implementation of the Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management”, the programme area consists of six provinces (Bokeo, Luang Prabang, Xieng Khouang, Phongsaly, Xayaboury and Savannakhet) being among those most vulnerable to climate change in Lao PDR. These six provinces represent approximately 35 per cent of the national territory and 12 per cent of the country’s forest cover. Around 70% of the population lives in these provinces, which are characterized by remote areas within these provinces. The remote accessibility of this territory and limited public industrial infrastructure have resulted in a high level of poverty and lack of access to basic services due to massive constraints in transportation, accessibility of social welfare, promotion of industry and trade, among others.

Initially, the programme submitted as FP117 was conceptualized as a mitigation-focused single project. Later, due to limited GEF funding available at the time of submission, the project was reformed as a programme with three sub-projects (Project 1, Project 2 and Project

## Component 3: changes the concept from “climate change mitigation through forestry” to “climate change mitigation through sustainable land management”. This was re-framed as a cross-cutting output, Project 3, in addition to climate change mitigation, and one of its outputs is to reduce emissions and strengthen the environment (introduced by Component 1), and will reduce emissions and strengthen the resilience of local livelihoods and forest ecosystems through sustainable forest land management. The third output on climate change mitigation through forestry (Component 2) has a focus on village and conservation forests. The third output on climate change mitigation through sustainable land management (Component 3) focuses on protection/creation forests to allow landscape management and restoration.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

The country is committed to effecting a paradigm shift in the agriculture sector, moving from traditional, agrarian practices based on deforestation to a low-emission development pathway (LEDP) that will reduce GHG emissions by up to 50% and avoid further loss of forest cover. The project aims at reducing deforestation through implementing various innovative financial schemes and programmes.

The project has several innovative elements supported by GIZ and the Emission Reduction Programme (ERP). These include: (a) a new approach in financing the agricultural, financial sectors and business activities is critical to enable real land use changes and more sustainable forest and food systems. The project is also enabling a sustainable agriculture economy ensuring that small-scale farmers are able to earn their livelihoods from activities that have potential demand.

The national carbon module (GIZ/REDD+) developed by the German Federal Ministry for Economic Development Cooperation (BMZ) and implemented through the German Development Bank (KfW) and the Emission Reduction Programme, supported by the World Bank (IFC), is a key element of the project. The financial scheme has been successfully piloted in 193 villages.

(a) GIZ will work with local communities to improve forest management practices, reduce deforestation hotspot villages to improving planning to increase the efficiency and productivity of land use, while also promoting the sustainable management of forest areas. In total, 200 villages are planned for this activity. The first 193 villages in project 1, 193 PFLPs have already completed, from which seventy are updated existing PFLPs and thirty-seven new PFLPs.

(b) Free, prior, and informed consent (FPIC) activities which should help small-scale farmers, with a focus on ethnic groups in remote places. It is possible that the programme will be able to contribute to the reduction of poverty and improve some families' livelihoods. Therefore, it is of the utmost importance for the programme to establish strong relationships with local communities and other stakeholders from diverse stakeholder groups, including civil society organizations (CSOs), in programme implementation and monitoring processes which should also be part of the FPI process.

(c) Existing villages means villages which already, before project 1, have had one or two projects supported by international development agencies. These villages were selected since 1996 and 2018. These existing land plot areas were reviewed, updated or reduced during PFLP 2.0.

# Project 1: FPG already completed in all 240 villages. FPC 2 and 3 activities planned for project 2 are already completed in 144 villages.

The fund for Village Forest and Agriculture Grants (VFAG) will be established under activity 2.1.2 (investment in sustainable climate resilience and deforestation-free agriculture). The VFAG is a community-managed revolving fund that provides conditional payments for implementation of FSAP activities from the "White List" and annual village forest management plans. Currently, the VFAG has been established in all 240 villages targeted under project 1. A Village Forest Management Plan (VFMP) will be established in each of the 290 target villages. Currently a VFAG structure is set up in the 240 villages targeted under project 1 and financial management training has been completed.

The VFMPs are being designed using a combination of spatial planning, community consultation, and participatory mapping to ensure that they enable the sustainable management of village forests. A total of 172 VFMPs were established in the project territory (Project 1 and Project 2).

The Forest Carbon Partnership Programme was accepted into the FCPF Carbon Fund in June 2018 and an Emission Reductions Payment Agreement was signed in December 2020 for R4.8 million to be paid out over a period of five years, making Indonesia the first partner country in the FCP since 2008. This Emissions Reductions Programme covers the programme territory (Project 1 and Project 2).

The project is demonstrating a paradigm shift in ensuring an effective monitoring, reporting and verification system and coordination with relevant projects taking place in the region to ensure that the project contributes towards achieving national climate change for the jurisdictions and the country. By doing so, the project is providing new financial sources for village forests through downsizing the process of a REDD+ finance model.

The main principles of Green Growth Strategy are to: (i) support the development of the region; (ii) shift from unsustainable and hazardous business to sustainable and deforestation-free businesses; and (iii) ensure that the project is supported by national policies. It should be considered as the most effective approach to achieve sustainable transformation of the country's economy and should be further supported to reach a high transformative impact.

## Sustainable development potential

The underlying principle of the entire programme is that of sustainable development.
Project 2 will further generate additional environmental, social and economic benefits that contribute towards achieving the Green Growth Strategy to 2030, and the country’s recovery from the COVID-19 pandemic.

The main principles of Green Growth Strategy are to: (i) support the development of the region; (ii) shift from unsustainable and hazardous business to sustainable and deforestation-free businesses; and (iii) ensure that the project is supported by national policies. It should be considered as the most effective approach to achieve sustainable transformation of the country's economy and should be further supported to reach a high transformative impact.

All project activities will proactively involve empowerment elements for village women, including promoting their leadership roles in local structures, including ensuring a quota of at least 30 per cent female presence on village committees and participation rates of women in community-based organizations. The project aims to provide training and capacity building as well as skills development for women that will help to address common participation gaps and barriers, including lack of access to information and resources. The project also seeks to create opportunities for women related to agroforestry, forest management and climate resilient agricultural activities.

There are at least 23 specific ethnic groups present in the project area. The project aims to work with these groups through an inclusive approach that is sensitive to cultural diversity and targeted to their needs. An Ethnic Group Development Plan (EGDP) was prepared, and is attached as Annex I of this document. The EGDP provides a framework for the project to promote an inclusive approach, ensuring beneficiaries from diverse ethnic groups and marginalized villages are included and empowered. Priorities outlined in the EGDP include strengthening community-based organizations; improving access to education, health care and natural resources for livelihood purposes; respect for customary use of land (including for ancestral burial grounds); and gender equality.

Potential of the project to significantly contribute to the sustainable development of Lao PDR is very high.


### Needs of the recipient Scale: High

* **Lao PDR** is a least developed country with a human development index rating of 0.613, ranking at 137th (out of 194 countries) globally. Prior to the COVID-19 pandemic, Lao PDR’s growth had been strong and steady, but has slowed in recent years. The poverty rate was still higher than 7.7 per cent per year for most of the last decade. In 2016–2021, the gross national income (GNI) per capita increased by an average annual rate of 5.3 per cent and the poverty rate has declined from 27.6 per cent in 2008 to about 18.9 per cent in 2019.
* **Economic expansion**. Due to such rapid economic expansion and poverty reduction achieved, Lao PDR is now facing significant challenges related to economic vulnerability in an unsustainable manner; significant challenges are faced by the country: degradation and depletion of natural resources; climate change; environmental pollution; increase of greenhouse gases (GHG); and climate change and natural disasters.
* Despite the economic growth observed in Lao PDR, the country continues to face economic vulnerabilities. The World Bank’s Doing Business 2019 report for Development Policy, a subsidiary body of the United Nations Economic and Social Council based in New York, the 2018 review finds that Lao PDR ranks last among all countries in terms of ease of doing business with Assets Index, a composite index evaluating its human capital. The Economic Vulnerability Index, measuring economic vulnerability to climate change, is also high.
* **GHG emissions**. According to the country’s Second National Communication to the UNFCCC, 83 per cent of greenhouse gas emissions emitted from Lao PDR territory are from changes in land use and forestry (including deforestation and forest degradation), mainly for land clearance for expanding agricultural production areas; and infrastructure development projects. About 15 per cent of GHG emissions are from the agriculture sector and about 2 per cent are from the energy sector.

### Climate change and natural disasters

Such unsustainable use of local natural resources and changes in the global climate as reflected on local climatic parameters have caused adverse impacts on agricultural production and led to the occurrence of natural disasters such as floods, droughts, and landslides.

According to the statistics from the Ministry of Planning and Investment (MPI), during the period from 1970 to 2018, about 3.5 million people were affected by floods annually, resulting in the displacement of more than 4.9 million people (cumulative number) and causing a total loss amounting to more than US$600 million per year. The average annual flood damage is estimated at US$2 billion. The cost of flood each year is between 2.8 per cent and 3.6 per cent of GDP, and the government had spent about 2.7 per cent of the budget in flood protection and relief.

### Green Climate Fund (GCF)

Laos PDR's economy has been greatly affected by the COVID-19 pandemic. UNICEF released a series of reports in 2021 that noted that the country’s poverty rate increased from 35 to 47 per cent, and 13 per cent of children had stunted growth. In addition, the pandemic has generated other substantial impacts including loss of income, mental health impacts for the population, increased unemployment, poverty, reduced nutrition, and an increase in food insecurity.

The government has been implementing a number of interventions to support agriculture and agriculture conducted by the World Food Programme (WFP) in May 2020 found producers in Bolaven Plateau and other areas were facing difficulties in accessing markets for their produce, especially cash crop producers (cf. for example, cardamom, rubber, tea and coffee) and horticultural producers.

### Key documents

Two key strategic documents should be considered within the context of the funding proposal, covering the country's needs and planning ways of shifting to low-emission, sustainable development. These are the country’s nationally determined contribution (NDC) and Green Climate Fund (GCF).

### NDC: Conditional and unconditional targets are considered in the NDC

(a) An unconditional target for 2020–2030 is to reduce land-use change and forestry emissions by at least 57 per cent below business-as-usual levels, which is a threefold reduction objective compared to those achieved between 2000 and 2015. According to the government’s NDC, this target will be achieved through implementing the GCF FP117 and the World Bank “Land Lociances and Livelihoods Project (P179359).” The country's position is that in all activities, forest management issues shall be addressed with a focus on sustainable land management. This includes efforts to enhance land data management and accessibility. The national level unconditional target for 2030 is to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation by around 62,000 tCO2e in absolute terms.

(b) The conditional mitigation scenario and targets are the GHG emission reductions effects due to the PDR’s NDC implementation with increased levels of financial support from developed country parties. A conditional target for the land-use change and forestry sector is to reduce emissions by 75 per cent below business-as-usual levels. This will be achieved through increased forest cover to 70 per cent of land area (on 16,38 million ha). The government’s NDC also includes a number of other measures such as afforestation, fostering conservation, sustainable management of forests, buffer zones of national parks and other reserves, and enhancement of forest carbon stocks.


---
**Page 164**
---

### Ongoing mitigation efforts combined with the new 2030 unconditional and conditional targets established above would position the country on a pathway to net zero emissions by 2050, even earlier if the conditional target is achieved in the forest sector.

- Financial support for implementation of mitigation measures;
- Support in the design of innovative financial mechanisms that can blend public and private resources to finance projects mitigating risks and unlocking private sector investment in climate projects;
- Continuous administrative, legal, technical and institutional capacity-building, policy development and coordination with relevant stakeholders (e.g. UNFCCC);
- Measurement, reporting and verification, data collection, processing and management for GHG emissions inventories, GHG and climate modelling, carbon trading and other related activities;
- Enhanced mainstreaming of climate change into national and subnational policies, including through climate action planning tools such as climate risk screening and vulnerability assessments.

30. The country would welcome legal and technical assistance in adjusting forest lands demarcation to match existing forest cover and avoid conversion to other land use of forested areas consistent with the National Forest Strategy.

35. The programme and Project 2 contribute to most of these conditions for fulfillment of the determinants of success.

40. In 2020, the estimated national GHG emissions reduction progress assessment showed that between 2000 and 2020 Lao PDR achieved a reduction of emission by 34 per cent compared to business as usual scenario. The country has been able to maintain this trend in sustainability but this trend cannot be guaranteed at that time, bearing in mind that progress is not yet observed on several key areas.

### Green Growth Strategy: For mitigation of ongoing economic expansion and degradation of natural resources, the Government of Lao PDR developed the National Green Growth Strategy (NGGS) for 2016-2035. The NGGS was approved by the Council of Ministers in June 2017. The GGS consists of Vision 2030; 10-Year Strategic Plan (2016–2025); and the ninth Five-Year Socio-Economic Development Plan (FSDP) (2016-2020). These sectoral plans are the basis for government policies. It is also vital that it is crucial that GHG-related indicators are incorporated in these plans. They are outcome-based plans, including clear development outcomes and outputs corresponding to each of the five pillars of green growth. The plans are designed with strong harmonization with national plans and are closely aligned with available sources of funding (including international climate finance, domestic resources, private sector investments, grants, and investments in the financial system).

45. The updated NGGS highlights the role of the National Green Growth Strategy for 2030 for climate change adaptation and mitigation focus area with the objective of reducing vulnerability of the country and population, especially disadvantaged groups, to natural disasters and extreme weather events that are becoming more frequent, severe and unpredictable. Transitions to a green growth model implies a behavioral change that needs to be embedded in all sectors of the economy.

45. In its Second National Communication to the UNFCCC, Lao PDR identifies itself as an LDC with limited adaptive capacities that is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. The Economy of Lao PDR is one of the most climate-vulnerable economies (in terms of GDP) and PDR is one of the most climate-vulnerable countries in the region.

## Country Ownership

### Scale: High

- The funding proposal scales up the implementation of the Lao PDR Forest Rehabilitation Programme (LPRFP) towards improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management (Project 2) is part of the Lao PDR GCF Country Programme 2020–2023 under the Priority Axis 1: “Strengthening the capacity for climate change adaptation, including through forest restoration, conservation, improved forest governance and private sector investment”.
- The country Programme states that the national designated authority (NDA) of Lao PDR nominated the project to the GCF. In the context of the NDA’s submission to the EPF was provided by GEF. By continuing with work on EPF, the project will continue to build the capacity of the NDA and other relevant stakeholders in Lao PDR.
- The project is closely aligned with the Government of Lao PDR's socioeconomic development plans and strategy, as well as priorities for climate change mitigation and adaptation. The project design has been updated since the approval of PI17 by GEF. Since the approval of PI17 by GEF, the design of Project 2 has been adapted to reflect the (draft) Climate Change Strategy (DCCS), updated NDC (2011), and Future Strategy in 2015, among others, strengthening the focus on adaptation and climate change measures in the AWDU sector.

### GCF (R3/192/G2/AD40/Rtn-01)

#### Section 56

In recent years, the Government of Lao PDR embarked on several reforms that provide a platform for launching the GCF project interventions. In 2012, the government issued a suspension on granting new concessions for mining and rubber investments, which remains in effect until 2037. The Forest Law Enforcement Governance and Trade (FLEGT) Action Plan took effect since 2013 in order to protect natural forests from unsustainable exploitation. In 2016, the government adopted a National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (NCCSAP), which is already demonstrating significant impact and underlies the ongoing Forest Law Enforcement Governance and Trade (FLEGT) Action Plan and the Lao PDR’s membership in the Voluntary Partnership Agreement with the European Union. The adoption of the Central Party Committee Resolution on Land in 2017, indicating the need for reform in land management, has paved the way for a new Land Law in 2019 (entered into force in January 2020). The government’s decision to submit the draft Land Law to the National Assembly in June 2019, provides improved rights for local communities for land use.

In 2018, the Lao PDR adopted a new Forest Policy and Strategy, which is higher-level policy statements and decisions, including the sixth NBSAP: 2021 - 2025. The National REDD+ Task Force, representing diverse economic sectors, including forestry, agriculture, mining, energy, transport, water resources, environment, health, education, local administration, Ministry of Justice, Ministry of Finance, Ministry of Planning and Investment, the Lao Front for National Development (LFND), and other relevant stakeholders.

The government, together with development partners, has put in place an extensive institutional framework at national and subnational levels to implement REDD+, including the Emissions Reductions from Forests and Land Use (ERFLU) Task Force, representing diverse economic sectors, including forestry, agriculture, mining, energy, transport, water resources, environment, health, education, local administration, Ministry of Justice, Ministry of Finance, Ministry of Planning and Investment, the Lao Front for National Development (LFND), and other relevant stakeholders.

The National Project Steering Committee (NPSC) will provide administrative oversight of the project, ensuring coordination across ministries and will provide strategic implementation guidance. The NPSC is supported by two sub-committees: the Provincial Project Steering Committees (PPSCs), the Provincial REDD+ Task Force. At the provincial level, the PPSC will provide technical implementation progress and provide support to address potential bottlenecks related to implementation.

The Government of Lao PDR, represented by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry25) and the National Project Steering Committee (NPSC), signed a Memorandum of Understanding with the GCF. The Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment in the GCF NCA of Lao PDR and the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry serves as the Chair of the REDD+ Task Force and is (together with the Ministry of Finance) the contracting signatory party with the GCF Carbon Fund to set GHG emission reductions and revenue generation targets. The NPSC will be responsible for project implementation, and the government is responsible for the FFF, which will have two roles in the project: (a) as a beneficiary receiving capacity building support from the FFF; and (b) as a potential donor once its capacities are sufficiently built, to channel funds to beneficiaries.

While the legal agreement with GIZ (as the ADF) was signed with the government, the GCF-funded project will be implemented by the EPF, which will be responsible for the financial management and disbursement of grants to other beneficiaries.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

#### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

As a part of the initial programme, potential efficiency and effectiveness of Project 2 is assessed by the independent TAP based on the information reported through Project 1 APRs provided to the TAP in December 2020 and January 2021. A preliminary assessment of the independent TAP assessment are from the latest APR (2021) which provides information on the progress made during the period between 1 July 2020 and 31 December 2020, and the planned year of implementation (1 January 2021 to 31 December 2021). The 2021 APR demonstrates that:

- Highest implementation progress was achieved in Component 1 "Enabling environment for REEDD+ implementation". Average progress of this component is in the range of 50 to 70 per cent. This component includes activities related to policy and institutional strengthening (see paragraph 10a), support on land-use planning, and improvement of land tenure security.

- Component 2 "Market solutions for agricultural drivers of deforestation", has achieved 20 per cent progress for activity 2.1 ("Local incentives for good agricultural practices and agroforestry") and 30 per cent progress for activities 2.2 (Sustainable management investment in value chains) and 2.3 (“Sustainable rural infrastructure watershed management areas”).

- Component 3 "Climate change mitigation action through forestry", has achieved 20 per cent progress in two activities, 3.1 ("Village forest management") and 3.2 ("Sustainable management of community forests"), and 30 per cent for activity 3.3 (National Protected Area Management).

It is the understanding of the independent TAP that activities under Component 1 are back on track after some delays in the implementation progress during components 2 and 3 which will produce results in GHG mitigation, in increasing of local populations' livelihoods and economic benefits from REDD+ projects. The TAP also notes that while there is a delay in requesting finance for Project 2, some tangible results should already be achieved in the implementation stage minimum by July 2021.

During the call, AE clarifies that the main delay in progress of results across components (2 and 3) was due to the COVID-19 pandemic. The TAP also notes that delays are related to these components, which were blocked due to COVID-19 shutdowns. The independent TAP fully shares this argument but has expressed concerns that the risks reported in the APR still need to be mitigated. It is noted that while progress on some activities has been made, engagement of local key players still requires strengthening; operational structures remain low barriers for

#### Government of La PDR, which is mainly through staff time contributions, has been overestimated during the programme design phase. #### In general, the independent TAP's opinion is that the project is well constructed, very important for La PDR, which has committed to an ambitious target to increase forest cover by 70 per cent of land area but has a lot of barriers to successful implementation. The project should be implemented in phases and should include a revised risk management plan (as condition) developed through preliminary risk analysis rather than managed as a single phase project.

#### The revised feasibility study (Anna 2a) infers that "the concept note was foreseen that a local financial institution would manage the matching grants based on performance criteria. This is not possible in the current context due to the absence of a NAMA Facility". Financial institutions had been considered as an adequate conduit for channeling funds from the GCF to La PDR, but this option has now been abandoned as well as due to the initial intention of supporting the development of a green credit line under the project. Five local banks were consulted during preparation of the feasibility study. Results show that they are not interested in providing loans to small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) or microfinance institutions, even with guarantees from La PDR. They expressed concerns about the associated monitoring and reporting needs, as well as an inability to provide sufficient credit for SMEs. The banks also indicated that their operational capacity is insufficient. Hence, EPF was chosen as the fund to channel the matching grant for SMEs. EPF has a proven track record in managing microfinance institutions and social risk management standards; managing the matching grants for agri-SMEs will help EPF develop its own climate finance projects. The project document should include EPF's GCF accreditation process and develop its own climate finance projects and programmes; EPF is already involved with the World Bank in managing the matching grants under Project 1, and the World Bank also explored the possibility of channeling microfinance institution grants to private sector companies.

#### The independent TAP highlights the significance of EPF at national level and very much welcomes strengthening its capacity as a potential DAE. However, it is the opinion of the independent TAP that involving local financial institutions in the processes involved in strengthening their internal capacities through participation in the project activities greatly diminishes the expected efficiency and effectiveness of the funding proposal results. Without strengthening EPF's capacity, the project will not be able to achieve its intended goals and efficient implementation of GFS 2030 and the NDC, as well as the expected outcomes of the programme.

#### According to the Project 2 lending proposal document, emission reductions expected from the project during the four-year implementation period equate 4.6 million tCO2e (1.14 million tCO2e for GEF mitigation costs for GCF in Project 2 alone equal EBR $7.15 m tCO2e and for the entire programme, these are respectively EUR 12.9 m for tCO2e and EUR 10.6 m for CO2eq). Costs of avoided deforestation (CfAD) are estimated at EUR 34.8 m. The reductions of emitted emissions considered are only for the project implementation phase.

# Introduction

Lao PDR is a landlocked, Least Developed Country (LLDC) with dwindling forest cover, from 70% in the 1960s to 58% in 2015. Forests are not only an important sector for Laos' national economy, but also central to the income, nutrition, and livelihoods of its people. Especially the poor rural population is strongly dependent on the natural resources provided by forests. The loss of forest cover poses a significant risk to the livelihoods of a majority of the Laotian population. Women, the poor, and geographically remote communities are particularly vulnerable.

The core national strategy (9th Socio-Economic Development Plan, Central Party’s Resolution on Land, Forestry Strategy 2015-2030, National Forest Policy 2016) and the Lao PDR Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) acknowledge the importance of Laos' forest resources and their sustainable man- agement. The Government has also adopted a national climate change adaptation strategy (2021) to strengthen linkages between climate change mitigation and action within the agriculture and forestry sectors, as well as with other relevant sectors such as energy, water, transport, and urban development policies and emphasize the importance of all stakeholders, including households, communities, Government and private sector actors, as a major contributor to reducing deforestation and degradation, and to promote forest restoration and reforestation.

## Overview of the overarching programme

This project is embedded in the overarching programme 'Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable Forest landscape management' (Funding Proposal FIP 171, Project Implementation Unit: Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment).

The objectives of the overarching programme is to support the Government and people of Lao PDR transition to sustainable forest landscape management. The project will reduce greenhouse gas emissions by approximately 11.7 million tCO2e and directly increase the resilience of more than 375,000 villagers over the 7-year implement- ation period (2021–2028). This is in line with the Government’s commitment to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Lao PDR Emissions Reduction Programme (ER Programme) under the FIP for the aforementioned six provinces covered by the ER Programme.

Initially, FIP 171 was conceptualized as a pure mitigation single project which had to be reformed as a programme with 5 Sub-Projects due to the limited scope of the original Project. The Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme (ER Programme) is divided into Project 1 covering 3 out of 6 provinces in the Laotian ER Programme (Louang Namtha, Phongsaly and Bokeo), and the subsequent Sub-Projects 2 to 5 and 3 separate Project 2 to expand the coverage of the ER Programme. The project will support the Government’s efforts to address the complex transformational change of forest and land management in the uplands of the Lao PDR.

Project 1 (ER Programme) is a multi-provincial programme that aims at strengthening supporting policy mainstreaming, strengthening the regulatory framework, implementing and improving the MRV system, among others. The project will be implemented in six provinces: Louang Namtha, Phongsaly, Bokeo, Luang Prabang, Luang Prabang (and Sayaboury), and Luang Xayaburi. However, to fully reach the paradigm shift towards low emission and climate resilient forest and land use management, it is necessary to implement a separate project in the remaining three provinces for Lao PDR transition to sustainable and climate resilient management of forests and landscapes at scale.

Project 2 is conceptualized as follows:

The programme of Project 1 (Project 2021-2026) will address the three provinces of Phonsavan, Sayaboury and Luang Prabang, which contain the highest rates of deforestation and forest degradation within the programme areas. Project 2021-2024 will cover the first four years of implementation (2021–2024) with a focus on policy mainstreaming, regulatory framework strengthening, and MRV system improvement. The remaining two years of project implementation (2025–2026) will be dedicated to the geographical reach of the programme in the additional provinces of Sayaboury, Bokeo and Luang Namtha (FIP 171, page 3).

The specific objectives for Project 2 are as follows:

The technical components of this project are divided into two main groups: Component A and Component B. The former is a stand-alone MRV project (Project 1) to be approved. Two sub-components arise from projects embedded in the same programme context and theme of change in the Lao PDR Emission Reduction Programme (ER Programme). These are:

• **Component A:** This component will be implemented under Project 2021-2026 (FIP 171, page 4).

• **Component B:** This component is a stand-alone MRV project (Project 1) to be approved. Under Project 2021-2026 (FIP 171, page 5).


---
**Page 177**
---

* Component 1 addresses barriers at the national and sub-national levels, including measures that aim to scale-up climate-informed participatory land use planning, strengthen land tenure security, improve forest law enforcement and monitoring, and scale-up and ensure access to sustainable financing for the AFU/REU sector.
* Component 2 builds on the enabling environment (Component 1), and addresses key drivers of deforestation and forest degradation. It aims at improving the livelihoods of local communities by supporting the expansion of agricultural activities into forested landscapes, and promotes climate resilient agricultural practices. The component will also support the development of a national framework for REDD+.
* Component 3 builds on the enabling environment (Component 1**), and will reduce emissions and strengthen resilience to climate change in priority provinces through the implementation of landscape restoration projects and the promotion of Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR), with a focus on village and conservation forests.

## Figure 1. Overview of project components and activities

Project 2 is complementary and additional to Project 1. Together both projects will facilitate a paradigm shift in the forestry and land use sector in Lao PDR that will be sustained by unlocking additional sources of results-based payments, improved governance, and enhanced capacity for climate change adaptation and mitigation. The projects aim at strengthening capacities for mobilizing and channeling climate finance. Project 2 has been revised and re-designed as a cross-cutting component to support the implementation of the Lao PDR National Strategy on the Conservation of Ecosystems and Local Rural Livelihoods, in full alignment with the Paris Latest Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the country's draft Climate Change Work Plan (CCWP). It is expected that the projects will contribute to the achievement of climate change and forestry practices across six provinces, building on the strengthened regulatory framework utilising the tools and guidelines developed under Project 1.

With approximately 273,700 direct beneficiaries (136,850 women and 136,850 men) from at least 23 different ethnic groups, and an additional 723,732 indirect beneficiaries (161,691 women and 361,691 men), the ER Project will be implemented in Laos. Project 2 will be overseen by GIZ’s Headquarters in its role as GCF Accredited Entity, and implemented by GIZ Lao PDR with co-financing from the Global Environment Facility Fund (GEF). It includes co-financing from the government of Laos POR, BMG, ADB, IFAD, World Bank and KfW.

### Overview of Gender Equality

This document comprises the Gender Assessment for Project 2 in order to guarantee a gender-sensitive approach and implementation of Project 2 and meet the standard requirements of the GIZ and GCF. This gender assessment is part of the overall project design process and will be conducted as an integral component of the Lao PDR Emission Reduction Program through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management (Project 2).

Although by no means exhaustive, this gender analysis attempts to provide both a general and sectoral overview on the state of gender equality in Laos, covering direct and indirect factors which can impact the implementation of the program.

## GCF and GIZ Guidelines for the Promotion of Gender Equality

According to the GCF’s Gender Policy, proposed projects or programmes submitted to the Fund are required to be aligned with national policies and priorities on gender and with the Fund’s gender policy². This includes a mandatory gender analysis as part of the project design process in order to identify risks and mitigation safeguards (350 process).

The results of this assessment is therefore to determine how the project can respond to the needs of women and men in view of the addressed forest degradation and deforestation, and the proposed measures.

Gender dynamics and related drivers of change will be identified to achieve the project goals in a sustainable manner and will be reflected in the proposed activities. The assessments are required to include stakeholder consultations.

Additionally, implementation budgets will be provided alongside realistic indicators at output, outcome and impact levels.

In line with the above-mentioned GCF Gender Policy, GIZ’s Safeguards and Gender Management System and Gender Strategy³ require that a gender analysis is conducted at an early stage of the preparation phase of a project in order to identify risks and mitigation safeguards. The results will be integrated into the project and mitigated through specific measures.

The results presented in this document are directly taken into account for the objective, indicators, the methodological approach and the results monitoring system of the project.

The assessment will conclude with gender-responsive strategies and measures with tangible benefits to women, and a commitment by GIZ to ensure that the project contributes effectively to maximizing benefits and women’s empowerment in the forest and landscapes sector in the accounting area.


## Methodology

This gender analysis is an update of the gender analysis conducted for the GCF Programme “Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Program through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management”². The methodology used to conduct this assessment is based on the GIZ Gender Assessment Framework, which consists of four main steps:

1. **Contextual Analysis**: This step involves a review of existing gender-related policies, laws, regulations, and institutional frameworks in Laos.
2. **Gender-Related Data Collection**: This includes gathering data from various sources such as government statistics, research studies, and interviews with key stakeholders to understand the current state of gender equality in Laos.
3. **Analysis of Gender Dynamics**: This step involves analyzing how gender dynamics affect forest degradation and deforestation in Laos, including examining the roles and responsibilities of women and men in these areas.
4. **Gender-Sensitive Project Design**: This final step focuses on designing a project that is sensitive to gender issues and takes into account the needs and perspectives of both women and men.

The updated Gender Assessment has been elaborated in three phases:

1. A desk review of the original gender analysis and adjustment to changes in relevant national policies, legal frameworks, and other developments impacting women's rights in the sector.

Feedback from the consultations, including comments and recommendations from both consultation rounds (Phases 1 & 2) are included throughout this document. The findings and observations are crucial to the planning, implementation and monitoring of the planned project. In actively integrating gender-sensitive and gender-responsive approaches into the project design, it is possible not only to challenge the perpetuation of traditional gender stereotypes, but can also contribute significantly to improving gender equality and equity in the target areas. This will largely add to the positive impact the project can have.

2. Information on gender dimensions in Lao PDR

Lao PDR currently ranks 113th* on the Gender Inequality Index (Gini: 0.57) or general Human Development Index, as of 2019). Major negative contributors to this ranking are the maternal mortality ratio, the adolescent birth rate and the low female proportion of people with at least some secondary education. Positively influencing contributions are the proportionally high share of female parliamentarians and the high female labor force participation rate.

### 2.1 The Meta Level: Norms and Traditional Roles of Women and Men in Laos

Despite a strong legal framework stating and promoting the equality of Laotian women and men, the influence of gender norms and traditional roles is still one as an major obstacle in achieving factual gender equality in Laos. This is especially true at the village level where women continue to struggle to participate on equal terms and in equal numbers.

Gender equality is additionally influenced by ethnic background in Laos. The Lao-Tai group represents 67% of Laos PDR's population, followed by the Tai Dam (12%), the Hmong-Mien (8%) and the Chinese-Tibetan (3%). These groups further split into 49 distinct ethnicities.

Many traditional norms within both Tai-Lao cultures are favorable with regard to gender equality: women are often featured in stories as strong, independent characters. However, this is not true for all ethnic groups. The other three ethnic linguistic groups mostly have stronger patriarchal traditions and norms, limiting women's access to credit, decision-making power, property rights and inheritance.

Violence against women is also a reality, resulting for women from all ethnic backgrounds in Laos. Research indicates that around 20% of Laotian women have been physically and/or sexually abused by a partner or non-partner, and almost half (49%) of women report having experienced some form of sexual violence at least once since cases are unreported. Perhaps more jarring though, the majority of women and almost half of all men in Laos believe it is acceptable for a husband to beat his wife if she does not obey him, or if he believes her to be leaving the house without permission or 'burning the food'. This indicates that problems are both in enforcement and broader issues of traditional culture that cannot be fixed in the short-term. Village Maternal Units are an effective way to address these issues at a community level by providing education on the importance of village unity and the reinvigorated status of "free village life" (no reference to district courts). Traditional gender norms derive from the patriarchal structure of Lao society, which is based on patrilineal descent. Women do not have the same rights as men in terms of inheritance or property ownership. They also do not have access into the village structure due to illiteracy, lack of language skills and legal knowledge, and lack of male influence.

Lao PDR has one of the highest rates of early marriage in the region as part of traditional practices, including "bride price" (a payment made by a groom's family to his bride's family) and dowry. The average age at first marriage is 19 years old. Early marriage is often associated with early pregnancy. In 2012, 19.4 percent of reproductive-age women had their first child before the age of 18. This has a negative impact on education and livelihood opportunities of women.

The impact of these factors on a programme cannot be underestimated. It affects women's participation, their confidence in decision-making, and their ability to perform household management tasks or income-generating management tasks. Many rural women themselves also think that women are generally not capable of decision-making (or at least not as capable as men). This is reflected by the fact that women were not mentioned or expressed in two out of three village interviews during the assessment mission. The third village had higher income rates for the women and a culture of women being part of decision-making. The latter is more decisive factor.

### Implications for the planned GCF project

In addition to avoiding the continuation of existing gender stereotypes and norms which are of disadvantage to Lesotho women, the proposed GCF project has a significant potential to contribute positively to gender equality. In- clusive awareness-raising measures targeting women as carriers of traditional knowledge and change agents within their communities will be an important element in this regard. These measures should also target men with lower education levels.

Since one of the GCF project's central approaches will be to promote more sustainable production methods and value chains, and therefore new alternative and more diversified income streams for communities, it is im- portant to consider that women are often responsible for food security in their households. This can lead to serious problems increase the likelihood of domestic violence, especially in families where women have lower educa- tion levels or limited access to information on their rights. If women are empowered and given more income gen- erous, or communities are supported to change traditional ways of living and earning, it can be assumed that women or girls will be less likely to experience violence within the household. This is because an improvement of their situation but can also bear certain risks that need to be considered. As another instance, a study on public work programme in Laao PDR found that "The program was successful in increasing female income, however, it also increased domestic violence against women". The GCF project could therefore contribute to a prevention of violence against women through the formulation of gender-sensitive and context-specific action plans for each community. These should include measures for empow- erment, and men's change of attitudes towards gender equality. The projects need to carefully consider local con- texts, however, as the study in Laao PDR also found that "The program was successful in increasing female income, however, it also increased domestic violence against women".

Under Project 2, coping strategies to address the risk of gender-based violence could include:

* **Maintenance of safe spaces for women and girls.**
    * Provide women with access to information to become aware of their legal rights under national and inter- national law.
    * Engage men and boys to promote non-violent and gender equity. This may include community-based train- ing programmes, awareness raising campaigns or other activities that engage the entire family, even at an age, including gatekeepers.
* **Promote equality and challenge traditional gender norms in the context of participatory activities promoted by the project.**
    * Develop a Gender Action Plan on Gender and countering Gender-based violence (GBV)
    * Elaboration of Code of Conduct for the implementation of project activities
    * Integrated awareness raising projects programmes and the grievance redress mechanism.
* Whenever possible, avoid the use of meetings focusing only on women or girls. A balanced meeting or discussion meeting should proactively invite components of empowerment for village women. This includes separate meetings between men and women and village youth groups to create safe spaces for everybody's views, trainings for community-based staff including gender focal points, staff who are responsible for the project implementation and design any concrete activities with the specific social conditions of a village in mind.

The project’s Grievance redress Mechanism (GRM) has been designed to be gender-sensitive. Access to men and women. The GRM is expected to receive 10% of all complaints from women and girls, while only 2% from men. This approximately half of the village population is participating (out of which 44% are females). Further measures have been taken to ensure that the GRM is gender-sensitive by ensuring that it is staffed with a mix of both genders. The project has also designed different GRM mechanisms and approaches to address language barriers, as mostly women and ethnic groups that do not speak English or Tswana are expected to use the GRM. See the description of the program's GRM in the EISA Chapter 7.5 for more information on the GRM mechanism.

# 2.2 Macro Level: International and Regional Commitments & National Legislation and Policies

At an international level, Laos is a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and the ASEAN Comprehensive Implementation Plan for CEDAW implementation in law conducted by the Association for Development of Women and Legal Education (ADWLE) concluded that, despite having a very advanced legal framework, women are still not fully protected under the law. This is due to lack of enforcement, lack of human resources capacities, knowledge and budget[1] – basically, the exact same situation as in the forestry sector, with strategic frameworks and policies in place but no implementation.

Believing that the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) are having a strong overall influence on law strategies and policies, including SDG 5 which is aiming to achieve gender equality and empower women and girls. The emphasis of the ASEAN region is on economic empowerment and participation of women in the economy as a key factor for achieving the Sustainable Development Goals. Even though the original goal to graduate in 2020 cannot be reached anymore, the Government is determined to further push towards it. Efforts to promote women’s economic integration and opportunities are reflected in the ASEAN Women’s Declaration on Economic Empowerment of Women.

At the regional level, several ASEAN declarations for the foundation for gender mainstreaming in the region. The Declaration of the Advancement of Women in the ASEAN Region[2] was signed in 1984 and focuses mainly on the promotion of women's rights and equality. The ASEAN Human Rights Declaration (AHRD) adopted in 1993, though the declaration mentions the political, social and cultural sphere, the emphasis is on women’s economic participation.

The social dimension was added consecutively in later declarations, namely with the ASEAN Declaration Against Trafficking in Persons, especially Women and Children[3] (2005), the ASEAN Declaration on the Elimination of Violence against Women and Children[4] (2010), the ASEAN Human Rights Declaration, the Declaration on Gender Mainstreaming in the ASEAN Community[5] (2011) and the ASEAN Declaration on Gender Responsive Implementation of the ASEAN Community Vision 2025 and Sustainable Development Goals in 2013. A crucial element is that these declarations are not only a statement of principles, but also include specific goals, action plans, and policy review, empowers the necessity to end violence against women (including making men and boys responsible for their actions) and encourages cooperation with women’s groups and organizations for improved gender-responsive implementation[6].

Lao PDR has a strong legal framework for promoting gender equality. The revised Constitution of 2003 and other laws explicitly state that women have the same rights as men in all spheres, including political, social, and the family. Women's rights are also stipulated in the Family and Land Law[7], the Labour Law[8], the Civil and Commercial Code[9] and the Penal Law[10]. The Law on the Development and Protection of Women[11] is the most specific law applicable to women’s rights, which was enacted in 2004. This law defines women's rights, and served also as a basis for the National Commission for the Advancement of Women (NCAW).

Some sources claim that women’s land tenure rights have been weakened by the revised Land Law[12] as a paragraph on old names (baeuf and luud) on lands has been removed from the law. The NCAC, by law women and their families are entitled to inherit land in Laos. However, there is no specific provision for communal or village ownership of land is recognized by the Land Law[13] and a common form of land ownership is the family farm. In some areas, especially in the north (the Lao language group) and the south (the Khmer language group), inheritance customs follow the female lineage. Daughters and sons have equal rights to inherit their parents' property. However, this law does not apply equally among ethnic minority groups, such as the Hmong-Isan and the Khmuu (Mon-Khmer), the pattern of agricultural

### Implications for the planned GCF Programme

The legal foundation for the promotion of gender mainstreaming into policies and regulations is strong, and has been further facilitated through targeted actions under Project 1. Lagos has an elevated interest in adhering to regional entry points, and the national framework to integrate gender into policies and strategies at all levels is broad and comprehensive. The National Commission for the Advancement of Women (NCAW) has a mandate on gender in sector-specific legislation, including in the forestry sector: as mentioned also by interviewee Government officials, it is understood that this commission’s legislative remit is sufficient to inform the interpretation of sector-specific laws such as the forestry Law.

Project 2 can promote gender equality in its efforts on law enforcement and capacity development. Guidelines developed by the project for adoption mainstreaming, land use planning, agricultural investment planning and village forest management are designed to ensure that women have access to information and resources to empower and benefit women, in addition to ensuring gender-responsive monitoring. In adherence with the CAFI guidelines, the NCAW has been consulted on these guidelines. Other organizations such as ADEWELE can assist with gender-proofing planned intervention in this area. To develop capacity of the national forest service, it is recommended that the National Commission for Women and Child Development should also be involved in these processes as much as possible (more under 5.3).

## Meso Level: Institutions and Non-Governmental Organizations

The National Commission for the Advancement of Women and Mother-Child (NCAWM) is responsible for formulating and implementing national policy for the advancement of women, as well as for mainstreaming gender issues in all sectors. NCAWM has a mandate to provide technical assistance and financial support for capacity development.

The NCAWM is also responsible for formulating the National Strategy for the Advancement of Women (NSAW), which is to promote women’s empowerment and seeks to promote and enhance equality between men and women in all spheres. The inclusion of the strategy's goals in other strategies and plans, including the National Socio-Economic Development Plan (NSED) and the National Forest Policy, is a key element of the NCAWM's mandate.

The lead government agency in the forest sector, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF), was the first ministry to create a Division for the Advancement of Women ("Sub-CAR"), and a minority internal Gender Unit with work focal points in each department. The MAF has been involved in promoting gender development significantly between 2006-2008. The final report of this programme (\"Capacity Building for Gender Mainstreaming in Agriculture and Forestry in Lafo Pori\") was published by the MAF in 2010. However, since the publication of this report, the role of gender within the sector has diminished significantly. As a result, the gender division remains understaffed and therefore with low absorption capacity.\" Interviews with two Pro- ence and one Gender Officer at the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF) in 2013 found that there was a Gender focal Point (GFP) appointed in the office, the concerned staff member was not involved in the initiatives because

# Implications for the planned GCF project

Based on the existing institutional structures and strategies, the planned GCF project has strong leverage to link up with other initiatives in order to promote gender equality and women's empowerment (GEWE) throughout all stakeholder processes from the national to the local level. The political commitment seems currently particularly high at the national level based on the approach should be two-fold: utilizing the existing gender structures (GPA, MAF, Sub-CAR, etc.) as a basis for GCF project implementation and strengthening these structures to foster a Government culture where gender is increasingly mainstreamed.

Gender equality and women's empowerment are not new concepts in the Lao PDR. However, few Government staff possess in-depth knowledge on gender and GEWE at this same time. The efforts which have already been put into mainstreaming gender into national development planning (NDP) and sectoral plans (e.g., the National Strategic Plan for GCF project can support the RDE to compile these to practical plans and to disseminate them to other line agen- tis, departments, and local governments. The Government has already made significant progress in this area. However, demonstrated high commitment towards supporting the planned GCF project but admitted that they need more time to mainstream gender into their work.

A technical training for all concerned Provincial District Units (UDK), F/DMA, F/DDH (Forest Inspector) on gender and social inclusion is recommended. A capacity building programme in gender equality and women's management is recommended to mainstream efforts and align the local implementation and decision-makers.

Project 1 involved the LUBU as a key actor in its FRIC process and other participatory activities at village levels. The cooperation between the LUBU and the F/DMA was very good, and the LUBU staff was trained on participatory approaches such as the establishment of Village Forest and Agriculture Grant (VIFAG) committees and village forest managers.

Project 2 has demonstrated that with the knowledge from Project 1 and other initiatives is utilized. Under project 1 implementation, gender aspects have been integrated into Standard Operating Procedures, guidelines manuals and training for field staff and local communities.

The activities will be continued under Project 2. Training and mentoring guidelines alone will likely not be sufficient to ensure effective implementation. High-level, regular meetings, such as programme steering committee meetings, should make it a requirement to report on gender aspects and related implementation efforts.


## 2.4. Micro Level: Gender Equality among the Target Group

This section briefly examines the gender equality situation in the areas of political participation, decision-making and leadership, education and economic participation.

### Political Participation: Decision-making and leadership

With 21 female Members of Parliament, Laos is well above average (13%). However, women in de- cision-making positions in the district, provincial and national Government agencies constitute only 5% (as at 2012). The proportion of women in administrative support roles is even lower. At the local level (at the provincial or national level) more than a quarter), the lowest proportion of women by administrative support roles can be found at the Provincial and District level. This is likely to be the fact explained on the sections on education and health.

Within the potential partners of the planned GCF project, representation seems to vary between different min- istry and departments, but generally the project also faces a lower representation of female leadership throughout all levels of Government. Women are underrepresented in the private sector as well, especially in col- lecting non-timber forest products (NTFPs) and making up a significant proportion of the small business work- force in the rural areas.

The interviewed village women and men noted that women spend usually more time in the forests and have a more detailed knowledge about the resources. However, they also pointed out that women are often excluded from the decision-making processes within their communities.


---
**Page 186**
---

# Education

The gender equity gap has narrowed at all three levels of education enrollment in Laos, but challenges persist in completing secondary school.

Two key determinants drive the patterns of gender inequity in education: First, girls are more likely to be kept at home due to safety concerns and household responsibilities, especially if the secondary school is far from home. Second, parents often do not send their daughters to school because they believe that girls should stay at home to help with domestic chores or care for younger siblings. Girls who live in rural areas without road access are more likely to be kept out of school.

The widest gap in gender equity is found among children from rural areas without road access, children from the poor quintiles, and children whose parents have little or no education. This gap is much larger in secondary education: for example, in the appropriate age group of the poorest quintiles, 64% more girls than boys are out of school.

The majority of interviewed village women had no or very low education. This lack of education was named by men and women alike as the main reason for the absence of women in village decision-making committees and groups in general, and particularly with regard to the village forestry.

# Economic participation

An equal share of men and women make up the working population (75% each, as of 2015), but women generally occupy lower-paying jobs than their male counterparts. Women are relatively more excluded from formal labor markets and the social protection that this entails. Some 64 percent of women work in the elementary occupations and 63 percent of those who do not have a job or business, compared with only 29% for men.

For the majority of civil servants, professionals, technicians and other sectors: the gender gap is even more pronounced. Women are underrepresented at all levels of government and in public service. The Lao government has already set a 30% goal of having women hold positions in the conservation sector, but implementation lags behind.

Although women have significant roles in agriculture and forestry, they have less access to, and control of, farming and forestry-based inputs and outputs. The village interviews showed the same tendency: the villages where women all stated that the key decisions on land and forest are usually taken by the husbands and the village authorities.

Experience from other sectors, such as fisheries, shows that women's multiple roles in traditional, complex and lengthy value chains often result in their exclusion from decision-making. This may also hold true for the tourism sector, but the lack of data does not allow a clearer picture.

Women who collect wild tree products tend to be restricted to products that are not profitable or have little commercial benefits.

# Gender, wages and financial resources

The share of women in wage employment is low in attractors, at 35 percent instead, among the unpaid workers for the family, women are more likely to be involved (61%). This is also reflected in the demographic data of CCF, which suggests that women are less likely engaged in productive work with income they control. This reality was mirrored in the interviewed villages: women and men agreed that women work longer hours in a day while men do so part-time. Women are more likely to be involved in subsistence farming, animal husbandry, and fishing and have more time available for management-related tasks. In the visited Ahlu village in Luang Namtha, the village authorities reported that women were more involved than men in decision-making on household matters and community decision-making. Several women stated that they would strongly like to be involved in the management of forest resources.

Gender wage gaps are present, and women work longer work hours than men, as they spend 7 days per day on productive and reproductive tasks (men: 5.7 hours/). Women’s GED and physical vulnerability also limit their access to income-generating activities. In the interviewed villages, it is mainly hindered women from participating in forest management tasks, including decision-making.

Women can face discrimination in the market when marketing their produce directly, and also be stymied by lack of financial resources.

Especially in the rural areas, women’s lower income often also directly translates into a lower decision-making power in the family; the lower their income, the lessen their voice. Women with higher income possess higher economic power within the household and are more likely to have access to land. The challenges from income disparities are therefore particularly relevant in combination with certain cultural gender norms. This does not only affect women’s empowerment but also their ability to participate in new income activities are promoted, or where regulations affect family businesses or access to forest resources.

Microfinance has been shown to empower communities if the villagers are not amongst the poorest. In that case, microfinance is often seen as too risky. Women are often more hesitant to take on debts to protect their families from poverty. The Lao Women’s Union (LWU) and the Community Development Fund (CDF) Village Development Funds are often managed by women, and many ethnic groups; women are responsible for the management of these funds in the villages. In addition, the LWU has been involved with microfinance through grants as planned by the GCF project would create more opportunities for the local communities to improve their socio-economic conditions.

The Lao Women’s Union (LWU) is a key partner in the GCF project and provides financial and technical rural practices. Semi-formal and informal microfinance, such as provided by the Lao Women’s Union, is an important source for women in the rural areas of use PDR.


## Implications for the planned GCF project

The planned GCF project will have great leverage to advocate for improving women's representation in forest management and decision-making. At the national level, the political willingness to increase the number of female Government staff will contribute significantly to this goal. The GCF project can also help address some of the underlying reasons for the lack of women in sector roles. A gender-profiler of Human Resources Procedures and Interviewing Techniques is being developed by the LWU. This will be used as a tool to train staff on a sector-based mentoring system to motivate superiors to promote younger colleagues, and to build the capacity of women to take up leadership positions.

At the Provincial and District level, the same applies; one but additional barrier hinder fields from implementing this is the lack of female field officers. This is more relevant knowing that women living in remote villages. This is even more relevant knowing that women living in remote villages are often the ones who work in the forest and have a better understanding of the local conditions. The other major staff barrier to training is usually safety concerns and family considerations. The GCF programme could investigate possible support measures in addition to building female staff confidence in technical areas through mentoring, coaching and capacity-building.
