3. Gender dimensions in the sector

The following chapter provides an overview of relevant gender strategies in the forestry sector at international, regional and national level.

### 3.1 Meta Level: Gender gaps in the sector

#### Gender and Agriculture

Gender-related roles and expectations of ethnic women and girls make their lives difficult with long working hours to be done by them alone. Women do most of the farm work (planting, weeding and harvesting crops), tend small livestock and collect NTFPs (men occasionally hunt wild animals in some villages). Hard work is associated with seasonal variations in agricultural activities and weather conditions.

Women spend around four times more time than men on household and caring work leading to significant time constraints for women. For example, women are responsible as well for household chores, community meetings or other local decision-making bodies and long-term economic planning. Gender inequality between male- and female-headed households in the agricultural sector is evidenced by the agricultural land they used, both of the use of fertilizers and pesticides. Women-headed households have less access to credit facilities than men. They also have less labour and productive assets available to them while having a less diversified crop base than men.

Due to traditional expectations, women are often less mobile than men. The social expectation is that women must adhere to their homes and families. This makes it difficult for women to participate in training or go out to work or trade. In addition, women's mobility is limited by household and family responsibilities which does not allow them to travel far from home. Women's participation in the agricultural sector has been hampered as economic development and regional trade increased cross-border exchange and industrial development in other centers of the country. This has led to a decline in the number of jobs available for women, especially for men but also for women. Yet, despite in remote rural areas, traditional norms concerning the role of women in agriculture are still strong.

The increasing use of chemical fertilizers and pesticides in agriculture has had significant effects on women's health impacts on their involvement in economic activities and training activities which needs to be considered in gender equality strategies.

Current trends in agriculture have seen rotational periods shorten, while promoting more intensive production systems or cash crops that accelerate soil degradation. When shifting agriculture is reduced to only a three-year rotation, the land becomes exhausted after two years of continuous cultivation with maize and then left fallow with weeding. If female labor is not enough to keep up with weed pressure, the next step is often herbicide use. The cost of herbicides is high and women are unable to afford them. This has led to a situation where women have a slightly higher use than men. One reason for this might be that pesticides and chemical fertilizer reduces the workload on women by decreasing the time needed for tasks traditionally performed by women, while increasing the workload for men. Women also face more health risks from the negative effects of pesticides and chemicals, as women are frequently the ones spreading them in fields, often with their children at their side.

While several projects and initiatives aim to provide support to local villagers, often approaches applied are not gender-sensitive or do not consider the needs of women. In some cases, projects have been designed to target male farmers who are considered the main decision-makers. Generally, women are less reached by extension and training activities than men. Women's access to credit facilities is also limited as they are burdened with household and family chores, receive information through their husbands and have in many cases lower levels of education. In addition, women's participation in agricultural research has been low due to women's involvement in agriculture is often undervalued by agricultural investments and public services. The Project will need to ensure that value chains are targeted which involve the participation of women and empowers their roles and responsibilities in the respective value chain. Gender and Forests

Often the poorer a family is, the more likely they are dependent on forest resources. Therefore, it can be assumed that woman-headed households tend to have a higher dependency on forest resources than male-headed households. According to the 2015 National Household Survey (NHS), in rural areas of Laos PDR, women collect firewood and charcoal, and children collect non-timber products from the surrounding forests for food and some income. The forest is an important source of livelihoods for many poor families. In 2014, the average annual household expenditure was at an equivalent of 48% of the average rural household cash income. Overall poverty and lack of knowledge on sustainable harvesting methods resulted in decreased forest productivity and greater demands for labor, and time spent gathering firewood and other non-timber products from forests increased. This has led to a decline in NTFP to maintain their livelihoods.

NTFPs play an important role in providing the livelihood of rural population and household economies. About 70% of households living below poverty line in rural areas, are dependent on upland farming and forest products for their food, cash income, and livelihood. They use NTFPs for their daily subsistence. They also play an important role in a household’s economic activities as well as in the family’s nutrition security.

Women's active roles in harvesting and using, but they often do not have direct control of the income derived from commercialization of these resources. This is because women are more likely to be excluded from decision-making at all levels of forest resource management. Women are also more vulnerable than men to poverty and other forms of discrimination such as violence against women. The situation for women in rural areas is even worse, especially when they are women who are being displaced by men when new labour-saving technologies for NTFP processing are introduced.

Approximately 10% of land households are led by women with a strong concentration in urban areas. It is suggested that less than 5% of households in rural areas are headed by women. In the project area the share of women-headed households is even lower, at approximately 2%. Women-headed households in rural areas are more likely to be poor and landless, and they have limited access to credit and other financial services.

Women as entrepreneurs in agriculture and forestry

The private sector is creating new opportunities for entrepreneurs in Laos PDR, with at least 40 percent of all enterprises owned by women. However, the majority of these women are not owners but employees. At current prices, the majority are women-owned however, women-owned enterprises are usually smaller (mostly of micro-enterprises) and have lower productivity than men-owned enterprises. Women entrepreneurs often play a role as primary caregiver locate their business close to their homes. Among the 19 interviewed entrepreneurs in preparation of Project 2.0, six female owners (some of which were also employed with the household).

## Gender and Climate Change Adaptation

Disaster management has worked as a critical entry point for challenges related to environmental sustainability with a high degree of acceptance. The environmental burden of disease due to climate change already constitutes 26 percent of the disease burden in Low PDRs, women and men experience the impacts of floods and droughts differently. Women are more likely than men to be affected by natural disasters because they often bear the brunt of their employment, they are more likely to be the brunt of the impacts of floods and droughts.

Different studies show that women are disproportionately impacted by climate-induced natural disasters, which is in many cases due to gender-based discrimination. Women's vulnerability to natural disasters is often higher than those of men. According to Thurston et al., this is often rooted "in biological differences, greater domestic responsibilities, lack of access to resources and information, and the intersectionality of race, ethnicity, and gendered poverty". Natural disasters at different scale create risk factors that, in turn, increase the risk of gender-based violence (GBV). Women are more likely than men to be affected by natural disasters because they often bear the brunt of their employment, they are more likely to be the brunt of the impacts of floods and droughts.


---
**Page 193**
---

Women are often more affected by the negative impact of climate change. For instance, families may decide to take girls out of school to increase the household’s resources, while boys are more likely to be kept in school during crises. Women also bear a disproportionate burden when it comes to caring for their children and ensuring their safety; in rural areas, ecosystems degrade from climate change, which may increase household burdens on women as they have to spend more time collecting firewood and water.

Lao women play key roles in both climate change mitigation and adaptation strategies, but these are often insufficiently recognized. Women’s contributions can be undermined by gender inequalities that limit their access to resources or benefit men and women unequally if gender considerations are not fully considered and main- tained throughout the project cycle. This is particularly true for women who have limited access to land, which is a key determinant of their responsibilities in their everyday life, leaving women with less time and resources. Adaptation to climate change is often hindered by social norms such as wealth, technological power, access to information, all of which are me- diated by gender dynamics. Women’s roles within the household and their traditional responsibilities for managing the household and stewardship of natural resources, also position them well to develop strategies for adapting to changing conditions. However, women often lack the means or capacity to implement these strategies, such as access to credit or loans for household consumption (benefiting both family health and the environment) and in main- taining their traditional roles in the protection of biodiversity – particularly in highland upland areas and in national protected areas.

### Implications for the planned GCF project

As the analysis showed, women are among the key knowledge carriers regarding the status of community forests and its resources. Since the programme plans to support the development of a National Forest Inventory and Monitoring System (NFIMS) and the establishment of a forest management system, it is important that women receive training and technical support. The interviewed women and men, and local Government partners, all agreed that such measure would be valuable for the project.

Further, research could be commissioned by the planned GCF project to identify and highlight what oppor- tunities exist to strengthen women’s roles as producers and processors in Agriculture and Forestry, including support for women’s access to credit. The analysis also showed that the gender dimension of climate change should influence any further analysis on one potential value chain promoting deforestation-free agri- culture. The project will need to ensure that women are included in all aspects of the project, from planning work with men and women. As women farmers face significant constraints, the project will ensure that women research conducted under Project 1 is used to inform the development of a gender-sensitive extension services as well as access to information on climate change impacts and sustainable agricultural practices.

Project 1 initiated a number of processes to improve access to finance for rural women, increase the involvement of women in decision-making at all levels of the project, and promote women’s empowerment. Women are given the opportunity to participate in all Project 1 events. The Annual Programmes Report shows that 70% of PSAP participants were women; 65% of women attended all village meetings and in all key areas of interest (e.g. FPR 34%, PFR 40%, PSAP* 47%, VAFD 42%).

Furthermore, female headed households are given priority in the selection of PSAP participants. 75% of PSAP beneficiaries were women-headed households and 81% of women-headed households participated in training on rural land. This proves that the prioritization was implemented in practice. In 80% of villages women are rep- resented as members of the village forest management committee, which is a key role for women’s participation and inclusion in economic activities promoted by Project 1.

These processes were extended to the three new Provinces covered under Project 3 and extended to the three new Provinces covered under Project 2. The strengthened focus on climate resilience will further raise awareness of

### 3.2 Macro Level: Policies and strategies in the sector

#### Agenda 2030, FAO’s Criteria and Indicators

The Agenda 2030 for Sustainable Development, including its seventeen Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), represents a comprehensive framework to achieve global development throughout different sectors, including land use and forest management. The following are some of the criteria and indicators that will provide guidance for the planned programme:

- **Sustainable agriculture and food security (SDG 12):** development of production and consumption that takes into account the limitations of natural resources and empowers local populations, including women, to participate in relevant policy dialogues.
- **Climate action (SDG 13):** women, indigenous peoples and local communities, and other groups have the right to be represented in climate summits as climate change affects them in different ways and they are also responsible for adaptation strategies. Women’s participation is essential in this regard.
- **Conserving forests and biodiversity (SDG 15):** limiting the industrialization of agriculture and forestry in order to conserve natural resources, including women’s rights.

The Women 2030 Program is a coalition of gender network organizations supporting the implementation of the 2030 Agenda. It is implemented in 50 countries all over the world, including Latin America, Asia, Africa, Europe, and North America. The program empowers women to participate as leaders in the development process, especially in relation to forest management. It also strengthens the role of women’s civil society organizations and enables them to participate in relevant policy dialogues and monitoring, with an emphasis on gender equality.

Amongst the agencies of the United Nations, the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) leads the way regarding an integrated approach of gender and forestry. FAO’s Criteria and Indicators for sustainable forest management (SFM) are a set of principles that guide countries in their efforts to achieve SFM. The criteria include the integration of gender aspects for the forest development sector.21 FAO recognizes that, even though women play a very important role in the management and use of forests, especially with regard to the generation of income from forest resources, this is rarely reflected in forestry-related planning and programming.

Another important international framework is provided through the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), which lists gender equality as one of its principles. The CBD also provides guidance for action plans for Biodiversity and provides concrete tools and action steps to integrate gender into related national policies and implementation efforts. Parties to the Convention are requested to report on actions undertaken to implement the Gender Action Plan.

The four strategic objectives of the CBD Gender Plan of Action are:

1. Integration of gender perspectives
2. Promotion of women’s rights
3. Empowerment of women in decision-making processes
4. Strengthening the capacity of women and men to contribute to biodiversity conservation, sustainable use of its components, and fair and equitable sharing of benefits arising from genetic resources


---
**Page 195**
---

3. Demonstrate the benefits of integrating gender
4. Increase the effectiveness of implementation efforts

Actions based on these objectives are organized around four spheres of work: policy, organizational, delivery, and constituency. The Plan further provides concrete actions to integrate gender aspects into all mentioned spheres relevant to successful implementation of the Convention.

### Regional Agreements on Forestry in the ASEAN region

The only regional agreement in the forestry and biodiversity sector is a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) on ASEAN Cooperation in Agriculture, Forest Products and Forest Product Promotion. This MoU has been revised several times since 1994, with the current one being in force since 2006. An MoU, as it does not require further ratifica- tion or deposit of instruments of accession, is not a legally binding instrument under international law. None of the related documents considers gender aspects of the identified schemes and production chains.

The year 2007 was seen as a political landmark in ASEAN cooperation with regard to forestry and related forest use enforcement. The Forest Law Enforcement, Governance and Trade (FLEGT) Action Plan and the FLEGT Work- shop Agreement (FLEGA) were issued in 2007 and paved the way to take action against illegal logging and its associated trade issues in a joint effort with regional partners and international stakeholders. The FLEGA work plan (2008–2013) was adopted by ASEAN member countries, which is aimed at promoting sustainable management of wildlife or timber or illegal logging, but it recognizes the social and cultural aspects in general, and poverty as a driver for forest degradation.

Gender considerations are notably absent in the actions set for improved market access, capacity building for new enforcement mechanisms, and promotion of forest products. The FLEGA work plan does not provide information on forest governance for business actors, civil society organizations and local communities.

### Forestry, Agriculture and Biodiversity in Laos PDR

The current Lao National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan 2016–2025 mentions the Lao Women’s Union as a potential civil society stakeholder to reach out to women, but it does not take gender issues into account beyond this. The Lao Women’s Union is also mentioned in the Forest Law Enforcement, Governance and Trade (FLEGT) supported under the National Strategy should also consider women, youth and ethnic groups beyond the internal Governmental structures. The Lao Women’s Union has a strong presence at the national level and push leaders as specialists and training resources in their functions as keepers of Traditional Knowledge.

With regard to the Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) on ASEAN Cooperation in Agriculture and Forest Prod- ucts with Indonesia, Malaysia, Thailand and Vietnam, the Lao Women’s Union is mentioned only once. The role of women in the MAF is to be the national coordinator and focal point for the other ASEAN member countries. The overall orientation of the MoU is to promote sustainable forest management and conservation through a multi-stakeholder approach. The Club – led by the Malaysian Timber Industry Board and therefore the private sector. Given the variety of stake- holders involved in the MAF, it is not clear how gender issues are addressed or whether they will be taken up at all, but do not form part of any guiding principles of the Club or the agreement itself. It is best to consider that women’s roles are not specifically mentioned.

The MoU on ASEAN Cooperation in Agriculture and Forest Products Promotion Scheme 2014–2018 with Indonesia, Malaysia, Thailand and Vietnam has a specific gender component. The Lao Women’s Union is mentioned only once as the focal point for the other ASEAN member countries. The overall orientation of the MoU is to promote sustainable forest management and conservation through a multi-stakeholder approach. The Club – led by the Malaysian Timber Industry Board and therefore the private sector.

The Lao FLEGT Voluntary Partnership Agreement (VPA) process is still in the negotiation phase, which was to be concluded at the end of 2016. The VPA is an agreement between a country and the European Union for gender-sensitive inclusion in areas concerning production and village forests, and the implications of the VPA on forest governance. In the context of the Lao FLEGT VPA process, the GIZ-FLEGT team conducted a gender analysis. The GIZ-FLEGT programme in Laos pointed out that most FLEGT-VPA meetings were predominantly led

The 9th National Socio-Economic Development Plan (NSED), as Lao PDR’s strategic document, refers to environmental protection and disaster risk reduction under Outcome 4, but its content is not specifically gender-sensitive or inclusive of women's rights. The Lao Women’s Union has been working on various initiatives aimed at directly or indirectly influence the forestry sector and therefore can be used for strategic decision/communication of laws, policies, and programs. The Lao Women’s Union has also been working with other organizations such as the Lao Women’s Union to cover all areas of work, and strengthen the capacity of female leadership and man- agement at various levels in the forestry sector. The Lao Women’s Union has been involved in a number of train- ing such as agricultural production, farming, animal husbandry, handicrafts, financial services, business manage- ment, banking, taxation, laws and business regulations, as well as research and formulate policies on access to finance and credit for women entrepreneurs. The Lao Women’s Union has also been working with other organizations and created conditions for women to take up leadership and management positions at various levels, reach an overall of 40% in the forestry sector.

Lao PDR1 adopted NDC (2015) puts significant weight on the forestry sector, for both mitigation and adaptation. It states that nature-based solutions shall be prioritized to counter climate-induced disasters such as floods, landslides, droughts, and heat waves. The Lao PDR has been working with other organizations in developing adaptation strategies and adaptation plans2, showing the potential to strengthen the promotion of cross-cutting measures for women’s empowerment and gender equality in all sectors, including forestry sector. Women are or were in any of its articles. The intervened Government partners at Provincial and District level all agreed upon the importance of involving women in decision-making processes on climate change adaptation.

The Constitution, the Labor Law, the Family Law, the Law on Women's Union, and the Law on the Development and Protection of Children are some examples of laws that have been used to promote gender equality. The (draft) Climate Change Strategy (2011) provides a comprehensive strategy for climate change adaptation and mitigation in Lao PDR, and strongly highlights the role of nature-based solutions. For instance, it highlights the need to “protect and restore forests, wetlands, and land for flood water storage, retention and regulation” to strengthen the resilience against an increas- ingly frequent occurrence of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, landslides, heat waves, storms and cyclones, and the impacts of climate change on defined vulnerable groups. This provides an avenue for addressing gender equality in forestry sector by ensuring that women are involved in decision-making processes and women. It also promotes a participatory model of action on climate change, including the participation of women’s organizations in risk assessment and adaptation planning and implementation. Northern Lao PDR is particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts due to its high dependency on rain-fed agriculture. Women are among the poorest in the country, and villagers in the region are largely dependent on rainfed upland agriculture for their livelihoods.

The Lao National Adaptation Program of Action (NAPA) does not explicitly address gender challenges or oppor- tunities in relation to climate change. In 2009, NAPA was released and includes a list of 45 adaptation priority projects in the forestry sector. The Lao PDR is currently developing its second National Adaptation Program of Action (NAPA) which will be submitted for approval by the UNFCCC in 2016. The NAPA II strategy or plan, as well as the NAPA II implementation plan, will address gender issues and provide a gender-sensitive approach to climate change adaptation.

The National REDD+ Strategy to 2025 and Vision to 2030 guides REDD+ implementation in Lao PDR, and together they aim to improve the quality and extent of forests nationwide to provide economic, social and environmental benefits. The strategy is based on three pillars: (i) ensuring sustainable forest management; (ii) reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation; and (iii) conserving biodiversity. It also includes three core elements: (i) ensuring the sustainable management and use of forest resources ii) conserving forest resources in order to protect forests, forestland, flora and fauna, aquatic animals and wildlife, including through

## Implications for the GCF Project

Regional structures and existing legal frameworks do not provide sufficient guidance for all concerned stakeholders on a gender-sensitive approach to forest governance. The project will contribute to the development of implementable guidelines for the practical integration of gender into forest governance from international frameworks and action plans, in order to ensure that women’s voices are heard.

A valuable contribution of the project can be its support in the development of guidelines and regulations for forest governance at the national level. These guidelines would provide a framework for integrating gender considerations into gender, economic and social status. Furthermore, the project might also lead to significantly promote gender-sensitive approaches within the legal frameworks and action plans.

So far, Project 1 has promoted the development of a number of guidelines in a gender-sensitive process. Guidelines for the Promotion of Sustainable and Depletion-free Agricultural Practices and Values Chains (PSDAP), which are being developed by the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fishery (MAFF) in collaboration with the project, will be used to promote sustainable agricultural practices that are also gender-sensitive.

In a similar way, the development of guidelines for Village Forest and Agricultural Grants (VAGs) has been supported by Project 1. The guidelines will provide guidance on how to ensure that VAGs are distributed equitably among women’s households and young farming families. Project 2 will draw in these guidelines and scale up their application.

### 3.3 Meso Level: Gender dimensions at sectoral institutions and actors

The interviews showed that the Provincial and District Foresteries agency might possess only limited awareness on the importance of gender equality in forest governance, as well as the need to promote women’s participation which lacked concrete understanding of gender mainstreaming, as well as the fact that most interviewee partners in the forestry offices were men. One office mentioned that they have a gender focal point but did not consider involving her in the implementation process and that she was not considered for any decision-making positions within the project structures.

The key message from the interview questions was coherent with other programme findings, indicating that effective gender mainstreaming in the forestry sector is only partly in the legal framework but mainly at the implementation level. This includes knowledge and awareness, as well as personal and financial resources, and lack of documentation on women’s participation in forest governance.

The institutional structures for improved gender-responsive forest governance exist (ministers’/gender focal points, Laos Women’s Union), but currently lack in-depth technical knowledge, budgets and the internal mandates to be taken seriously enough.


## Implications for the GCF Project

The project should actively pursue the cooperation with the LWW during implementation. One on one hand to make sure that the LWW can provide guidance and local networks to promote gender equality in the land use management. On the other hand, to further capacitate the organisation to promote gender equality in the context of deforestation and forest degradation, which is a key issue for the project’s success at the national level.

The LWW has been involved as a partner since 2015 when it was selected by the GCF as a lead implementing agency. During implementation of Project 1, The Laos Women’s Union (LWW) became a key partner in the context of gender mainstreaming and gender equality in forest governance. The LWW has also been actively involved in the promotion of gender perspectives. As per Annual Progress Report, monitoring data shows that LWW has participated in 87% of an average time allocated to its Mission Cooperation with the GCF and was the Continuous Smart Project 1.

Further, the project should aim to improve capacities of implementation partners on GESI, gender equality and inclusive processes through adequate training. Further, GESI needs to be integrated as cross-cutting issue into all technical training materials.

### 3.4. Micro Level: Consultations with the Target Groups in the Sector

#### Summary of stakeholder consultations on gender for the Programme (Project 1)

The field consultations took place between 15-27 January 2019, one mission to Houphouet Province from 15–18 January and two missions to Langu Namdria District from 23-24 January. The stakeholder consultations were conducted with the Provincial Agriculture and Forestry Office (PAFO) and the Provincial Land Women Union (PLWU). At the district level, interviews were conducted with the District Agriculture and Forestry Offices (DAFO) and District Lao Women’s Union (DLWU) in Kam Neua (Houphouet) and Langu Namdria District. In total, four village consultations were held in Kam Neua District and five village consultations were held in Langu Namdria District. One village in Langu Namdria District is not included. In total, 148 people were interviewed and consulted (79 men and 69 women).

The selected villages are of different ethnic and economic backgrounds, and showed different levels of women’s participation.

- **Ban Yond Village/Houphouet:** Khmu and Hmong ethnicity, with two villages merged into one. High poverty and unemployment rates, low education standard. Strong weaving tradition, livestock as central income source. Women were not consulted in the village meetings. The PAFO supported the consultations, and the organization of five separate meetings for women and men was supported.

- **Ban Nam Mai Village/Langu Namdria:** Akha ethnicity. Village was moved several times in the past to avoid conflict with other ethnic groups. High poverty rate, low education level, high unemployment rates, and low literacy levels are still very low. Rubber provides the main income, in addition to income from forest resources. Women were not consulted at village meetings or during the district-level consultations. The women needed continuous translation between Akha and Lao, and appeared over very shy to speak up in front of the village authorities and other villagers.

- **Ban Nam Kham Village/Langu Namdria:** Hmong ethnicity. The village is an ex-situ tourism site. Income levels are proportionally higher, but with high dependence on funds for the tourists which will end soon, and on their income from rubber with strong price fluctuation (monopoly of one Chinese company). Women were not consulted in the village meetings or during the district-level consultations. The village did not organize separate meetings, and the village head continuously overemphasized the attention to women’s participation could also be encouraged through the attending national Lao Women’s Union representative.

In general, the programme was well received by all consulted stakeholders. At the district level, many participants were aware of the importance of gender equality in development projects. Women are the main collectors of forest products, know forest well and have seen the impact of deforestation and forest degradation on their livelihoods. However, they do not have access to land or other resources for agricultural production (except for rubber). They also lack knowledge about alternative uses of forest products such as non-timber forest products (NTFP) for domestic consumption. Also, some stakeholders noted forest biodiversity has declined. In terms of agriculture, women are the main producers and they have a good understanding of their crops. However, they tried to use dung fertilizers, but then switched to some chemical fertilizers. While they can see the impact of deforestation on their livelihoods, they do not know how to address this issue. Women also noted that due to lack of available finances and knowledge of alternative “good” practices for land use and land practices. They further noted that women are not involved in decision-making processes (based on land and plantations). A summary of comments from the general specific stakeholder consultations and their implications, as well as how they have been incorporated into the programme and/or GAP is provided in the following table.

# Summary of stakeholder consultations on gender for Project 2

With updating the subproject documentation during preparations for Project 2, field consultations have been conducted in Dodongwe, Luing Prabang and Ssebukubu from March 13 to May 26. The stakeholder consultations at local level included male and female villagers from diverse ethnic backgrounds. At district level, governmental organizations (GOs) such as the Ministry of Gender Development, Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry, private sector interviews have been conducted, including consultations with 39 stakeholders from agri- MOUs), community-based organizations (CBOs), non-governmental organizations (NGOs), and civil society organizations (CSOs) have been interviewed, and national and provincial level kick-off meetings (all in provinces) have been held. The following is a summary of the comments and recommendations collected during these consultations.

The Gender Development Association (GDA) and the Association for Development of Women and Legal Education (ADOLE) were consulted at the district level. The GDA reported that women had equal opportunities to participate and gain benefits from the project. In some villages, it was evident that women were more involved in project activities than men. This could be attributed to the project implementers. With Project 1, women became specifically involved in law enforcement and participated in land and forest management committees and forest patrolling groups at village level. The project was found to strengthen women's involvement in management operations at the local level, especially for women, who have a better understanding of gender roles.

# Gender responsiveness, expertise, and gender equality in the partner organisation

The main implementing partner of the planned GCF programme will be the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF) and the EP [host of the National REDD+ Funding Window]. The majority of staff – especially at senior level – work in the responsible departments and divisions, including the REDD+ division. This is a significant gender imbalance at partner level. This imbalance becomes worse at the Provincial and District Level. As mentioned above, the DoF is striving to hire and promote more women into positions so as to increase the number of women in conservation work to at least 30%. A current figure could not be obtained.

Representation in terms of numbers is one issue: The factual participation of female staff who are already working in the partner organisation in meetings, workshops, or other training events for staff are often not actively participating in meetings. This was also observed in the Provincial DMFS/DOH interviews.


## Implications for the planned GCF project

Besides supporting the partners in proactive and improved recruitment and promotion processes of female staff, the current gender capacities of all staff seem to be rather low. The GCF programme should emphasize the importance of gender equality in all aspects of the project implementation, including capacity building activities. Knowing that partners do particularly well in response to quantified analysis of the costs of gender inequality, the DoF could use this as a tool for mobilizing resources from a common pool used for partner communication and capacity development measures.

A gender strategy will be developed, including the LWRU – should be submitted, focusing on the gender dimensions of the forestry sector, REDD+ and climate resilient and deforestation-free agriculture and agricultural value chains.


The Lao Women’s Union is not seen as a direct implementation partner but should be utilized by the programme in much more proactive ways for reasons such as lack of gender capacities and to ensure a continuous commitment to gender equality (see also Chapter 3.3).


---
**Page 208**
---

As Project 3 has not started yet, nothing can be said about the gender responsiveness of the not yet assigned project staff; however, the performance of Project 1 as the successor of the Project 2, can be assessed.

For Project 1, a consulting team was hired to ensure that environmental and social safeguards are in place and also to monitor their implementation. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is an integral part of the Environmental and Social Safeguards and the Gender Action Plans for the actual processes, a brief set of activities and all systems of the project.

One of the key elements of the GAP is the integration of gender into the project’s safeguards and monitoring system to confirm their integration of gender. Also, the Master Budget was thoroughly checked for risks associated with gender, either negative or positive impacts on women and men. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is an integral part of the Environmental and Social Safeguards and the Gender Action Plans for the actual processes, a brief set of activities and all systems of the project.

When developing the system, the ESMP and the Gender Action Plan were analysed in detail by the Safeguard Team and gender mainstreaming was embedded into the design of the safeguards and monitoring system. Consideration of gender is fundamentally embedded into the approach of all village level activities, mainly Free Prior Informed Consent (FPIC), Participatory Land Use Planning (PLUP) and Village Forest Management and the Promotion of Sustainable Agricultural Practices (PSAP).

### Implications for the planned GCF project

The planned Project 3 should build on the experience of Project 1 and established a Safeguards Team to implement and monitor the project’s safeguards and gender management system. It should employ at least one advisor with expertise in gender mainstreaming, ensuring that gender equality and mainstreaming is the conservation sector. The vacancy should explicitly require such expertise.

To ensure sound integration into all activities, this position would ideally be held by a senior technical advisor rather than with a project officer. This would allow for a more effective role of the Safeguard Team as the focal point of the project to oversee gender mainstreaming efforts, including gender sensitization for both men and women.

It is important, however, to equally empower all team members to be able to mainstream gender into their work area expertise. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) should be a key tool in this regard. If not all staff possess sufficient gender expertise, the project should invest in a technical gender training aiming to develop the capacity of all staff to mainstream gender into their work areas.

All standard procedures of the programme should be streamlined to review gender aspects throughout the course of the project.

The G2 Gender Strategy, GG Guidelines on Designing a Gender-Sensitive Results-Based Monitoring System, the Practical Guide to Gender Responsive Programme Management and the Guidelines on Gender in Reporting provide useful guidance for this purpose. Additional technical expertise to integrate gender into forestry projects is provided by FAO’s Practical Field Guide “How to mainstream gender in forestry.”

# Gender gaps and recommendations to integrate them into the GCF programme

The following table connects the identified gaps (i.e., in addition to those identified in the stakeholder consultations), with regard to gender with proposed recommendations to act upon these drivers. The third column shows the integration into the existing outline for components and activities of the planned GCF programme, and the fourth column provides a brief description of each recommendation.

They are not listed according to priority: They should be integrated as much as possible throughout the course of the programme, starting with the funding proposal, the Log frame, the operational planning, and monitoring and evaluation.

Furthermore, it is strongly recommended to review this analysis and the effectiveness of the suggested activities on a regular basis, and to include it in the required on-boarding documents for new programme staff.

7. Measures for a gender responsive/transformative project design

| Table 5: Overview of the needs of the survey, observed risks and conclusions for the project |
| --- | --- |

### Gender aspects and risks which are particularly relevant for the implementation of the project

- **Violence against women:** A reality for women from all ethnic groups.
- **Limited access to education and training:** Women have lower levels of education and lacking skills.
- **Limited access to land, credit, and capacity to improve their livelihoods:** Women lack access to resources and are unable to participate in village management tasks.

### Measures to promote gender equality

#### Overarching project measures
1. Mainstream gender sensitization throughout all project activities.
2. Ensure that women’s voices are heard at all levels of the project through their legal rights under national and international law in the implementation of the project.
3. Develop and provide training on Gender and Country Gender Equality Strategy (GCES) for project staff.
4. Provide training to project staff on the VSLA as a tool for project implementation: Project 1 installed the VLSA as an important tool for women’s empowerment.

#### Measures to prevent gender-related risks and possible unintended negative impacts
- **Engage men and boys to promote non-violence and gender equality:** Engage men and boys in activities that promote non-violence, including gender equality.
- **Ensure that women are included at all levels of the project implementation process:** Ensure that women’s voices are heard at all levels of the project through their legal rights under national and international law in the implementation of the project.

# Measures for component 2 - Market solutions for agricultural drivers of deforestation

- Assessment of value chains to be promoted under the project and their potential contribution to women's empowerment
- Ensure that a gender assessment is integrated in every market analysis conducted as part of the project
- Ensuring that women receive targeted training opportunities, including on climate change adaptation and mitigation, as well as access to information on climate change impacts and adaptation strategies
- Provide women with better means, knowledge, and networks to improve their livelihoods

# Measures for component 3 - Climate change mitigation and adaptation through forestry

## Introduction
Quote text: Introduce quotes for the participation of women and women's organizations in REDD+ activities.

## Gender considerations
- Ensure that gender is a cross-cutting topic throughout all training sessions, workshops, and meetings organized by the project
- Ensure that gender equality aspects are female forest patrols, as they often carry out dangerous activities, and may be far away from their homes

## Training and capacity building
- Set up a quota of 50% participation of women in community-based forest management committees
- Establish a gender-sensitive selection criteria to determine which field level activities included in REDD+ related meetings
- Ensure that women are represented as members of village forest management committees at the local level

## Promotion and support for women-led, participatory groups
- Promote and support their creation with capacity development and awareness raising activities
- Facilitate their participation in village-level forest management committees by meeting their specific needs (communication, time poverty, lack of resources, limited mobility, and networks)

# Conclusion
Quote text: Conclude the document with a summary or final thoughts.


---
**Page 223**
---

Key findings on gender expertise, gender responsiveness, and main impediments to the project implementation (when under management):
1. What skills are needed to go from gender mainstreaming and to the established structure is very high, the gender division re-mains in many cases.
2. Most staff – especially at senior level - within the responsible ministries and agencies are men. This means that women are more likely to be gender experts than men; hence, there is a greater gender imbalance at partner level. This can lead to a lack of gender expertise at partner level.
3. Last Women’s Union is not seen as a direct implementation partner but rather as an advisory body. It will do its best to compensate for the current lack of gender capacity.

A gender training for all involved partners – including the LWU, gender focal points, foundations of the project, and representatives from the forestry sector, REDD+ and climate-related and deforestation projects – was held in January 2013. The training was very positive involving LWU in all project activities as local implementation partner.

Key findings on gender expertise and responsiveness, in the present context of Project 2:
1. As Project 2 has not started yet, nothing can be said about the gender capacity of the project team.
2. For Project 2, a consulting team was hired to ensure that planned activities are gender responsive and to provide advice for mainstreaming gender.
3. Since the safeguard Team has focused on main-streaming gender and the Gender Action Plan into the ac-tion plan, it is expected that this will be reflected in all aspects of the project.
4. The ESMP and the Gender Action Plan were integrated into the project’s strategy and operational systems.

The planned Project 2 should build on the experience of Project 1 to ensure that gender mainstreaming is fully embedded within the project’s safeguards and gender management system.


Emphasize the importance of gender mainstreaming nearly from the very beginning of the way through all stages of activities to change processes that could reinforce gender in-equalities. This can be done by involving more women into its discourse to increase the number of women who are able to influence decision-making. This can be done by emphasizing the need to collaborate with female en-gineers and other professionals, as well as to involve them in existing gender initiatives by training main public staff only.

It is important to equally employ all team members to be able to mainstream gender into their work tasks; especially for men who are not used to work against gender-based inequalities at implementation level.

10. Annex 2: Transcripts of stakeholder interviews

GCF/GIZ, GoI interviewees: PAFO & PLURU Houaphan

Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Program through Improved Governance and Sustainable Forest Landscape Management

1. What is your role in RE+EDD mechanisms implementation?
Supporting the CUPA activities

2. From your point of view, what are women's specific roles in forest land use, and in forest protection? Collection of NTFPs.

3. How are women already part of RE+EDD mechanisms?
PROM: Women should be part of RE+EDD activities but in fact are not yet, except for women’s participation in village meetings (PRAP etc.). Awareness-raising campaigns on forest laws and regulations also target women. Training is provided to women from the villages (trainer from PAFO, participants LWF, DMO, DOANE, police, DirCommOffice). More trainings will follow in the coming months (e.g., EFP).

4. How could they be more part of it?
Higher levels (Provincial and national) need to be trained further so that they can support building the capacities of the Districts. Also, activities in the villages need continuous effort to reach out to women.

5. Which challenges do you see when it comes to remunerate areas, women, ethnicity? 
BOS remote, mountainous communities, so change of livelihood will take time and a multi-sector approach is needed. The main challenge is that women are not yet involved in the process. There have been women having truly participate; lack of understanding and education esp. amongst remoter living ethnic groups. Women are not yet convinced to be part of the process. They need to be more convinced of the suggested change (lifestyle, livelihood & agriculture changes etc.). It usually does not happen in the communities.

Note: Village Development Funds (VDF) are mainly used by women (livestock projects mainly).

6. Do you feel women have the same access to benefits from forest resources and their management? 
Women have limited access to forest resources. They do not have access to VDFs, community managed forests, collaboratives forests, protection forests, national parks, wildlife reserves, and buffer zone communities.

NTFPs & firewood are usually collected and sold by women (often to Chinese merchants), so the benefits include them. Forest landscape plans help to determine roles and responsibilities in villages’ forest use.

7. What women groups are currently involved in forest management modalities?
Women groups are usually less active in forest management activities, but the LWF is part of the Livelihood Improvement Group, and women are mainly responsible for the VDF management.


56


---
**Page 229**
---

8. How do you assess your current capacities to support the planned program in regards to gender equality?

PARF: Our knowledge and experience to support gender equity are not sufficient, despite the many projects we have implemented. We need trainings in management, procurement, accounting, and financial management. We also need training on how to integrate gender aspects into our programs and agriculture-related technical trainings. Study tours to other countries are useful to understand practical experiences, even between villages (peer-to-peer learning between different villages from different Provinces).

9. How gender-sensitive do you think the current REDD+ mechanisms are? How much of REDD+ and gender aspects of it integrate into the SEGP?

Clarity: This question is important for our understanding about gender issues and women's participation. [Note: Interviewees did not answer this question.]

10. The programme will support the development of regulations and guidelines based on the new Forest Law: from a gender perspective, what needs to be thought off?

Decree PM, LWU law, SEGP all suggest the equal participation in laws. This must be mentioned in the regulation. It is important that women are involved in making these new regulations, even if only in the programme area first as a pilot. The laws for equal participation are in existence – but it's up on regular basis to ensure that they are applied.

11. How can women be part in law enforcement measures?

We have a Law Enforcement Action Plan until 2020 (responsible: Dep. Of Forestry Inspection, POSI). But it is very generic; no mention of women’s participation. We should think of ways to include women in law enforcement measures. If they would like to go on patrolling, they should be supported in that.

12. LUP and improved tenure security: what are the current challenges esp. for women, what needs to be improved?

LUP plans have a tenure management plan, so it pays the path to equal participation. The plans should be very specific (ignoring at least 20% suggested) how many women have to participate in which land & forest use or management.

So far, LUP has only been done for areas not, for individual cases, so it’s hard to draw conclusions on the factual tenure security from that. Having both names of husband and wife on land titles will dramatically improve the situation.

13. Development of a National Forest Monitoring System: how can women contribute?

Note: Question was not understood correctly.

14. Programme wants to promote deforestation-free agriculture, away from shifting cultivation practices, and promoting alternative cash crop production practices. Which crops are most suitable for this? What would be the impact on people’s livelihoods? What would that mean for the extension services provided? Programme will also include community-based learning and communication activities.

Agricultural Strategy leads the way. Cash crops promoted for mainstream: coffee, Chinese cinnamon; pilot projects are already running (too early to draw conclusions). One main difficulty is the lack of markets.

15. Promotion of deforestation-free value chains and access to markets: what are women’s greatest challenges to develop such value chains now, what are their barriers to access markets now?


---
**Page 230**
---

Key problem is the general lack of markets, not the production. P4P/IP has to address the market gaps and production needs. This is not identified as a commercial crop in Housaphan, only for domestic consumption.

16. Access to microfinance for these new value chains: how are women benefiting from that?

LWI: Micro finance is a very essential mechanism to promote women's economic activity. However, MFs are not always the best option and should be used as an additional tool. In Housaphan, we think it would be essential to increase access to microfinance/literacy banks in Housaphan to promote people's confidence in their own abilities.

17. How do you see the potentials and challenges in inviting private sector investments in community-based agroforestry in national production forests, including in the timber and fiber industries? Do you think villages will benefit directly from such investment? How could the Govt ensure that the in-vestments are benefiting the villages?

Investment is a huge potential but need to be tailored to the different potentials of the Districts, e.g. Livestock in Housamensing and Sam Neua, Vineyard for fruit trees, etc. Ecodurism as huge potential for the timber industry. The private sector has to be involved in the development of the forest. Govt. has to support with careful study of the impacts on local communities – ifthe investment is not beneficial to the villages and create increased income for people, we cannot allow such investment.

18. Any other remarks?

LWI: national laws & strategies, as well as leadership position quotas are in place - the gap is on the implementation level; where we need very concrete guidelines, with details on gender equality, participation, etc.

Women lack knowledge because of lower education levels, young marriage an issue, lower roles in society despite laws stating equality. Just setting quotas does not help if it is not combined with awareness raising and training. Women are also often involved in other activities (working with allergics; LWI is present in all villages but needs stronger utilization via village management.

Women can play a huge role in deforestation-free agriculture, e.g., silk worm farms & silk production, cotton farming, etc. The textile industry is very inclusive and women dominate it in Vietnam, but we could support the development of these value chains in Housaphan. Textile industry is very inclusive, since it does not require high education and also disabled women can work in it.

58


---
**Page 231**
---

### What is your role in REDD+ mechanisms implementation?

Districts/DAFO are key implementers of REDD+ mechanisms. Roles: planning process, hotspot and potential analysis.

#### From your point of view: what are women's specific roles in forest and land use, and in forest protection?

Women are main users of forests, so they need to get involved in all activities and facilitated by all sector agencies (incl. UN).

#### How are women already part of REDD+ mechanisms?

Involvement of women in the planning processes very limited, since mostly only the head of HK participates.

#### How could they be more part of it?

We have to have more women participating already from the planning process in villages onwards. That starts with awareness raising for the women (DLWU key responsibility), in collaboration with DAFO and other agencies.

### Which challenges do you see when it comes to remote areas, women, ethnicity?

Language barriers: kuman level meetings usually include travel which women cannot do, so only men attend.
Solutions: hire more ethnic staff in District teams. Keep fewer meetings at the village level only, or allocate resources and organize pick-ups for women to be able to travel.

### Do you feel women have the same access to benefits from forest resources and their management/decision-making?

That is different for different forest management modalities, like community forests (conservation areas), protection forests, national parks, wildlife reserves, and buffer zone community forests.

Women have little access to decision-making and planning processes on forest resources, but are the most users. Women who participate in the village level meetings, which are usually chaired by men that work in the form of shared decision-making, families definitely discuss purchases and investments jointly. However, women do not have a say as much as the traditional head of the mts.

### What women groups are currently involved in forest management modalities?

No women groups except for UNU.

### How do you assess your current capacities to support the planned program in regards to gender equality?

Still need CB, new technology which needs more training and staff fluctuation are challenges; need more focus on gender issues, plus regular opportunities to discuss and exchange on implementation experiences.

### How gender-sensitive do you think the current REDD+ mechanisms are? How much is REDD+ and gender aspects of it integrated into the SEEDP?

59


---
**Page 232**
---

10. The programme will support the development of regulations and guidelines based on the new Forest Law and its implementation.

The Forest Law is too general, so regulations need to be based on the reality of Districts and Provinces. For the creation of the regulations, it would be best to include both District authorities and villages in the consultations. Follow-up/monitoring.

11. How can women fit into law enforcement measures?

Villagers, especially women, do not understand why the laws are relevant; so enforcement starts with encouraging villagers' support to adhere to the law.

12. Development of a National Forest Monitoring System: how can women contribute?

Women could be part of NTFP monitoring, but needs a lot of initial support and follow-up.

13. LUP and improved tenure security: what are the current challenges? esp. for women; what needs to be improved?

LUP did only allocate land on a village level so far, but most of the times, no land titles have been issued yet. Women are disadvantaged because the law decisions are at village and are taken by the (mostly male) village authorities and heads of HK. LUP needs to be more specific in regards to land use and half-completed LUP process need to be completed. So land tenure security on the current basis.

14. Programme wants to promote deforestation-free agriculture, away from shifting cultivation practices. What are women's greatest challenges in this? What implications will that have on a woman's work life? People's livelihoods? What would that mean for women's access to resources?

The programme needs to include community-based learning and consultation activities with participating farmers/villages.

Needs in depth village-by-village potential and risk analysis. Funding: need to invest needs to be available. People are already willing to stop shifting cultivation but currently don't have alternatives.

15. Promotion of deforestation-free value chains and access to markets: what are women's greatest challenges? What implications will that have on a woman's work life? People's livelihoods?

Lack of financial resources, lack plus lack of confidence to risk investment.

Markets are local, and many villages are far off the market roads and cannot access them. DFFP is not yet established in the area, so there would be no market for the products and end up where, so that market assessments become possible, and prices can be regulated for the benefit of the farmers (who otherwise have to sell at a lower price). Data collection: data comes from the Provincial Office for Industry and Commerce, collection of data by their District Officer.

16. Access to microfinance for these new value chains: how are women benefiting from that?

They would probably benefit from that if there would not be the immense risk to lose investments (e.g., when a woman invests in a small business and it fails). Also, the interest rates need to be low so that poorer women can access MF, e.g. via Village Banks.

17. How do you see the potential and challenges in inviting private sector investments in community-based forest management? What are the forest sectors, including in the timber and non-timber resources? Do you think villagers will be benefiting from such investment? How could the Govt ensure that the investments are benefitting the villages?


---
**Page 233**
---

Proposals will go through FAFO which will review the benefits for the villagers carefully. Consult with the villagers, and do an area survey. Monitoring and follow up by DAFO.

18. Any other remarks?

Plans need to be realistic in terms of budget. Use regular village visits to raise rather than usual topics (e.g., gender equality) in a frame of livelihood improvement, something tangible for the villagers). Make meetings more attractive for villagers, making clear what the benefit for them is in attending.

One should target the younger generation if you want to promote gender equality, and keep it simple and interesting.(videos, games etc.).

1. What is your role in REDD+ mechanisms implementation?
   - FPIC and VDF implementation and management, no direct implementation responsibility for REDD+, e.g., village resource management aspects of FVC process

2. From your point of view: what are women's specific roles in forest land use, and in forest protection?
   - LWU contribute to legal dissemination and mobilization of women in the villages to aid forest protection.

3. Which challenges do you see when it comes to matter area, women, ethnicity?
   - Knowledge and understanding in women low which affects meetings and participations significantly; low education level esp. among older age groups

4. Do you feel women have the same access to benefits from forest resources and their management/decision-making? Is that different for different forest management modalities, like commu- nity forestry, private sector, or government-managed forests?
   - Women have limited access to power as it is very limited; it is difficult for more women for this task because most don't feel educated enough do the technical tasks related to that responsibility.

   - Once District Steering Committee is in place, conduct a gender training for them and us focused on the kind of activities which will be implemented on the village level.

10. Programme wants to promote diversification free agriculture, away from shifting cultivation practices, and promoting alternative cash crop production practices. What would that mean for the extension services provided? What would that mean for the livelihoods of women? What would that mean for the extension services provided? Programme will also include community-based learning and consultation activities with participating farmers' villages.

Promotion of more equitable share of domestic work (awareness raising campaigns).

11. Promote value chains in which women are involved, what are women's greatest challenges to develop such value chains now, what are their barriers to access markets now? MOD has to support the identification of new markets. Markets now are too far away for most remote villages. Sometimes villages produce a lot (e.g., vegetables) but markets are too far for them to bring it there.

12. Access to microfinance for these new value chains: how are women benefitting from that?

MF can aid women to start businesses in handicraft, livestock, agriculture. Main customers of MF institutions are currently women. Combine access to MF with business trainings for women. In case of cash crops, women need to be trained on marketing and sales. Women need to know what they are prepared and more confident.

More focus should be given to the skills needed (e.g., handicrafts). Also not only talking about how to access markets, but organize learning tours for villages to markets. When women start businesses, they often have to deal with people who are sensitive to their fears and insecurities regarding risk and lower education levels.

13. How do you see the potential and challenges in inviting private sector investments in community-based enterprises? What is your view on the role of the village and the government? Do you think villagers will be benefiting from such investment? How could the GOI ensure that the investments are benefitting the villages?

Concession & labor fees should go to villagers, land goes back to village. Technical knowledge should be provided by the company to the villagers.

### What is your role in REDD+ mechanisms implementation?

Preparing new structure for REDD+ implementation right now. PRAP finalized but awaits signing. Con- sulted with communities on forest management modalities and their roles in REDD+ activities.

REDD dissemination, forest regulation dissemination, scaling up PRAF activities & training, admin budget

### From your point of view: what are women’s specific roles in forest land use, and in forest pro- tection?

No separation from Got. side. VDF or conservation fund oftenes more lead by women and are seen as key players in management and accounting. Patrolling is traditionally done by men, even though women are just as capable to do that. And if long as forests are close to the villages, women could also do that task.

### How are women already part of REDD+ mechanisms?

Women could have many roles—because they are more perceptive and considerate of the natural re- sources. They can be involved in activities like handcrafts work, like handicrafts/leather. NTFP collection. Rubber harvest done more efficiently and successfully by women.

### How could they be more part of it?

We are aware that we need to involve especially women when it comes to the dissemination of forest related information. Women will also be involved in training and non discussion groups because otherwise women will not speak out. Women are also important educators towards their children.

### Which challenges do you see when it comes to remoter areas, women, ethnicity?

Women are the key persons in collecting NTFPs etc., but in ethnic villages, it is mostly men who take all decisions. We need to increase our awareness raising to these communities regarding equal decision- making.

Lack of Lao language skills makes it difficult to communicate with ethnic villagers, esp. the women. Their education level is also lower than that of the majority population. Women’s participation has signifi- cantly in the last ten years, so it is getting easier to access them.

### Do you feel women have the same access to benefits from forest resources and their manage- ment?

Women are more involved in forest management modalities like community forests, collaborative forests, protected forests, national parks, wildlife reserves, and buffer zone conservation areas.

Women as money-keepers of the family possess higher benefits from the resources. In many ethnic minorities, men however have often the “last word” in decision-making. Not the case in the cities, where women are more involved in forest management.

Men have more access to forest resources management. We need to get more women into the forest management modalities and make sure that they can see the “cash value” of forest resources and their management; they will naturally want to become more involved.

### What women groups are currently involved in forest management modalities?

LWU only as a group. VDF has promoted a lot of women in managing roles, especially in regards to SME development. Ecotourism activities have promoted women into management positions.


---
**Page 237**
---

What are their capacities, what would they need to improve?

PAFO female staff has some capacities, but child birth puts them behind. Two female patrollers in Long District who work together with POF as part of law enforcement.

How do you assess your current capacities to support the planned program in regards to gender equality?

PAFO has two more gender focal points, men and women, who is often involved in setting up activities and sometimes goes to the villages. Also, LWU is the go-to structure to implement any specific activities.

A specialized training on REDD+ and gender would be useful before implementation starts. Also a training on gender mainstreaming facilitation and presentation skills will be of great value for village patrols.

How gender-sensitive do you think the current REDD+ mechanisms are? How much are REDD+ and gender aspects of it integrated into the SEEDP?

Women were very much involved in the planning process of the current REDD+ plan. The overall gen-der-oriented approach has been positive.

The program will support the development of regulations and guidelines based on the new For- est Law: from a gender perspective, what needs to be thought off?

Not necessary to mention gender specifically in the regulations, but instead bring more women into the implementation process. This is not easy, but not impossible. On Provincial level, balance is fine, but not in most villages, so focus there.

How can women be part in law enforcement measures?

Patrolling is accompanied by PAFO, DMO, DOF together with militia and police. Not possible without village support. Villages often request guard. Women can be dangerous (hunters, loggers). Some women might be the opposite, but not general.

LUP and improved tenure security: what are the current challenges esp. for women, what needs to be improved?

LUP has majorly completed except of villages with low integration which makes it difficult for au-thorities to enforce. The LUP is more complex than previous land use plans. It takes longer time than it previously took. Sing District has particular immigration issues due to fast develop-ment in the area and lack of land. Women are not always included in discussions on how to divide land equally. Participation in forest-related decisions was very equal, and women and men feel equally in-volved.

Development of a National Forest Monitoring System: how can women contribute?

Women know the forests well and should be largely involved in their monitoring. They are also viewed as more reliable and honest than men in providing this kind of information.

Programme wants to promote deforestation-free agriculture, away from shifting cultivation prac-tices, and towards improved land use practices. Which implications will that have on women’s work? How’s livelihoods? What would that mean for the extension services provided? Programme also include community-based learning and consultation activities with participating farmers/villages.

**Page 238**

16. Promotion of deforestation-free value chains and access to markets: what are women’s greatest challenges in developing these? How can we discuss potential work divisions needed for a new product (e.g., men do planting, women harvest etc.)?

Women usually know more about market access than men, and also what prices certain products get. That can be useful to them.

17. Access to microfinance for these new value chains: how are women benefitting from that?

Microfinance institutions often a high barrier for people to use as starters for a new value chain/product etc., because people are afraid of the interest rates and paying back the money on time, and people don’t have any collateral. But there is an increasing number of microfinance institutions which can be useful for the villages to learn how to identify sources for investment themselves.

18. How do you see the potential and challenges in inviting private sector investments in community-based agroforestry in national production forests, including in the timber and fiber industries? Do you think that such investments are beneficial to such investment? How could the GIZ ensure that the investments are benefiting the villages?

Lessons learned from past: organized training tour for villagers and provided them with seeds to empower them. In some cases, the companies provided the funds for these activities; sometimes PAFO if they have the budget available. Sometimes a paper company was built, so PAFO trained villages how to plant bamboo to sell to that company.

But also, sometimes it doesn’t work out well because of fluctuating prices. Price fluctuations tend to heavily impact the village often leading to deforestation because villagers simply need to plant other crops in case additional income. If more companies are interested in invest, maybe agricultural land can be directly allocated to them.

### What is your role in REDD+ mechanisms implementation?

PAFO usually coordinates with PLWU about forest-related activities, and vice-president and one technical advisor from PAFO also join JUFORD activities on village level. Also gender-sensitive information dissemination to villagers.

2. From your point of view: what are women's specific roles in forest land use, and in forest protection?

LUP has been improved; many stopped shifting cultivation; jobs were created

3. Do you feel women have the same access to benefits from forest resources and their management activities as men? If not, why do you think this is so?

Women have equal access to benefits from forest resources and their management activities.

4. What groups of women are currently involved in forest management modalities?

LWU is not a key implementer but coordinates with all sectors to ensure gender equality. We wish more women to be part of the management also at village level. We need to mobilize women in the villages, especially those who have been left behind by their husbands. Women are more involved only if the head of the family are called into meetings; because then only men join. Customary practices and norms also prevent women from participating fully in forest management activities as they feel less confident in participating. All these factors need to be taken into account when planning village activities.

5. How do you assess your current capacity to support the planned program in regards to gender equality?

We had some trainings as introduction into the sector but would appreciate more in-depth trainings on REDD+ and how to support the programme activities best.

6. How gender sensitive do you think the current REDD+ mechanisms are? How much are REDD+ and gender aspects of it integrated into the SEFP?

It was good that PLWU was involved in all consultations towards formulating REDD+ activities in the last year, but there is still a lack of transparency of the process.

7. The programme will support the development of regulations and guidelines based on the new Forest Law: from a gender perspective, what needs to be thought off?

LWU should be involved in the development of these regulations and guidelines. The Forestry Law has not been reviewed since 1980; it is very outdated. They want to improve their understanding once the law has been improved in the National Assembly.

8. How can women take part in law enforcement measures?

Women are parts of different committees in the forest and should be systematically trained to also take part in law enforcement measures including patrolling.


---
**Page 240**
---

9. LUP and improved tenure security: what are the current challenges esp. for women, what needs to be improved?

No specific challenges by gender, rather the general challenge that LUP has not been completed in some areas.

10. Development of a National Forest Monitoring System: how can women contribute?

Women know the forests better than men and should therefore be strongly involved in the monitoring of forest resources.

11. Programs to promote deforestation-free agriculture, away from shifting cultivation practices, and promoting alternative cash crop production:

What would this mean for the extension services? What would this mean for the extension services? Include community-based learning and consultation activities with participating farmers/villages.

New crops need a gender-sensitive analysis before introduction that crop would benefit everyone in the village. Community-based learning and consultation activities need to separate women and men to make sure that all voices are heard.

12. Promotion of deforestation-free value chains and access to markets: what are women's greatest challenges to develop such value chains now, what are their barriers to access markets now?

Make women more confident in starting a new crop/value chain. LWU and other sectors should be included in the planning process. Women need to have better access to extension services and better access for women to these markets. For example, this year cardamom suddenly did not sell well which caused many women to lose money. The government needs to provide more support. We would need a thorough documentation of lessons learned like this, and find sustainable solutions together.

13. Access to microfinance for these new value chains: how are women benefiting from that?

Having microfinance support is better than nothing but not the sole or optimal solution. For some women, it can be too early to get started. Very often they do not hit the risk in the first place, so microfinance alone is not the right approach.

14. How do you see the potentials and challenges in inviting private sector investments in community-based agroforestry in natural production forests, including in the timber and other industrial uses? Village consultations for such potential private sector investments need to ensure that village women's concerns and concerns are actively taken into account (only women meetings etc.).
