LWU should always be involved in village level activities because they can easily access village women. So they recommend to make the responsibility very clear that LWU is responsible to implement all activities related to ESDM and gender. This way it is ensured that gender is always an integral part. They feel ready to fully support the project.

### Implementation of the Laos PDR Emission Reductions Program through Improved Governance and Sustainable Forest Landscape Management

#### 1. What is your role in REDD+ mechanisms implementation?
DLRU is usually invited by DAFO to join forest-related activities. No specific implementation experience exists, but DLRU staff have attended meetings held at Lao WO and participated in consultation meetings for ICBM (by KWR) and Bank projects.

#### 2. From your point of view: what are women's specific roles in forest land use and in forest protection?
Women take part in forest management and protection, especially regarding NTFPs. In the ICBF project, women also serve as village meeting committees (usually the village WO/rep representative).

#### 3. How are women already part of REDD+ mechanisms? How much do they play a role in it?
Language, traditions, lower education. Early marriage in many ethnic groups. Children are being so close to nature and traditional life that they don't want to go to school. In some ethnic groups and villages, the men have more influence over women's lives.

#### 4. What challenges do you see when it comes to remote areas, women, ethnicity?
Language barriers. Women are not as well educated or literate as men in many cases. They often have to travel long distances for education and health services. In some villages, women are not allowed to participate in village meetings.

#### 5. What challenges do you see when it comes to remote areas, women, ethnicity?
Language barriers. Women are not as well educated or literate as men in many cases. They often have to travel long distances for education and health services. In some villages, women are not allowed to participate in village meetings.

#### 6. Do you feel women have the same access to benefits from forest resources and their management/decision-making? Is that different for different forest management modalities, like community forestry areas, protected forests, national parks, wildlife reserves, and buffer zone community forests?
Women are the key collectors of forest resources but most of the time are not given the opportunity to participate in meetings related to village politics, including forest management.

#### 7. How do you ensure your current capacity to support the planned program in regards to gender equality?
They feel they need more technical training on REDD+ and its gender implications to be able to fully support the new program.

#### 8. How gender-sensitive are you? Do you think the current REDD+ mechanisms are? How much is REDD+ and gender aspects of it integrated into the SEEDP?

**Page 242**

Very little. Gender is not an integral part of these planning processes and consultations.

The LWU Law should be consulted when drafting those guidelines and regulations. Opportunities for women in forest management are not always clear or obvious, and sometimes do not happen. Also, include that the LWU should always by standard be involved in planning and implementation ac- tivities, and any consultations. This would also then include that the LWU gets delegated support to do this.

LWU would like to be part of forest law enforcement measures. Village women should be part of the village steering committee where decisions about forest management and law enforcement needs are taken.

Should partici- pation have both names, but since women are still often at disadvantage when it comes to local legal principles, they should be given a special attention and support. Whenever village committees allow, both men and women should participate in forest resource monitor- ing efforts. Women are often more aware about what is happening on their land than men. ### What is your role in REDD+ mechanisms implementation?

During, DAFO had usually seen REDD+ consultations, but they feel they still have only limited knowledge of REDD+. The DAFO is responsible for forest tenure and their species, as well as general biodiversity/wildlife protection and emission reduction, and the adherence to laws and regulations, patrolling, deforestation.

### From your point of view: what are women’s specific roles in forest land use, and in forest protection?

Forest protection on village level, women have more roles and responsibilities because women usually go to collect firewood/NTFP (production/collection) and vegetable collection. Men mostly do documentation and enforcement of regulations; only a few women are part of that role of protection/resource management.

### How are women already part of REDD+ mechanisms?

In general, all activities aim to benefit all villagers equally. But especially in many ethnic groups, women are participating less in activities due to traditions.

Language, education, understanding of regulations and rights are also very low. Use modern communication tools (mobile phones), which shows LCDs can engage more and use convey messages more playfully.

Is that different for different forest management modalities, like community-based forests, national forests, protection forests, national parks, wild reserves, and buffer zone community forestry?

In some villages, women are already part of village and forest management committees. DAFO thinks that the local LWG representatives are the best choices to systematically place into the forest management committee.

Implementation alone we are not ready, but already appreciate additional specialized training to improve our work. Especially when the program requires certain knowledge and technical skills. The activity is still new for them to be able to assess that.

### The programme will support the development of regulations and guidelines based on the new Forest Law. From a gender perspective, what needs to be thought off?

The Forest Law is gender-neutral because law is law, but both men and women equally need to follow

# How can women be part in enforcement measures?

Women are key actors in forest protection. They protect, men destroy. So we need to find ways to support women’s natural protection efforts.

Women should be involved in all aspects of forest management. Even before formalized beforehand, the village authorities will just take over. Instead of approaching the village head (chieftain), approach village LWF/ICF. Could be entry point for PUVUSUWAS. Also, women are more systematically placed in village leadership than men.

## 10. LUP and improved tenure security: what are the current challenges esp., for women, what needs to be improved?

Women’s and men’s names are both on land use titles now, so the legal status is equalized. Same for LUP.

The challenge is within internal decision-making power relations in which we are harder to address. Lack of training and awareness is also a problem.

## 11. Development of a National Forest Monitoring System: how can women contribute?

If such system is developed, formalize women’s support and contribution to that from the beginning. Women need to be part of that because they are more users and protectors of the forests.

## 12. Programme wants to promote deforestation-free agriculture, away from shifting cultivation practices. What are the challenges? Which implications will this have on women's work? How’s livelihoods? What would that mean for women in terms of land rights? What kind of support would be needed? Include community-based learning and consultation activities with participating farmers/villages.

If we want to stop deforestation, the focus needs to be on income-generating activities, including NTFP conservation, sustainable use of NTFPs, value-added products. Government needs to provide training and extension services. Women will be part of the implementation.

## 13. Promotion of deforestation-free value chains and access to markets: what are women’s greatest challenges to develop such value chains now, what are their barriers to access markets now?

It is essential to focus on market demand when developing new value chains, but all in the frame of local production and consumption. Women need to be part of the process.

Government needs to be part of the negotiations to ensure monitoring and support.

## 14. Access to microfinance for these new value chains: how are women benefitting from that?

Luang Namtha has only limited experience with microfinance. It could be very beneficial for people through small loans, but it is not yet available in Luang Namtha District.

Besides microfinance, the IDCF sees VSF as great way to empower especially women in leadership and management. Introducing village-managed funds for forest protection would empower women to take over that role.

## 15. How do you see the potential and challenges in inviting private sector investments in community-based forest management? What are the potential sectors, including in the timber and fiber industries?

For you think villagers will be benefiting from such investment? How could the Govt ensure that the investments are benefitting the villages?

In Luang Namtha, rubber plantations are one of the drivers of deforestation. This needs to be taken care of, and avoided to do the same mistake again with another potential investment promotion. Government should be part of the negotiations to ensure monitoring and support.

Feasibility studies of every village are necessary before activities are decided upon because every village is different.
Road access in Luang Namtha is a huge issue for project implementation, where villages where deforestation is an issue are remote and hard to reach. So small infrastructure improvements should be part of the program. DWO staff needs vehicles to access more remote areas.

### GOV/GIZ Village Interviews: Houaphan, Sam Neua, Ban Yerd (Khmu & Hmong) - Women Group (15 women)

#### Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Program through Improved Governance and Sustainable Forest Landscape Management

1. **Tell us something about how your life is like**
   - Working, selling to Sam Neua; sometimes they have traders who regularly buy their weaving, shifting cultivation, paddy fields, livestock.

2. **What are women’s and men’s specific roles in forest and land use?**

| Forest: |
|---|
| Demarcation border clearing (both), natural regeneration activities (both esp. youth). |
| Men lead demarcation group, parcelling, timber harvesting for domestic construction, firewood collection (together with women), hunting. |
| Women collect firewood, gather fruit and nuts, collect free fruit & bamboo shoots. |

| Land: work together; paddy field or garden |
|---|
| Livestock: poultry, everything near the house – women; further away (bigger animals): men |

3. **...and in forest protection?**
   - Small planting activities; plantations of trees (rubber) inside the forest cover, adhere to different forest management modalities according to LUP

4. **Do you feel like the work related to forest and land use are distributed fairly between men and women?**
   - Women are having higher work load than men because they take care of all the “less hard” tasks; men do the heavy tasks like plowing the field. This makes women busy over the course of a whole day.

5. **Do you know of REDD+ mechanisms and why they are existing?**
   - Not of REDD+ as such, but about Environment Protection, climate change, forest protection and that we have to take care of it.

6. **From your knowledge of the forest and land: did you see any changes over the years? (E.g. less resources?)**
   - Resources becoming less and less over the last years. Since they started forest protection area, the trees recovered and to them it seems like there are more trees again; NTFPs got less. Women don’t want to go too far away from the village (more constraints), so they have less NTFPs for domestic consumption.

7. **Do you feel like you benefit from forest resources? How?**
   - Not balanced within the village depending on how much you can collect and sell. Within Hm, equal benefits.
   - Women take care of finances. Decision-making about finances: small things sole decisions; bigger investments are discussed and decided jointly.

1. Tell us something about how your life is like.

Unity between men and women. Very poor living conditions/poverty. Low education, few jobs. Many people are unemployed. They have no money to buy food. Deforestation decreased. Water supply good. Road access is at least two, so better off than other villages in the area. The village has a school. There was a primary school before but it closed down and rose throughout the year. Village has primary school. Compared to more remote villages, we feel we are having better education. We have many things to improve. In the past, there were commercial trade routes. Some rice raised livestock. Now they raise livestock, but not too successful. Chal lenges in the village (Nabben) also include that the village is divided into two ethnic groups and villages which makes it difficult for them to work together. There are no roads leading directly to the village so access. Improve school building. The group does not have enough paddy fields. They dug a road and now they can get their produce to market. They sell their produce at the market from their village (30km). For secondary school, the kids walk about 1km to the neighboring village.

2. What are women’s and men’s specific roles in forest and land use?

Forest: men and women work together
Land: men work more than women (working paddy fields)

Fire prevention, restoration.

4. Do you feel like the work related to forest and land use are distributed fairly between men and women?

They also like they always have to think about how much as possible to get from the forest.

Fruits on the trees increased because of the forest restoration measures, as well as bigger trees due to protection/restoration. Wildlife increased for the same reason. Good forest results in good/mushrooms. NTFPs are used. Shifting cultivation is very hard and lots of work, so they would very much like to change to alternative crops. The deforestation meant less work for everyone; at least after the initial period (young trees need more care).


12. The programme wants to make the villagers an active part of forest management (village forest management planning & agreements, forest plantations, restoration activities and monitoring incl. patrols). What do you think about this? What are your fears?

Women want to be part of the forest management; restoration activities, planning. Villagers want to increase patrolling in the future because currently, everyone is using the forests as they want and they see the trees being cut down.


13. What are your barriers to access markets? Which support would you need to access them?

They keep some for their own consumption, and sell the rest at local markets in Luang Namtha town nearby and a market in a nearby village. Rubber is sold to a company which is either coming to pick up harvest or the villagers deliver it to the company.


14. What do you think would be adequate to transition into a new agricultural practice etc.?

The village has no experience with any form of microfinance yet. With access to small amounts to borrow, the men would start small shops; the women would like to increase their handcraft and textile production and create a small business.


82


---
**Page 255**
---

15. The programme wants to invite private sector investments in community-based agroforestry in national production forests. What do you think of that?

The village would be open to a collaboration with the private sector, if the land is suitable.

16. Any other remarks?

They would like to have access to the VDR, a bridge (requested since 10 years). They had built the school building by themselves but need more furniture for it. They feel like they are often requesting for things but rarely anything happens.


---
**Page 256**
---

### Tell us about your everyday life.

Nalban villagers cultivate rice, paddy rice fields, some of them do cultivation, livestock (pig, chicken, ducks), goats. They have small shops like paper production and textiles & handcraft mainly done by women. The village has a primary school with 10 students. In 2007 when they also re-ceived funding support for that by ADB, India today Nalban’s 3 Fund for Community Development is supporting with funding until 2020 under the idea of one of the villages (a man). 7 villagers created a cooperative to manage the forest. The village has 15 members in the cooperative and 60% of the income from the site stays with the village, rest goes to Provincial Tourism Office and Tourism Fund and other government funds. The women are not involved in the management committee but they have some role in the management committee for the site because village women who stay in the village have low education level.

The Nalban recognizes that they need external support to find ways to include women more in the management of the forest, especially in terms of income generation and other economic activities. Women: They join meetings if they feel they can learn something. They feel shy, not educated enough, and as if they cannot contribute much.

### What are women’s and men’s specific roles in forest and land use?

Villagers don’t use the forests much as the forest area is very small. Also see answer above.

### ...and in forest protection?

Only individual measures, nothing systematic.

### Do you feel like the work related to forest and land use are distributed fairly between men and women?

Work is equally divided in our village: women do handicraft, paper, textile and clothing; men do construction, only rice area. Cooking, animal rearing, rubber plantation care are done by everyone jointly.

### Do you know of REDD+ mechanisms and why they are existing?

Some families have little awareness about forest protection and watershed protection. They did not have any knowledge on the importance, environment protection, and they try to educate villagers who contribute negatively to that.

### From your knowledge of the forest and land: did you see any changes over the years? E.g., Less resources, NTRs etc?

10-15 years ago the biodiversity was significantly higher; now they perceive a stagnation.

### Do you feel like the benefit from your forest/ natural resources equally?

Income and benefits are shared equally within the mill. Women: normally, the families keep the earned money together, but some men spend too much

84


---
**Page 257**
---

8. What kind of forest do you have access to? Examples: community forests, collaborative forests, protection forests, national parks, wildlife reserves, and buffer zone community forests.

LUP completed. Protection forest and use forest

9. Do you feel you are participating in forest management(decision-making)? Is that different for different forest management models (like community forests, collaborative forests, protection forests, national parks, wildlife reserves, etc.)?

Women who have education to participate in any decision making, and but we not so strong enough. We would like to be part in decision making, but don’t fulfill the requirements. The few women who have education either leave the village or do it want to be part in village man- agement.

10. LUP and improved tenure security: what are the current challenges esp. for women, what needs to be improved?

Need to improve the existing (LUP) enforcement. When villagers don’t adhere to the LUP and cut down protected trees, they are called to the village office and have to pay a fine, and the District office is involved. But once the villagers do it again, no further consequences happen, so the villagers don’t take this seriously. The women who are involved in the forest management committee are not aware of this. If the population is growing as expected, the LUP will also need to be adjusted and they will need more enforcement.

11. Programme wants to promote deforestation free agriculture, away from shifting cultivation practices, and promoting alternative cash crop production practices. Any idea on how you can do that in your forest?

Note: Programme will also include community-based learning and consultation activities with par- ticipating farmers/communities

Villagers are not involved in the consultations. Consultations teams need to be very clear that they want women to participate in meetings. Less than 20 families on shifting cultivation, so it is not seen as a big problem. We see that rubber prices are falling right now, so we need to think of alternative crops.

12. The programme wants to make the villagers an active part of forest management (village forest management planning & agreements, forest planting, restoration activities and monitoring (incl. parameters such as tree species, age, height, diameter, etc.) and other related activities).

See answer to question 9.

13. What are your barriers to access markets? Which support would you need to access them?

Rubber is currently the village’s main income source and bought by the only rubber company (Yun- nani), which is trying to lower the price. Villagers are afraid that they lose income because of that mo- nation. Handicraft and textiles are not really a centre in Luang Prabang, and some of the Luang Namtha Township women have tried to sell their products there but without success. The New Zealand Fund connected them and established a regular business. This is, however, just an additional income for the village; they are more dependent on rubber.

14. What do you think of microfinance to start a business/invest in a new agricultural practice etc.?

They have a village bank which made it easier for the villagers to take small loans. They need more training for the village bank steering committee though, because financial literacy and understanding is still low.


85


---
**Page 258**
---

Women most women do not take loans because they don't know what kind of business/new product they could invest in. So a training and support in market demand analysis would be appreciated by both women and men.

The village started their own VDF one year ago, and the money will be used for small infrastructure projects like roads.

15. The programme wants to invite private sector investments in community-based agroforestry in national production forests. What do you think of that?

In the past, we had a company approaching us to plant crops for biofuel but in the end, no one bought it. So a private sector investment would be good as long as there is a guaranteed utilization of crops and products to incur income despite market fluctuations. Their dependency on tourism is too high, especially with the existing monopoly by Yunnan which already has huge concessions.

16. Any other remarks?

The funding support for the tourism site is ending soon, so the villagers are afraid what will happen then. The village needs more maintenance than they can afford to provide. They also need funding and technical assistance in developing the site because many tourists just come to take photos of the entire area but do not stay long enough to see other things like the local market or villages with accommodation for tourists, and other interesting features like in other sites (swings etc.).

They would also like more traders coming to the village and buy handicrafts—if that would happen regularly, they could increase production. They are also interested in developing their own smallcraft businesses.

Agricultural practices need to be improved, and market access beyond rubber.

Watersheds are shared with other villages, so it is very polluted due to rubber pesticides. So even though villagers live at the stream, they experience severely limited water access.

# Background and Introduction

This Gender Action Plan (GAP) is for the Green Climate Fund (GCF) project "Scaling up the implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reduction Programme through improved governance and sustainable development." It provides a gender analysis framework, methodology, key findings, and recommendations of the gender analysis. It contains specific gender elements to be considered in the project design and implementation phases.

The GAP aims to mainstream gender equality through climate change mitigation and adaptation actions; and minimize social, gender-related and climate-related risks in all its actions. Moreover, it helps to monitor implementation progress against the GCF's Gender Strategy (GCS) and ensures that gender mainstreaming and integration of a consistent gender perspective into the GCF project in order to maximize positive impacts on women and girls.

The GAP aims to create an enabling environment for drivers of change and positive gender dynamics as well as to manage and mitigate potential negative impacts. The GAP is aligned with the GCF's Gender Strategy (GCS) and its guidelines from the "Updated Policy and Gender Action Plan 2020-2030 (GCAP 24/15)."

The GCF's guidelines from the "Updated Policy on the Prevention and Protection from Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Harassment in the Context of its Operations" are also taken into account.

### The Gender Analysis

The GAP is closely aligned with the components' and outputs of the logic frame and planned activities outlined in the GCF Funding Proposal, where gender considerations are mainstreamed into all activities. These include the Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA), Project Appraisal Data (PAD), Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), EIA, PAD, and Ethnic Group Development Plan (IPF) Annex 6(d) that already contains gender-related aspects.

In addition to these, the GCF has also developed a Gender Action Plan (GAP) which has mentioned GCF gender related policies, but also systematically apply some general measures in accordance with the GCF's Gender Strategy. The GAP is aligned with the GCF's guidelines and aims to enhance gender equality within the project's governing structure and day-to-day operations and it aims to promote the goals of gender equality and women's empowerment through its decisions on the allocation, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting.

This GAP also commits to GIZ gender policy guidelines that include, but are not limited to the following:

* A gender inclusive and gender-sensitive language is used in reports, training materials and publications.
* In the project team(s), competencies on gender and gender-sensitive forest management will be built through capacity building workshops and trainings.
* The GAP will be executed by the different entities involved in the project management, including all concerned Government line agencies and development partners and led by the responsible Gender Focal Person.

## 2. Gender Action Plan

The GAP, presented in the following Table, has been developed for the implementation of Project 2. It builds upon the GAP developed for Project 1, and has been revised and adjusted to reflect the design changes under Project 2 and lessons learned from Project 1 (see also the Gender Assessment in Annex B). As a result, the gender action plan is more specific than that of Project 1.

The gender assessment and GAP have been developed through expert interviews with actors involved in Project 1, including representatives at national, provincial, district and village level, and through the assessment of literature and key documents.

# Project Activities

## Measures to strengthen gender equality and women’s empowerment in Project 2

### Activity 2.1.3 (support)

#### Indicators
- **Women-led households:** Up to 40% of women-led households joining PSAP group activities, with an average of at least 35% of northern rural agricultural households having latest data available.
- **Women Country Gender Assessment, 2020:**
  - Note: Women-led households which do not have a head (e.g., husband died or left the family/household)

#### Targets
- Up to 40% of women-led households joining PSAP group activities and receiving support are women-led.
- Latest data available.

#### Timeline
Y, 1–4

#### Responsible
DAOL Safeguards' Team

#### Budget
Included within the budget of PSAP Activities

### Activity 2.2.3 (percentage of N% of PSAP training participants that are women)

#### Indicators
- Participation rates at least 40%
- At least 50% of meetings and trainings held below 40%

#### Timeline
Y, 1–4

#### Responsible
DAOL Safeguards' Team

#### Budget
Included within the budget of PSAP Activities

### Activity 2.3.2 (introduce quotes for the participation of women in VFG set-up and committees)

#### Indicators
- **Percent (%) female participants in VFG set-up meetings and community gathering:** At least 40%
- **Percent (%) female VFG committee members:** At least 30% VFG committee members.

#### Timeline
Y, 1–4

#### Responsible
Lao Women’s Union, other implementers, Safeguards Team

#### Budget
Within the budget of PSAP Activities

*As per the VFAO guideline.*
